referenceSystem.toolboxVersion

Version, build date and provenance of the referenceSystem toolbox.

SYNTAX

versionInfo = toolboxVersion() returns a scalar struct describing the
  release of this toolbox, the external authorities it is built against and
  the provenance tag that every help block in the toolbox carries. It is the
  single source of that information: no other file states a version literal.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

(none)

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                     Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                     ----   ----     -----  -----------
  versionInfo              1x1    struct   -      Fields listed below.
  .version                 1x1    string   -      Semantic version, release plus
                                                 patch, e.g. '2.1.204'.
  .release                 1x1    string   -      Major and minor only, '2.1'.
  .patch                   1x1    string   -      Patch component, e.g. '204'.
                                                 This IS the handover
                                                 revision number (D107).
  .buildDate               1x1    string   -      Source revision date, ISO 8601.
  .sofaRelease             1x1    string   -      Bundled IAU SOFA release, '20231011'.
  .iersConventions         1x1    string   -      IERS Conventions edition, '2010'.
  .author                  1x1    string   -      Author of the toolbox.
  .generator               1x1    string   -      Model and date the sources were written.
  .versionTag              1x1    string   -      Version line of the help template.
  .helpTag                 1x1    string   -      Authorship line of the help template.

METHOD

All values are compile-time constants of the source revision, not runtime
  queries: buildDate is the date the sources were written, never the date the
  function is called, so a report generated from an archived copy of the
  toolbox states the release that produced it. version, author and generator
  are the only literals; versionTag and helpTag are composed from them, so the
  two tag lines in every help block have exactly one authority each and a
  provenance test can verify the agreement.

  THE PATCH COMPONENT IS THE HANDOVER REVISION NUMBER (decision D107).
  Version 2.1.N is the state of the sources at handover revision 1.N, and the
  two are edited together or not at all. Major and minor identify the
  RELEASE; the patch identifies the DOCUMENT REVISION that produced these
  sources, so an archived copy can be tied to the revision log entry that
  explains it without guessing from dates. A test and auditPackage.py both
  read the top row of the handover's revision log and refuse a disagreement,
  because a convention nothing checks is a convention that drifts.

  versionTag CARRIES MAJOR.MINOR ONLY, AND DELIBERATELY. It appears in the
  help footer of every file in the toolbox; putting the patch there would
  rewrite 233 lines on every revision and bury each session's real diff. The
  footer states the release, versionInfo.version states the revision, and the
  test report prints the latter so a run is always traceable to a document.

  THE FOOTER IS TWO LINES AND NOT ONE (decision D103). Naming the author in
  full alongside the toolbox, the version and the model exceeds the 100-column
  limit of handover section C.1 by three characters, and a coding standard is
  not amended to fit a credit line. The version and the authorship are
  therefore separate assertions, which is also the more useful split: a
  version bump and a change of authorship are different events.

  The function is deliberately not called version: a package function of that
  name would shadow the MATLAB builtin version for every other function inside
  +referenceSystem, silently (handover section C.5 rule 3).

ACCURACY

Not applicable. The function returns metadata; no numerical quantity is
  computed and no approximation is made.

ERRORS

(none)

REFERENCES

IAU SOFA Software Collection, release 2023-10-11, IAU SOFA Board.
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, G. Petit and B. Luzum (eds.).

EXAMPLE

v = referenceSystem.toolboxVersion();
  fprintf('%s (SOFA %s)\n', v.version, v.sofaRelease);

KEYWORDS

version, provenance, build, SOFA, IERS Conventions, metadata

See also IERSCONSTANTS, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS.

referenceSystem.dataFolder

Get or set the persistent data folder of the toolbox.

SYNTAX

folder = dataFolder() returns the folder the toolbox reads and writes
  installed data in, creating it if it does not exist. Without a stored
  preference this is data/ beside the package, which is what ships.

  folder = dataFolder(path) stores path as the data folder and returns it.
  The choice PERSISTS ACROSS SESSIONS as a MATLAB preference, so it is made
  once and not on every call, and no startup.m is needed.

  folder = dataFolder("reset") discards the stored choice and returns the
  default. Without it a preference would be one-way and the shipped
  behaviour unreachable, which is a trap rather than a setting.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                     Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                     ----   ----     -----  -----------
  newFolder                1x1    string   -      Folder to store, the
                                                  literal "reset" to discard
                                                  a stored choice, or omitted
                                                  to query. Default "".

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                     Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                     ----   ----     -----  -----------
  folder                   1x1    string   -      ABSOLUTE path of the data
                                                  folder, which exists on
                                                  return.

METHOD

ONE AUTHORITY FOR THE LOCATION, AND THIS IS IT. downloadEop and
  downloadKernels each carried a private localDefaultCache that composed
  data/ from their own mfilename, so the toolbox held two answers to one
  question and neither could be moved. Both now resolve through this
  function, which is rev. 1.53's rule applied to a path: where a value has
  more than one definition, delete the copies rather than keep them equal.

  THE MECHANISM IS setpref AND IT MATCHES shAnalysis DELIBERATELY. The same
  author maintains both toolboxes and calls shLowLevel.dataFolder with the
  same three shapes - query, set, "reset" - so the habit transfers and the
  two do not disagree about where a session's data lives. The preference
  group is the toolbox name, so nothing here can collide with another
  toolbox's settings.

  THE FOLDER IS CREATED ON QUERY, WHICH IS A SIDE EFFECT AND IS INTENDED.
  Every caller of this function is about to read or write in the folder, and
  returning a path that does not exist moves the mkdir into each of them,
  where it would be written more than once and eventually forgotten in one
  place. The alternative - a query that only reports - was considered and
  rejected because it makes the common path longer to serve a case the
  toolbox does not have.

  A STORED PREFERENCE IS INVISIBLE IN A SCRIPT, which is the cost of
  persistence and is stated here rather than discovered. Two identical
  scripts on two machines can read different files with nothing in the call
  to say so. Where that matters - a test, a published result, a CI run -
  pass the folder explicitly through the CacheDir option of downloadEop or
  downloadKernels, which is unaffected by any preference.

ACCURACY

Not applicable; the function resolves a path and computes no numerical
  quantity.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:dataFolder:pathIsAFile
      The requested folder is an existing FILE, so no folder can be made
      there. Raised before anything is written, because mkdir would
      otherwise fail later inside a transfer and report a kernel when the
      defect is in the argument.
  referenceSystem:dataFolder:notCreated
      The folder does not exist and could not be created; the message
      carries the reason the file system gave.

REFERENCES

Handover decision D119 (rev. 1.207) and section C.10. The sibling
  implementation is shLowLevel.dataFolder in the shAnalysis toolbox.

EXAMPLE

% Where does installed data go, and how do I move it off the system disk?
  folder = referenceSystem.dataFolder()
  referenceSystem.dataFolder("E:\DATAPOOL\referenceSystem");
  referenceSystem.downloadKernels(Mode = "download");   % goes there now
  referenceSystem.dataFolder("reset");                  % back to the default

KEYWORDS

data folder, preference, cache, installation, kernels, EOP

See also DOWNLOADKERNELS, DOWNLOADEOP, SETUPREFERENCESYSTEM

referenceSystem.iersConstants

Named IERS, IAU and BIPM constants used throughout the toolbox.

SYNTAX

constants = iersConstants() returns a scalar struct of the defining and
  nominal constants of the IERS Conventions (2010), the relevant IAU
  resolutions and the GNSS time-scale definitions. It is the single authority
  for every one of them (design principle 2): no other file in the toolbox may
  contain these values as literals.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

(none)

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                     Size   Type    Units   Description
  ----                     ----   ----    -----   -----------
  constants                1x1    struct  -       Fields listed below.
  .ttMinusTai              1x1    double  s       TT - TAI, defining offset (+32.184).
  .lg                      1x1    double  1       L_G, TT/TCG rate ratio, defining.
  .lb                      1x1    double  1       L_B, TDB/TCB rate ratio, defining.
  .tdb0                    1x1    double  s       TDB0, TDB/TCB zero-point offset.
  .gpsMinusTai             1x1    double  s       GPS - TAI, defining offset (-19).
  .bdtMinusTai             1x1    double  s       BDT - TAI, defining offset (-33).
  .earthRotationRate       1x1    double  rad/s   omega_earth, nominal mean rate.
  .eraRatePerUt1Second     1x1    double  rad/s   d(ERA)/d(UT1 second), the
                                                  ERA formula's defining
                                                  coefficient. NOT the same
                                                  as earthRotationRate.
  .arcsecondsToRadians     1x1    double  rad     Radians per arcsecond, pi/648000.
  .julianDateOffset        1x1    double  d       JD - MJD = 2400000.5 (SOFA DJM0).
  .mjdJ2000                1x1    double  d       MJD of J2000.0 = 51544.5 (TT).
  .secondsPerDay           1x1    double  s       Seconds in one day of TAI, TT or SI.
  .mjdTai1977              1x1    double  d       MJD of 1977-01-01 0h TAI = 43144.0.
  .mjdTt1977               1x1    double  d       Same instant expressed in TT.
  .speedOfLight            1x1    double  m/s     c, defining (299792458).
  .lc                      1x1    double  1       L_C, mean 1-d(TCG)/d(TCB).
  .gmSun                   1x1    double  m^3/s^2 Heliocentric gravitational constant.
  .gmEarth                 1x1    double  m^3/s^2 Geocentric gravitational constant.
  .moonEarthMassRatio      1x1    double  1       Moon/Earth mass ratio.
  .gmMoon                  1x1    double  m^3/s^2 Derived: moonEarthMassRatio*gmEarth.

METHOD

Every value is either a defining constant fixed by convention or, where
  marked, a derived quantity computed here from the defining ones so that a
  second copy cannot drift from the first: arcsecondsToRadians is pi/648000
  rather than a decimal literal, and mjdTt1977 is mjdTai1977 advanced by
  ttMinusTai. The 1977 epoch is the common origin of TCG and TCB and is needed
  in both scales, which is why both forms are supplied.

  Sign convention throughout: a field named aMinusB holds the numerical value
  of scale a minus scale b, so GPS = TAI + gpsMinusTai.

  THE STRUCT IS BUILT ONCE PER SESSION AND CACHED (prompt P28). The values
  are compile-time constants of the source, so a persistent has nothing to go
  stale against, and MATLAB discards it when this file is edited or cleared -
  the same argument that already justifies the caches in earthRotationAngleRate
  and leapSecondTable. The reason is measured rather than assumed: the run-14
  profile of one single-epoch rotationItrsToGcrs call recorded 24 calls to
  this function, because every helper that needs one constant fetches the
  whole struct independently. Assembling thirteen name-value pairs 24 times
  per rotation was about a tenth of that call. The returned struct is a copy,
  so a caller that modifies it cannot reach the cache.

ACCURACY

ttMinusTai, lg, lb, tdb0, gpsMinusTai, bdtMinusTai, julianDateOffset,
  mjdJ2000 and secondsPerDay are exact by definition; they carry no
  uncertainty and their double representation is exact except for lg, lb and
  tdb0, which are rounded to the nearest double (relative error < 1.2e-16).
  arcsecondsToRadians is the correctly rounded double of pi/648000 and is
  bit-identical to the SOFA constant DAS2R.
  mjdTt1977 inherits one rounding of ttMinusTai/secondsPerDay, below 1e-19 d.

  speedOfLight is defining and therefore exact. THE THREE GRAVITATIONAL
  CONSTANTS ARE MEASURED AND ARE THE ONLY UNCERTAIN VALUES IN THIS STRUCT:
  gmSun carries 1e10 m^3/s^2 (7.5e-11 relative), gmEarth 8e5 (2e-9 relative)
  and moonEarthMassRatio 4e-10 (3.3e-8 relative). They enter only the
  1/c^2 potential term of gcrsToBcrs, where a relative error e produces
  e*U/c^2*|r| - so gmSun's 7.5e-11 is 4.7e-12 m at the Earth's surface, nine
  orders inside the 1 mm uncertainty IERS Conventions Eq. (11.19) states for
  itself. They must NOT be used as a force-model authority: gmEarth is the
  TCG-compatible value and a gravity field carries its own, consistent with
  its own coefficients.

  lc is measured, not defining: 2e-17 on 1.48082686741e-8, i.e. 1.4e-9
  relative, and it includes a 1.15e-16 contribution from terms in c^-4 and
  from the asteroids. gmMoon is DERIVED here from the ratio and gmEarth
  rather than stated, so a second copy cannot drift from the first.

  eraRatePerUt1Second AND earthRotationRate ARE DIFFERENT NUMBERS AND THE
  DIFFERENCE IS NOT ROUNDING. The first is 2*pi*1.00273781191135448/86400 =
  7.292115146706980e-5, the exact slope of the ERA formula in UT1; the second
  is 7.292115e-5, a nominal mean rate. They differ by 2.0e-8 relative. Use
  eraRatePerUt1Second for any PARTIAL DERIVATIVE with respect to Delta UT1 -
  rotationItrsToGcrsPartials does - because ERA is exactly linear in UT1 and
  that slope is exact. Length of day does NOT enter it: LOD governs how UT1
  relates to TAI, not the ERA formula, so this coefficient is the same at
  every epoch.

  earthRotationRate is the only nominal value: it is the mean rate, and the
  instantaneous rate departs from it with the excess length of day by up to
  about 2e-8 relative (1.7e-12 rad/s for LOD = 2 ms). Integrated over one day
  that is 1.5e-7 rad, about 0.9 m at the equator, so this constant must never
  be used to propagate an angle. Use earthRotationAngle for the angle and
  interpolateEop for the rate; earthRotationRate is for scaling and for
  order-of-magnitude work only.

ERRORS

(none)

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Tables 1.1 and 1.2.
  IAU 2000 Resolution B1.9 (L_G); IAU 2006 Resolution B3 (L_B, TDB0).
  IAU SOFA sofa.h, constants DAS2R, DJM0, DJM00, DAYSEC, TTMTAI.
  IERS Conventions (2010), Table 1.1 for c, L_C, GM_sun, GM_earth and the
  Moon-Earth mass ratio; the GM values are TCB- and TCG-compatible
  respectively, as that table footnotes.

EXAMPLE

c = referenceSystem.iersConstants();
  arcsec = 0.15;
  angleInRadians = arcsec*c.arcsecondsToRadians;

KEYWORDS

IERS, IAU, constants, TT, TAI, TCG, TCB, TDB, GPS, BDT, MJD, arcsecond

See also TOOLBOXVERSION, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS.

referenceSystem.ellipsoidParameters

Defining and derived parameters of a reference ellipsoid.

SYNTAX

ellipsoidInfo = ellipsoidParameters(name) returns a scalar struct with the
  defining and derived parameters of the named rotational reference ellipsoid.

  ellipsoidTable = ellipsoidParameters('list') returns a table with one row per
  supported ellipsoid and the same quantities as variables.

  Supported names, case sensitive: 'WGS84', 'GRS80', 'Bessel1841',
  'Hayford1924', 'Krassovsky1940'.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----  -----------
  name    1x1    string   -      Ellipsoid name, or 'list' for the table form.
                                 A char row vector is accepted and converted.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name             Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----             ----   ----     -----  -----------
  ellipsoidInfo    1x1    struct   -      Parameters of the named ellipsoid.
  .a               1x1    double   m      Semi-major axis, defining.
  .b               1x1    double   m      Semi-minor axis, derived.
  .f               1x1    double   1      Flattening, derived from 1/f.
  .e2              1x1    double   1      First eccentricity squared, derived.
  .ep2             1x1    double   1      Second eccentricity squared, derived.
  .n               1x1    double   1      Third flattening f/(2-f), derived.
  .name            1x1    string   -      Name as supplied, echoed for provenance.

  For name = 'list' the output is a 5x7 table with variables name, a, b, f,
  e2, ep2, n; name is a string column, the remaining variables are double.

METHOD

Each ellipsoid is stored by its two defining parameters only, the semi-major
  axis a and the inverse flattening 1/f as published by the defining authority.
  Everything else is computed here, so no derived value can drift from the
  defining pair:
      f   = 1/(1/f)
      b   = a*(1-f)
      e2  = f*(2-f)
      ep2 = e2/(1-e2)
      n   = f/(2-f)
  The struct echoes the requested name as a string. Carrying it makes the
  ellipsoid self-describing, so a downstream function can verify rather than
  assume which ellipsoid a coordinate set belongs to (design principle 3).
  That the field is a string and not a char row vector is deliberate and is
  documented here because v1's refell returned char and callers indexed it.

  'list' is available in MATLAB only. The table type is not supported by
  MATLAB Coder, so the branch is guarded by coder.target and eliminated from
  generated code; the named-ellipsoid path is codegen-clean.

ACCURACY

a and 1/f are exact by definition. The derived quantities carry one to three
  double-precision roundings, at most 1.5e-9 m in b and 1e-18 in e2, which is
  four orders of magnitude below any geodetic requirement.

  GRS80 uses the published inverse flattening 298.257222101, which is itself
  the rounded value derived from the defining set (a, GM, J2, omega). The
  rounding shifts b by 8.4e-9 m relative to the unrounded derivation, again
  negligible, and the published value is used because it is the one every
  other geodetic product uses.

  WGS84 and GRS80 share a but not 1/f. Their semi-minor axes differ by
  1.05e-4 m, so at sub-millimetre precision the two are not interchangeable
  and the choice must be stated, never defaulted, by the caller.

ERRORS

MATLAB:validators:mustBeMember
      Raised by the arguments block for a name that is not supported. The
      message lists the permitted values; matching is case sensitive.
  referenceSystem:ellipsoidParameters:listNotSupportedInCodegen
      Raised when 'list' is requested from generated code.

REFERENCES

NIMA TR8350.2 (2000), Department of Defense World Geodetic System 1984.
  H. Moritz (1980), Geodetic Reference System 1980, Bull. Geodesique 54(3).
  W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Table 4.1.
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Table 1.2.

EXAMPLE

e = referenceSystem.ellipsoidParameters("GRS80");
  allEllipsoids = referenceSystem.ellipsoidParameters("list");

KEYWORDS

ellipsoid, WGS84, GRS80, Bessel, Hayford, Krassovsky, flattening,
  eccentricity, semi-major axis, geodetic

See also IERSCONSTANTS, TOOLBOXVERSION.

referenceSystem.timeScale

Registry of the time scales this toolbox understands.

SYNTAX

scaleInfo = timeScale(name) validates and canonicalises a time-scale name
  and returns its properties. scaleTable = timeScale("list") returns every
  supported scale as a table.

  This is the single authority for the set of scale names and for which of
  them are uniform (handover principle 2). Without it the same list would be
  repeated in normalizeEpoch, convertTimeScale, addSeconds, secondsBetween
  and fundamentalArguments, which is the two-sources-of-truth pattern behind
  findings M-2 and C-2. It is the time-scale counterpart of the frame
  registry of handover section B.3.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----            -----  -----------
  name       1x1    string | char   -      Scale name, any case, or "list".

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name             Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----             ----   ----      -----  -----------
  scaleInfo        1x1    struct    -      Properties of one scale, below.
  .name            1x1    string    -      Canonical upper-case name.
  .isUniform       1x1    logical   -      True if the scale runs without
                                           steps, so that seconds may be
                                           added to it directly.
  .isLeapBearing   1x1    logical   -      True if the scale carries UTC leap
                                           seconds (UTC and GLONASS).
  .description     1x1    string    -      One-line description.

  For name == "list" the output is instead an Nx4 table with variables
  name, isUniform, isLeapBearing and description.

METHOD

Eleven scales are supported: the atomic and dynamical scales TAI, TT, TDB,
  TCG and TCB, the rotation-derived scales UTC and UT1, and the satellite
  navigation scales GPS, GST, BDT and GLONASS. Names are matched
  case-insensitively and returned upper case, because the IERS and BIPM
  symbols are upper-case acronyms and no two of them differ only in case, so
  folding the case resolves nothing and invents nothing.

  No aliases exist. The v1 converter accepted GAL for GST, BEIDOU for BDT and
  GLONASST for GLONASS; design principle 12 removes them, so exactly one
  spelling names each scale and a call site cannot disguise which it means.

  Nothing here is cached, and the comment at the head of the body records the
  measurement that says caching it made the function slower rather than
  faster. The function is a pure function of its argument with no state at
  all, so two callers cannot interfere by any route.

  isUniform is false for UTC and GLONASS only. It is the property that
  decides whether addSeconds and secondsBetween may work directly on an
  epoch: a second of UTC is not a second of proper time across a leap second,
  and GLONASS inherits that because it is UTC advanced by three hours.

ACCURACY

Not applicable; the function computes no numerical quantity.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:timeScale:unknownScale

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1 and Table 1.1.
  BIPM, The International System of Units (SI), 9th edition, 2019, Ch. 2.3.1.
  IS-GPS-200, Galileo OS SIS ICD, BDS-SIS-ICD-B1I, GLONASS ICD (system times).

EXAMPLE

info = referenceSystem.timeScale("tt");
  assert(info.name == "TT" && info.isUniform);
  supported = referenceSystem.timeScale("list");

KEYWORDS

time scale, TAI, TT, TDB, TCG, TCB, UTC, UT1, GPS, GST, BDT, GLONASS

See also NORMALIZEEPOCH, CONVERTTIMESCALE.

referenceSystem.normalizeEpoch

Validate an epoch struct and put it in canonical form.

SYNTAX

epochOut = normalizeEpoch(epochIn) checks that epochIn is a well formed
  epoch as defined in handover section B.3, returns its fields as Nx1 double
  columns, forces day to an integer day number and folds sec into the
  half-open interval [0, 86400) - or [0, 86401) on a UTC day that ends in a
  positive leap second.

  Every L0 to L2 function calls this on entry, so an epoch that reaches any
  arithmetic has already been validated exactly once, in one place.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name          Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----          ----   ----            -----  -----------
  epochIn       1x1    struct          -      Epoch struct, fields below.
  .day          Nx1    double          d      Day number, MJD at 0h of the
                                              scale. Scalar is broadcast.
  .sec          Nx1    double          s      Seconds since 0h of that day.
                                              Scalar is broadcast.
  .scale        1x1    string | char   -      Time scale, any case, one of
                                              the names in timeScale.
  .source       1x1    string | char   -      Provenance, free text.
                                              Optional on input.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name          Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----          ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epochOut      1x1    struct    -      Canonical epoch.
  .day          Nx1    double    d      Integer day number.
  .sec          Nx1    double    s      Seconds of day, [0, 86400), or
                                        [0, 86401) on a positive leap-second
                                        day in UTC.
  .scale        1x1    string    -      Canonical upper-case scale name.
  .source       1x1    string    -      Provenance, unchanged or "unspecified".

METHOD

The split day-and-second form is the reason this toolbox holds sub-
  microsecond resolution over decades: a decimal MJD near 60000 resolves only
  about 0.6 us in double precision, which is coarser than a GRACE KBR sample.
  Normalisation therefore never round-trips through a decimal MJD.

  THE CARRY IS DELEGATED, THE VALIDATION IS NOT. Since prompt P28b this
  function validates and then calls the package-private carryEpoch, which is
  the single implementation of the carry in the toolbox. convertTimeScale
  reaches the same function directly for epochs it has just built from one it
  already normalised, and that is the whole difference between the two paths.
  The description below is of what carryEpoch does and is kept here because
  this is the entry a caller uses.

  For a uniform scale the carry is exact and unconditional:
  carry = floor(sec/86400), sec = sec - 86400*carry, day = day + carry. A
  fractional part of day is folded into sec by the same relation before the
  carry, which is exact because a day of a uniform scale is 86400 s long.

  UTC is different, and this is finding C-3. A UTC day that ends in a
  positive leap second is 86401 s long, so the plain carry would turn
  23:59:60.5 into 00:00:00.5 of the next day, a different instant one second
  later, silently. The UTC branch therefore asks leapSecondTable for the length
  of the day before carrying, and iterates at most twice - which is why the
  input range is restricted, see ERRORS. For the same reason a non-integer
  day is rejected rather than folded in UTC: the fold would assume an 86400 s
  day.

  GLONASS is UTC advanced by three hours, so a leap second falls at 03:00:00
  in the middle of a GLONASS day rather than at its end, and the leap second
  itself has no distinct representation in the day-and-second form. GLONASS
  is therefore normalised as a uniform scale here, and convertTimeScale
  refuses to produce a GLONASS epoch that would fall inside a leap second.

  leapSecondTable is consulted only for a UTC epoch whose sec lies outside
  [0, 86400), which is the rare path. A normal arc never touches it, so a
  missing MEX gateway does not make ordinary normalisation fail.

ACCURACY

Exact. Every operation is an addition or subtraction of an integer number
  of days or of 86400, both exactly representable, so no rounding is
  introduced beyond that already present in the inputs. Round-trip through
  normalizeEpoch is bit-identical for an already canonical epoch.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:normalizeEpoch:missingField
  referenceSystem:normalizeEpoch:badFieldType
  referenceSystem:normalizeEpoch:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:normalizeEpoch:nonIntegerDay
  referenceSystem:normalizeEpoch:secondsOutOfRange

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.
  ITU-R TF.460-6, Standard-frequency and time-signal emissions (UTC).
  Handover section B.3 (the epoch type) and finding C-3.

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([57753 86400.5], "UTC");
  epoch = referenceSystem.normalizeEpoch(epoch);
  assert(epoch.day == 57753 && epoch.sec > 86400);   % the 2016 leap second

KEYWORDS

epoch, MJD, seconds of day, leap second, UTC, normalisation

See also TIMESCALE, LEAPSECONDS, DAYSECONDTOEPOCH, EPOCHTO, CONVERTTIMESCALE.

referenceSystem.mjdToEpoch

Epoch from a decimal Modified Julian Date.

SYNTAX

epoch = mjdToEpoch(mjd, scale) interprets every element of mjd as one
  decimal MJD in the given time scale and returns the canonical epoch struct
  of handover section B.3.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----            -----  -----------
  mjd     Nx1    double          d      Decimal Modified Julian Dates. Any
                                        shape is accepted and read in column
                                        order.
  scale   1x1    string | char   -      Time scale, any case, one of the
                                        names in timeScale.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epoch      1x1    struct    -      Canonical epoch, N entries.
  .day       Nx1    double    d      Integer day number.
  .sec       Nx1    double    s      Seconds of day.
  .scale     1x1    string    -      Canonical scale name.
  .source    1x1    string    -      "mjdToEpoch".

METHOD

day = floor(mjd) and sec = (mjd - day)*86400. There is no shape guessing
  and no Format argument: this function takes decimal MJD and nothing else,
  which is what makes finding M-1 structurally impossible. In v1 the input
  [60310 43200] was ambiguous between two epochs and one day-plus-seconds
  pair, and mjd_normalize guessed. Here mjdToEpoch([60310 43200]) is
  unambiguously two epochs and daySecondToEpoch([60310 43200]) is
  unambiguously one. Do not add a wrapper that accepts either.

  For a UTC input the leap second itself is not reachable through this
  constructor, because a decimal MJD has no room for a 86400.5 s day; use
  daySecondToEpoch for an epoch inside a leap second.

ACCURACY

The split form is exact; the resolution is that of the decimal MJD handed
  in. Near MJD 60000 one unit in the last place of a double is 7.3e-12 d,
  which is 0.63 us, so a decimal MJD cannot express a GRACE KBR sample
  interval and this constructor cannot recover what the caller has already
  lost. Use daySecondToEpoch or j2000SecondsToEpoch where that matters.
  Round-trip mjdToEpoch then epochTo(...,"mjd") reproduces the input to
  within two units in the last place of the input, measured 1.5e-11 d over
  1962 to 2100.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:timeScale:unknownScale

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.
  Handover section B.3 and finding M-1.

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.mjdToEpoch([60310.5; 60311.5], "TT");
  assert(all(epoch.sec == 43200));

KEYWORDS

epoch, MJD, Modified Julian Date, time scale

See also DAYSECONDTOEPOCH, DATETIMETOEPOCH, EPOCHTO, NORMALIZEEPOCH.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch

Epoch from an Nx2 day-number and seconds-of-day matrix.

SYNTAX

epoch = daySecondToEpoch(daySecond, scale) reads column 1 as the integer
  day number and column 2 as seconds since 0h of that day, in the given time
  scale, and returns the canonical epoch struct of handover section B.3.

  This is the lossless constructor and the one to use for precise work: it
  is the only inbound form that can express an epoch inside a positive leap
  second, and the only one that carries sub-microsecond resolution over
  decades without qualification.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----            -----  -----------
  daySecond   Nx2    double          -      Column 1 day number in days,
                                            column 2 seconds of day.
  scale       1x1    string | char   -      Time scale, any case, one of the
                                            names in timeScale.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epoch      1x1    struct    -      Canonical epoch, N entries.
  .day       Nx1    double    d      Integer day number.
  .sec       Nx1    double    s      Seconds of day.
  .scale     1x1    string    -      Canonical scale name.
  .source    1x1    string    -      "daySecondToEpoch".

METHOD

The two columns are handed to normalizeEpoch unchanged, so the carry and
  the leap-second rules of that function apply. The input is required to be
  Nx2 exactly; a 1x2 row is therefore one epoch and never two, which is the
  ambiguity finding M-1 records v1 as guessing at. mjdToEpoch is the function
  that reads [60310 43200] as two epochs.

ACCURACY

Exact. The day number and the seconds of day are stored as given, so the
  resolution is that of a double near 86400, which is 1.5e-11 s, and it does
  not degrade with the epoch. This is the reason the toolbox carries a split
  epoch at all; see the ACCURACY block of mjdToEpoch for the comparison.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:daySecondToEpoch:incorrectNumberOfColumns
  referenceSystem:timeScale:unknownScale

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.
  Handover section B.3 and finding M-1.

EXAMPLE

% The instant 2016-12-31T23:59:60.5 UTC, inside the leap second.
  epoch = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([57753 86400.5], "UTC");

KEYWORDS

epoch, day number, seconds of day, leap second, split precision

See also MJDTOEPOCH, EPOCHTO, NORMALIZEEPOCH.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.datetimeToEpoch

Epoch from a MATLAB datetime array.

SYNTAX

epoch = datetimeToEpoch(t, scale) converts a datetime array to the
  canonical epoch struct of handover section B.3, reading the datetimes as
  instants of the named time scale.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----            -----  -----------
  t       Nx1    datetime        -      Instants. A zoned datetime is
                                        converted to UTC first and then only
                                        scale "UTC" is accepted.
  scale   1x1    string | char   -      Time scale, any case, one of the
                                        names in timeScale.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epoch      1x1    struct    -      Canonical epoch, N entries.
  .day       Nx1    double    d      Integer day number.
  .sec       Nx1    double    s      Seconds of day.
  .scale     1x1    string    -      Canonical scale name.
  .source    1x1    string    -      "datetimeToEpoch".

METHOD

MJD 0 is 1858-11-17 00:00 by definition, so the day number is the whole
  number of days from that origin and the seconds of day are the remainder,
  both taken with datetime arithmetic rather than through datenum: a datenum
  near the present resolves only about 10 us, which would destroy the
  precision the epoch type exists to keep.

  An unzoned datetime carries no claim about its scale, so the caller states
  it. A zoned datetime does carry one - it is civil time, hence UTC - so it
  is converted to UTC and any scale other than "UTC" is refused rather than
  silently reinterpreted.

  The scale is required and has no default. A default would be a guess, and
  handing a UTC timetable to a TT-expecting routine is exactly the class of
  bug the scale field was introduced to remove.

ACCURACY

MATLAB datetime resolves about 1 ns over the range of interest, so the
  conversion is exact at the level this toolbox works at but does not reach
  the 1.5e-11 s resolution of daySecondToEpoch. datetime knows nothing about
  leap seconds, so a UTC instant inside a positive leap second cannot be
  expressed here at all; use daySecondToEpoch for that.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:datetimeToEpoch:zonedScaleMismatch
  referenceSystem:timeScale:unknownScale

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.
  ITU-R TF.460-6 (UTC and leap seconds).

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.datetimeToEpoch(datetime(2024,1,1,12,0,0), "TT");
  assert(epoch.sec == 43200);

KEYWORDS

epoch, datetime, MJD, time zone, UTC

See also MJDTOEPOCH, DAYSECONDTOEPOCH, EPOCHTO.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------

referenceSystem.gpsWeekToEpoch

Epoch from a GPS week number and seconds of week.

SYNTAX

epoch = gpsWeekToEpoch(week, secondOfWeek) converts the GPS broadcast time
  representation to the canonical epoch struct of handover section B.3. The
  returned scale is "GPS" by construction.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name           Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----           ----   ----     -----  -----------
  week           Nx1    double   week   Continuous GPS week number, counted
                                        from week 0 beginning 1980-01-06.
                                        Must already be unrolled; see METHOD.
  secondOfWeek   Nx1    double   s      Seconds since 0h of the Sunday that
                                        begins that week. Scalar is broadcast.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epoch      1x1    struct    -      Canonical epoch, N entries.
  .day       Nx1    double    d      Integer day number.
  .sec       Nx1    double    s      Seconds of day.
  .scale     1x1    string    -      "GPS".
  .source    1x1    string    -      "gpsWeekToEpoch".

METHOD

GPS week 0 begins at 1980-01-06T00:00:00 UTC, which is MJD 44244, and GPS
  time has run without leap seconds ever since, so
  day = 44244 + 7*week + floor(secondOfWeek/86400) with the remainder as the
  seconds of day. The arithmetic is done by normalizeEpoch, which carries
  exactly because GPS is a uniform scale.

  No scale argument is offered, because this representation names its own
  scale: a GPS week number is GPS time by definition. Galileo broadcasts the
  same week and second convention, and GST is nominally equal to GPS time, so
  a Galileo caller may use this constructor and then convert; the nominal
  nature of that equality is documented in convertTimeScale. BeiDou counts
  weeks from a different origin and is deliberately not accepted here.

  The week number must already be unrolled. The broadcast navigation message
  transmits it in ten bits, so it rolls over every 1024 weeks - in 1999, 2019
  and next in 2038 - and only the receiver knows which cycle a file belongs
  to. Guessing the cycle here would be finding M-1 in a new costume: it would
  be right most of the time and silently 19.6 years wrong otherwise.

ACCURACY

Exact. The week and day origins are integers and 604800 and 86400 are
  exactly representable, so no rounding enters beyond that of secondOfWeek.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:gpsWeekToEpoch:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:gpsWeekToEpoch:nonIntegerWeek

REFERENCES

IS-GPS-200N, Section 20.3.3.5.2.4 (GPS week number and its rollover).
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.gpsWeekToEpoch(2295, 86400*3 + 43200);
  tai = referenceSystem.convertTimeScale(epoch, "TAI");

KEYWORDS

epoch, GPS week, seconds of week, rollover, GNSS

See also MJDTOEPOCH, EPOCHTO, CONVERTTIMESCALE.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.j2000SecondsToEpoch

Epoch from seconds elapsed since the J2000.0 epoch.

SYNTAX

epoch = j2000SecondsToEpoch(secondsFromJ2000, scale) interprets its input
  as seconds since 2000-01-01T12:00:00 of the named scale, which is MJD
  51544.5, and returns the canonical epoch struct of handover section B.3.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name               Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----               ----   ----            -----  -----------
  secondsFromJ2000   Nx1    double          s      Seconds since J2000.0 in
                                                   the given scale. Negative
                                                   values precede it.
  scale              1x1    string | char   -      Time scale, any case, one
                                                   of the names in timeScale.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epoch      1x1    struct    -      Canonical epoch, N entries.
  .day       Nx1    double    d      Integer day number.
  .sec       Nx1    double    s      Seconds of day.
  .scale     1x1    string    -      Canonical scale name.
  .source    1x1    string    -      "j2000SecondsToEpoch".

METHOD

The J2000.0 origin is split before the seconds are added, as day 51544 plus
  43200 s, so the addition happens entirely inside the seconds field and the
  day number is never disturbed by a rounding. normalizeEpoch then carries.

  This is the representation an orbit integrator naturally produces, which is
  why it is a first-class constructor rather than something a caller derives.
  The scale is required: J2000.0 is defined in TT, but a state vector clock
  may well be counting TAI or GPS seconds from that instant, and assuming TT
  would be a silent 32.184 s error.

  For a UTC input the count is UTC seconds and therefore not proper time
  across a leap second; normalizeEpoch will refuse an offset larger than a
  day for that scale. Count in TAI or TT if the arc spans a leap second.

ACCURACY

Exact to the resolution of the input. For an arc of one day the seconds
  field stays below 1e5, where one unit in the last place is 1.5e-11 s, so
  the constructor adds nothing measurable. Over a century the input itself
  reaches 3e9 s, where one unit in the last place is 4.8e-7 s, and that
  limitation belongs to the caller's representation, not to this function.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:timeScale:unknownScale

REFERENCES

IAU 1994 Resolution C7 and IERS Conventions (2010), TN36 Ch. 1 (J2000.0).

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.j2000SecondsToEpoch(7.5e8, "TT");
  mjd = referenceSystem.epochTo(epoch, "mjd");

KEYWORDS

epoch, J2000, seconds since epoch, orbit integration

See also MJDTOEPOCH, DAYSECONDTOEPOCH, EPOCHTO.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------

back to contents

referenceSystem.epochTo

Convert an epoch struct to one of the five external representations.

SYNTAX

value = epochTo(epoch, format) returns the epoch in the requested outbound
  representation. format is one of "mjd", "daySecond", "datetime", "gpsWeek"
  or "j2000Seconds".

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----     ----   ----            -----  -----------
  epoch    1x1    struct          -      Epoch struct, N entries; see
                                         normalizeEpoch.
  format   1x1    string | char   -      Target representation, see above.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type                Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----                -----  -----------
  value   Nx1    double | datetime   -      "mjd": decimal MJD, days.
          Nx2    double              -      "daySecond": [day secondsOfDay].
          Nx1    datetime            -      "datetime": unzoned instants.
          Nx2    double              -      "gpsWeek": [week secondOfWeek].
          Nx1    double              s      "j2000Seconds": seconds since
                                            2000-01-01T12:00 of the scale.

METHOD

The outbound direction is one function where the inbound direction is five
  (decision D32), and the asymmetry is deliberate rather than an oversight.
  A constructor receives a bare numeric array, which cannot declare what it
  is, so the function name has to fix the interpretation - that is what makes
  finding M-1 unreachable. Every outbound call instead receives an epoch
  struct, which can only be an epoch, so there is no ambiguity left for five
  names to resolve and five names would buy symmetry rather than safety
  (handover section C.7 rule 6).

  Each format is the exact inverse of its constructor: "mjd" of mjdToEpoch,
  "daySecond" of daySecondToEpoch, and so on. "gpsWeek" is defined only for a
  GPS epoch and refuses any other scale rather than converting silently,
  because a week number is a statement about GPS time.

  MJD 0 is 1858-11-17 00:00 by definition; datetimeToEpoch carries the same
  origin for the inbound direction and a precision test binds the two.

ACCURACY

"daySecond" and "gpsWeek" are exact: they move integers and the unmodified
  seconds field. "mjd" and "j2000Seconds" collapse the split form back into
  one double and therefore lose resolution - 0.63 us near MJD 60000 for a
  decimal MJD, 0.48 us near 2100 for seconds from J2000 - so a round trip
  through them reproduces the original to about two units in the last place
  and no better. "datetime" resolves about 1 ns and cannot represent an
  instant inside a positive leap second at all, which is why that case is an
  error rather than a rounding.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:epochTo:wrongScale
  referenceSystem:epochTo:unrepresentableLeapSecond
  MATLAB:validators:mustBeMember

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.
  IS-GPS-200N, Section 20.3.3.5.2.4 (GPS week number).
  Handover decision D32 and section C.7 rule 6.

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([60310 43200], "TT");
  assert(isequal(referenceSystem.epochTo(epoch, "daySecond"), [60310 43200]));

KEYWORDS

epoch, MJD, datetime, GPS week, J2000, round trip

See also MJDTOEPOCH, DAYSECONDTOEPOCH, DATETIMETOEPOCH, GPSWEEKTOEPOCH,
  J2000SECONDSTOEPOCH.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------

referenceSystem.addSeconds

Advance an epoch by a number of seconds of its own time scale.

SYNTAX

epochOut = addSeconds(epochIn, offset) adds offset seconds to every entry
  of epochIn and returns the normalised result in the same time scale.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size        Type     Units  Description
  ----       ----        ----     -----  -----------
  epochIn    1x1         struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries.
  offset     1x1 | Nx1   double   s      Seconds to add. May be negative.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epochOut   1x1    struct    -      Epoch struct, N entries, same scale.
  .source    1x1    string    -      "addSeconds".

METHOD

The offset is added to the seconds field only, and normalizeEpoch performs
  the carry. Neither the day number nor the offset is ever routed through a
  decimal MJD, which is what keeps the operation exact over decades.

  On a uniform scale a second of the scale is a second of proper time, so the
  result is unambiguous. On UTC and GLONASS it is not: an interval that
  spans a positive leap second contains one more second of proper time than
  its UTC seconds-of-day arithmetic suggests. This function therefore
  computes the exact answer where no leap second lies in the interval, and
  raises an error naming the offending entries where one does, rather than
  returning a value that is silently one second wrong. Convert to TAI, add
  there, and convert back - which is unambiguous by construction.

ACCURACY

Exact to the resolution of the seconds field, which is 1.5e-11 s near
  86400 and 2.2e-16 s near 1. Repeated accumulation is bounded by the number
  of additions times half that resolution, so it depends on where in the day
  the accumulation happens rather than on how long the toolbox has been
  running: ten thousand additions of 1e-4 s starting at 0h reproduce 1 s to
  within 1.1e-12 s, and the precision test asserts better than 1e-9 s. One
  million additions of 1e-6 s carry the same bound, because the seconds field
  never leaves [0, 1] in either case.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:addSeconds:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:addSeconds:leapSecondCrossed

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.
  ITU-R TF.460-6 (UTC leap seconds).

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([60310 86399.5], "TT");
  later = referenceSystem.addSeconds(epoch, 1.0);
  assert(later.day == 60311 && abs(later.sec - 0.5) < 1e-9);

KEYWORDS

epoch, arithmetic, offset, leap second, precision

See also SECONDSBETWEEN, NORMALIZEEPOCH, CONVERTTIMESCALE.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:20 (CEST, UTC+02:00)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------

back to contents

referenceSystem.secondsBetween

Seconds elapsed from one epoch to another in the same scale.

SYNTAX

interval = secondsBetween(fromEpoch, toEpoch) returns toEpoch minus
  fromEpoch in seconds. Both epochs must be in the same time scale.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  fromEpoch   1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries or one entry.
  toEpoch     1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries or one entry.

  The arguments are not named epochFrom and epochTo, which would read better,
  because epochTo is the name of a sibling function: a parameter of that name
  would shadow it inside this file and would be reported by the section C.5
  namespace scan, which flags any bare occurrence of a sibling name.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----  -----------
  interval   Nx1    double   s      Elapsed seconds, positive when toEpoch
                                    follows fromEpoch.

METHOD

interval = (dayTo - dayFrom)*86400 + (secTo - secFrom). The day difference
  is an exact integer subtraction and the seconds difference stays below
  86401 in magnitude, so no cancellation of large nearly-equal numbers occurs
  and the result is exact to the resolution of the seconds fields. Forming
  the same quantity from two decimal MJDs would lose about 1 us per operand.

  The scales must match. Differencing a TT epoch against a TAI one is a
  32.184 s error that no test of the result would reveal, so it is refused
  rather than computed. As in addSeconds, an interval that spans a leap
  second is refused on UTC and GLONASS, because the count of seconds of day
  between two such epochs is not the elapsed proper time.

ACCURACY

Exact to about 1.5e-11 s for intervals within a day and about 2e-9 s for
  intervals of a century, both set by the representation of the operands
  rather than by this function.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:secondsBetween:scaleMismatch
  referenceSystem:secondsBetween:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:addSeconds:leapSecondCrossed  (raised on a leap-bearing
      scale; the check is delegated to addSeconds so that one implementation
      of it exists rather than two, and it therefore keeps that identifier)

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1.

EXAMPLE

a = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([60310 0], "TT");
  b = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([60311 43200], "TT");
  assert(referenceSystem.secondsBetween(a, b) == 129600);

KEYWORDS

epoch, interval, elapsed time, difference, precision

See also ADDSECONDS, NORMALIZEEPOCH, CONVERTTIMESCALE.

referenceSystem.leapSecondTable

The single leap-second authority of the toolbox.

SYNTAX

deltaAt   = leapSecondTable("deltaAT", epoch)   TAI - UTC per epoch, seconds
  table     = leapSecondTable("list")             the full step table
  table     = leapSecondTable("load", file)       install a session override
  table     = leapSecondTable("reset")            drop the override
  lastDate  = leapSecondTable("validThrough")     end of the table's coverage
  state     = leapSecondTable("state")            which table is in force

  No other file in this toolbox contains a leap-second value or date. v1
  carried three independent hardcoded tables, all frozen at 2017-01-01 and
  free to diverge, which is finding M-2; a contract test now greps the source
  tree for the pattern and fails if a fourth appears.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----            -----  -----------
  command    1x1    string | char   -      One of the commands listed above.
  argument   1x1    struct          -      For "deltaAT": the UTC epoch.
             1x1    string | char   -      For "load": the file to read.
                                           Not accepted by other commands.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                Size   Type        Units  Description
  ----                ----   ----        -----  -----------
  result              Nx1    double      s      "deltaAT": TAI - UTC.
                      Mx2    timetable   -      "list", "load", "reset": the
                                                step table, row times the UTC
                                                instant each step takes
                                                effect, variables mjd (the
                                                same instant as an integer
                                                MJD) and deltaAt (seconds).
                      1x1    datetime    -      "validThrough".
                      1x1    struct      -      "state", fields below.
  .source             1x1    string      -      "SOFA iauDat" or the override
                                                file that replaced it.
  .stepCount          1x1    double      -      Rows in the active table.
  .lastChange         1x1    datetime    -      Most recent step.
  .lastDeltaAt        1x1    double      s      TAI - UTC after that step.
  .validThrough       1x1    datetime    -      As the command of that name.

METHOD

The table is not stored here. It is discovered by asking the bundled,
  unmodified SOFA iauDat through the MEX gateway for TAI - UTC on every day
  from 1972-01-01 to 2200-01-01 and recording where the answer changes. There
  is therefore exactly one copy of the data in the toolbox, in mex/sofa/dat.c,
  and updating SOFA updates the toolbox with no MATLAB edit at all. The scan
  is about 83000 iauDat evaluations in one vectorised call, takes a few
  milliseconds and is cached in a persistent variable for the session.

  The coverage end date is discovered the same way: iauDat flags a year more
  than five years beyond its own release with status +1, and validThrough is
  the day before the first such day. It is not a literal here either.

  TRAP, measured against SOFA 20231011 and the reason this function
  range-checks rather than trusting the status. iauDat answers a pre-1960
  date with status +1 - a warning, not an error - and leaves TAI - UTC at
  zero. A layer that applied section C.6 mechanically would turn that into a
  warning and hand back a silent zero. UTC before 1972-01-01 is a rate-based
  fractional offset that this toolbox's integer-second machinery does not
  model, so such an epoch is an error here, named as such.

  Section C.6 otherwise applies unchanged: status +1 from a supported epoch
  becomes one aggregated dubiousYear warning per call, whatever N is, and a
  negative status becomes an error naming the offending epochs. No numeric
  status is ever returned.

  Concurrency, section C.9 rule 1. The override lives in a persistent
  variable, so it lives in the memory of the process that installed it and is
  invisible inside a parfor worker, which would silently fall back to the
  SOFA table. Install the override inside the parfor body, or pass the table
  in as sliced data. leapSecondTable("state") reports which table answered.

  THE COLUMNS "deltaAT" READS ARE DERIVED ONCE, NOT PER CALL (prompt P28b,
  rev. 1.122). The step days, the offsets, the discretize edges, the date of
  the first row and the coverage end AS A DAY NUMBER are all fixed for the
  life of the active table, and are computed when that table is installed:
  after the SOFA scan, after "load" and after "reset". A profiled single-epoch
  rotation reached this function six times, four of them on "deltaAT", and
  each of those four built a datetime for 1858-11-17, subtracted it, called
  days and floored the result to recover a number the scan already knew, then
  indexed the timetable three times for columns that had not changed.

  The cache is not an optimisation of the lookup: the lookup was already one
  discretize on integer day numbers. It removes work that was never a function
  of the epoch. It cannot go stale, because the only three ways the active
  table can change are the three places the columns are refreshed, and an
  override that fails to load leaves both the table and the columns as they
  were.

ACCURACY

Exact. TAI - UTC is an integer number of seconds from 1972-01-01 onward and
  is returned as such. The only inexactness anywhere near this function is
  the day-boundary lookup, which is exact because a step always occurs at 0h
  UTC and the lookup is on the integer day number.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:gatewayMissing
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:beforeUtcEpoch
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:wrongScale
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:unacceptableDate
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:missingArgument
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:unsupportedFormat
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:unreadableFile
  MATLAB:validators:mustBeMember

WARNINGS

referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:dubiousYear   One per call, however large N is.
      Raised from 2029-01-01 on the bundled SOFA release, because iauDat
      flags any year more than five beyond its own IYV. It is SOFA's
      reminder that leap seconds cannot be predicted, not a defect.

REFERENCES

IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauDat (mex/sofa/dat.c).
  ITU-R TF.460-6, Standard-frequency and time-signal emissions.
  IERS Bulletin C; IERS/IANA leap-seconds.list (NTP format).
  Handover finding M-2, decision D3 and sections C.6 and C.9.

EXAMPLE

epoch = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([57754 0], "UTC");
  assert(referenceSystem.leapSecondTable("deltaAT", epoch) == 37);
  steps = referenceSystem.leapSecondTable("list");

KEYWORDS

leap second, TAI, UTC, deltaAT, iauDat, IERS Bulletin C, override

See also CONVERTTIMESCALE, NORMALIZEEPOCH, MEXINFO.

referenceSystem.convertTimeScale

Convert an epoch between any two supported time scales.

SYNTAX

epochOut = convertTimeScale(epochIn, scaleOut)
  epochOut = convertTimeScale(epochIn, scaleOut, Name, Value)

  Supported scales are TAI, TT, TDB, TCG, TCB, UTC, UT1, GPS, GST, BDT and
  GLONASS. This is the single point of access for time in the toolbox; with
  the namespace in place, referenceSystem.convertTimeScale is already that,
  and no convenience wrapper is provided (design principle 12).

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----            -----  -----------
  epochIn    1x1    struct          -      Epoch struct, N entries.
  scaleOut   1x1    string | char   -      Target scale, any case.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size        Type     Default  Description
  ----         ----        ----     -------  -----------
  DeltaUt1     1x1 | Nx1   double   none     UT1 - UTC in seconds, the IERS
                                             quantity written Delta UT1.
                                             Scalar or one value per epoch.
                                             Used only when UT1 is the source
                                             or the target scale. OMITTING IT
                                             THERE MEANS ZERO AND WARNS: zero
                                             can be wrong by up to 0.9 s,
                                             which is 15 arcseconds of Earth
                                             rotation, and nothing in the
                                             result shows it. Pass an
                                             explicit 0 to accept zero
                                             silently. See WARNINGS.
  Observer     3x1 | 3xN   double   [0;0;0]  Observer position in the ITRS,
                                             metres, one column per epoch or
                                             one for all. Used only when TDB
                                             or TCB is the source or the
                                             target scale. The default is the
                                             geocentre, for which the
                                             topocentric terms vanish exactly.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type      Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----      -----  -----------
  epochOut   1x1    struct    -      Epoch struct, N entries, in scaleOut.
  .source    1x1    string    -      "convertTimeScale from <scaleIn>".

METHOD

Hub and spoke through TAI, as in v1, because that design was right: every
  scale has exactly one defined relation to TAI, so eleven scales need eleven
  relations rather than fifty-five, and a new scale costs one function arm.

    TT   = TAI + 32.184 s                                   exact, defining
    GPS  = TAI - 19 s,  GST = GPS,  BDT = TAI - 33 s        see ACCURACY
    UTC  = TAI - Delta AT                                   leapSecondTable
    UT1  = UTC + Delta UT1                                  measured, IERS
    GLONASS = UTC + 3 h                                     defining
    TCG  = TT  + L_G/(1 - L_G)*(TT - T0)                    IAU 2000 B1.9
    TDB  = TT  + (TDB - TT)                                 SOFA iauDtdb
    TCB  = TDB + L_B/(1 - L_B)*(TDB - T0) - TDB0/(1 - L_B)  IAU 2006 B3

  with T0 the instant 1977-01-01T00:00:32.184 TAI, taken from iersConstants
  rather than restated here.

  Finding C-1, and why the v1 TDB series is gone rather than repaired. The v1
  fbSeries table was numerically corrupted: amplitudes stood in SOFA order
  while frequencies and phases were misaligned from the second row onward,
  several rows were duplicated and the T-polynomial groups were absent. Its
  claimed accuracy of 2 ns was wrong by three orders of magnitude - measured
  against SOFA, the table is in error by up to 1.7e-4 s over 2020 to 2024.
  TDB - TT is now obtained from the unmodified SOFA iauDtdb through the MEX
  gateway, and the regression test asserts agreement with the published
  Astronomical Almanac approximation to better than 3e-5 s, a tolerance that
  the corrupted table fails by a wide margin.

  TDB to TT inverts that relation by evaluating iauDtdb at the TDB argument
  instead of at the TT one. SOFA states the argument may be either; the two
  differ by at most 1.7 ms and the rate of TDB - TT is below 3e-8, so the
  inversion error stays under 1e-10 s and no iteration is needed.

  Finding C-3, and why UTC survives a leap second. TAI - UTC is looked up on
  the integer day number, never on a decimal MJD, so it keeps its pre-step
  value throughout the leap second. In the other direction the UTC day is
  found by trying the TAI day first and stepping back one day when the
  seconds go negative, which lands an instant inside the leap second on the
  preceding day with a seconds-of-day value at or above 86400 - the one
  representation that exists for it. UTC to TAI to UTC across
  2016-12-31T23:59:60 is therefore the exact identity, and a regression test
  asserts it.

  GLONASS cannot represent the leap second at all: it runs three hours ahead
  of UTC, so the inserted second falls in the middle of a GLONASS day where
  the day-and-second form has no room for it. Converting such an instant to
  GLONASS is an error rather than a silent collision with the second before.

ACCURACY

TT, GPS, BDT, TCG, TCB and the TAI hub are exact to the resolution of the
  epoch, about 1.5e-11 s: every offset is a defining constant and every
  operation is a single addition inside the seconds field.

  TDB - TT carries the accuracy of SOFA iauDtdb, which reproduces the
  Fairhead and Bretagnon series to a few nanoseconds at the geocentre. With
  Observer left at the geocentre the topocentric terms vanish identically, so
  that value is exact for a geocentric epoch rather than an approximation of
  one.

  THE TOPOCENTRIC TERM IS NOT SMALL AND ITS SIZE IS MEASURED, NOT QUOTED.
  Swept over one day at MJD 60000 with iauDtdb through the gateway, a station
  on the equator departs from the geocentric value by -1.990e-6 to +1.992e-6 s
  - about 4 us peak to peak, three orders above the series' own accuracy. A
  station at the pole shows 5.8e-9 s peak to peak about a constant offset of
  -7.7e-7 s, because the diurnal term is proportional to the distance from the
  spin axis and vanishes there while the term in the distance north of the
  equator does not. Anything correlating a clock at a station, and any
  GRACE-like KBR or LRI reduction, needs Observer set.

  THE UT1 FRACTION iauDtdb ASKS FOR IS OBTAINED HERE FROM UTC PLUS DeltaUt1,
  AND THE COST OF GETTING IT WRONG IS BOUNDED RATHER THAN ASSUMED. The
  topocentric term is diurnal with an amplitude of 2 us, so its rate is
  1.5e-10 s per second of UT error; taking DeltaUt1 as zero, an error of at
  most 0.9 s, moves TDB - TT by at most 1.3e-10 s. That is below the series'
  own few-nanosecond accuracy, so DeltaUt1 is used when supplied and its
  absence is not an error.

  UTC is exact given the leap-second table; see leapSecondTable for the coverage
  of that table and for the warning raised beyond it. UT1 is exact given
  Delta UT1, whose own accuracy is that of the IERS series supplied, of order
  10 us for the C04 product and 20 to 30 us for a prediction.

  GST = GPS and BDT = TAI - 33 s are nominal, not realised. The broadcast
  system times differ from these definitions at the few-nanosecond to
  few-tens-of-nanosecond level, and the offsets are themselves broadcast
  quantities. Anything that needs the realised offset must apply it
  separately; this function returns the definitional value.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:convertTimeScale:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:convertTimeScale:unrepresentableLeapSecond
  referenceSystem:convertTimeScale:gatewayMissing
  referenceSystem:convertTimeScale:beforeUtcEpoch
      Raised HERE, by this function, when a TAI epoch maps to a UTC day
      before the leap-second table begins. It was named
      referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:beforeUtcEpoch until revision 1.52,
      which contradicted the binding section C.2 rule that an identifier
      names the function that raised it - the same correction revision 1.36
      made to the epoch scale check. leapSecondTable raises an identifier of
      its own name for its own range check; the two are distinct conditions
      in distinct functions and must be catchable separately.
  referenceSystem:timeScale:unknownScale

WARNINGS

referenceSystem:convertTimeScale:deltaUt1Defaulted   Raised once per call
      when UT1 is the source or the target scale and DeltaUt1 was omitted.
      The conversion proceeds with zero, and the caller is told what that
      costs. An EXPLICIT 'DeltaUt1', 0 is a decision and does not warn -
      decision D67's rule that a consequential physical choice is never
      silently defaulted, applied here (decision D113, rev. 1.184).
  referenceSystem:leapSecondTable:dubiousYear   Raised once per call by
      leapSecondTable when an epoch lies beyond the table's coverage.

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 1 and Table 1.1.
  IAU 2000 Resolution B1.9 (L_G); IAU 2006 Resolution B3 (L_B, TDB0).
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauDtdb and iauDat.
  Fairhead, L. and Bretagnon, P., 1990, Astron. Astrophys. 229, 240.
  Handover findings C-1 and C-3.

EXAMPLE

utc = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([57753 86400.5], "UTC");
  tai = referenceSystem.convertTimeScale(utc, "TAI");
  back = referenceSystem.convertTimeScale(tai, "UTC");
  assert(back.day == utc.day && back.sec == utc.sec);

KEYWORDS

time scale, TAI, TT, TDB, TCG, TCB, UTC, UT1, GPS, BDT, GLONASS, leap second

See also TIMESCALE, LEAPSECONDS, NORMALIZEEPOCH, EPOCHTO.

Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-24 19:55 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.fundamentalArguments

Delaunay, planetary and Doodson arguments of nutation and tides.

SYNTAX

fa = fundamentalArguments(epoch)
  fa = fundamentalArguments(epoch, 'Ut1Epoch', ut1Epoch)

  Returns all three argument sets, each field an Nx1 column in radians
  wrapped to [0, 2*pi), computed from a TT epoch. The three sets are named
  explicitly because "fundamental arguments" is ambiguous and feeding a tidal
  model the wrong set is a silent error.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----  -----------
  epoch      1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries, scale TT.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Default  Description
  ----       ----   ----     -------  -----------
  Ut1Epoch   1x1    struct   none     Epoch struct, N entries, scale UT1, at
                                      the same instants as epoch. Required
                                      for the Doodson argument tau and used
                                      for nothing else.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  fa          1x1    struct   -      Fields below, IERS symbols verbatim.
  .l          Nx1    double   rad    Delaunay: mean anomaly of the Moon.
  .lPrime     Nx1    double   rad    Delaunay: mean anomaly of the Sun.
  .F          Nx1    double   rad    Delaunay: L - Omega.
  .D          Nx1    double   rad    Delaunay: mean elongation of the Moon
                                     from the Sun.
  .Omega      Nx1    double   rad    Delaunay: mean longitude of the
                                     ascending node of the Moon.
  .lMe        Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Mercury.
  .lVe        Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Venus.
  .lE         Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Earth.
  .lMa        Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Mars.
  .lJ         Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Jupiter.
  .lSa        Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Saturn.
  .lU         Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Uranus.
  .lNe        Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: mean longitude of Neptune.
  .pA         Nx1    double   rad    Planetary: general accumulated
                                     precession in longitude. NOT wrapped,
                                     see METHOD.
  .tau        Nx1    double   rad    Doodson: mean lunar time. NaN unless
                                     Ut1Epoch was supplied.
  .s          Nx1    double   rad    Doodson: mean longitude of the Moon.
  .h          Nx1    double   rad    Doodson: mean longitude of the Sun.
  .p          Nx1    double   rad    Doodson: longitude of lunar perigee.
  .nPrime     Nx1    double   rad    Doodson: negative longitude of the
                                     lunar node.
  .pStar      Nx1    double   rad    Doodson: longitude of solar perigee.
  .scale      1x1    string   -      "TT", the scale the arguments were
                                     computed from, carried so a consumer
                                     cannot lose it.

METHOD

The Delaunay and planetary sets come from the unmodified SOFA routines
  iauFal03, iauFalp03, iauFaf03, iauFad03, iauFaom03 and iauFame03 through
  iauFapa03, evaluated in C through the MEX gateway. They are fifth-degree
  polynomials in Julian centuries of TT, about a hundred floating-point
  operations for the whole set.

  The Doodson set is derived from them by the relations of IERS Conventions
  (2010) Chapter 6:

      s      = F + Omega            mean longitude of the Moon
      h      = s - D                mean longitude of the Sun
      p      = s - l                longitude of lunar perigee
      nPrime = -Omega               negative longitude of the lunar node
      pStar  = s - D - lPrime       longitude of solar perigee
      tau    = theta + pi - s       mean lunar time, theta = GMST

  It is returned rather than left to the caller because the solid-Earth and
  ocean-tide models of Chapter 6 use exactly this set, and the tau relation
  in particular is easy to get wrong. Field names follow the IERS symbols
  verbatim (handover section C.7 Tier 1) rather than the function-name
  grammar, which is why they are single letters.

  tau needs Greenwich Mean Sidereal Time and therefore UT1, which is a
  measured quantity from the Earth orientation series and not a function of
  TT. This is layer L0 and cannot reach the L1 series, so the caller supplies
  the UT1 epoch when it wants tau. Without it tau is NaN - the documented
  undefined-but-valid value permitted by section C.6 rule 5, chosen over an
  error so that a caller wanting only the Delaunay set is not obstructed, and
  over a silent substitution of UTC for UT1, which would be wrong by up to
  0.9 s of Earth rotation, that is 12 arcseconds. The other five Doodson
  arguments do not depend on UT1 and are always filled.

  pA is the one field not wrapped to [0, 2*pi) (decision D52). It is the
  accumulated general precession, a secular quantity rather than a cyclic
  angle: it passes through zero at J2000.0 and reaches only about 0.16 rad
  over 1400 to 2600, so it never needs a wrap, and wrapping it would report
  a value near 2*pi for every epoch before J2000.0 and mislead anyone reading
  it directly. Inside a trigonometric series the two forms are identical.

  The epoch must be TT. A non-TT epoch is refused rather than converted,
  because a silent conversion is how a tidal model ends up driven by the
  wrong time scale, and because the caller that has a UTC epoch also has the
  information needed to convert it deliberately.

  Decision D27, the duplication this function exposes. The toolbox computes
  these arguments in two places: SOFA iauFa*03 here, and the ported IERS
  FUNDARG inside subdailyEopCorrection and zonalTideEopCorrection. Both are
  kept. This function is the public authority and no other MATLAB-level
  function may compute the arguments another way; the ported IERS routines
  keep their own FUNDARG byte-intact so that their published test-case
  validation stands unmodified. The agreement between the two is measured by
  a test rather than assumed, and the achieved number is in ACCURACY below.

ACCURACY

The polynomials are exact to their published coefficients; the evaluation
  adds a few units in the last place, below 1e-15 rad. Their physical
  accuracy is that of Simon et al. (1994), a few microarcseconds per century
  over the twentieth and twenty-first centuries and degrading outside them.

  Decision D27 agreement, re-measured in session S2 across 1960 to 2100
  rather than at the single epoch S1 used. SOFA iauFa*03 against the ported
  IERS FUNDARG in mex/iers/:

      l, F, Omega    agree exactly, to the bit, at every epoch
      lPrime         9.75e-12 rad = 2.01 microarcseconds, worst case
      D              9.83e-12 rad = 2.03 microarcseconds, worst case

  S1 recorded "better than 1e-13 rad" from one epoch. That was wrong, and
  the sweep is what found it. The discrepancy is not noise and it is not
  random: it is confined to exactly the two arguments whose leading constant
  the supplied FUNDARG.F carries with two digits fewer than SOFA does,
  1287104.79305 against 1287104.793048 and 1072260.70369 against
  1072260.703692. Two truncated digits of 2e-6 arcsec each account for the
  whole 9.7e-12 rad.

  Note which one is right: SOFA reproduces the test case printed in
  FUNDARG.F's own header to 1e-16, and the supplied FUNDARG.F does not
  reproduce it. The file is internally inconsistent. This function uses SOFA
  and is therefore on the correct constants; the ported routines keep the
  file's constants so that their own published validation still stands
  (D27), and 2 microarcseconds of lunisolar argument reaches the tidal
  models as under 1e-9 microarcseconds of polar motion, which is nothing.

  tau inherits the accuracy of the supplied Delta UT1, of order 10 us for the
  IERS C04 series, which is 4.4e-9 rad of Earth rotation; that dominates
  everything else in this function by six orders of magnitude.

  The wrap to [0, 2*pi) is exact in the sense that it adds a multiple of
  2*pi; near the wrap point the relative resolution of the result is
  therefore that of a number near 2*pi, about 4.4e-16 rad, and continuity
  across the wrap is asserted by a precision test.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:fundamentalArguments:wrongScale
  referenceSystem:fundamentalArguments:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:fundamentalArguments:gatewayMissing

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.7.1 to 5.7.2 and
    Ch. 6 (Doodson arguments and the tidal models that use them).
  Simon, J.-L. et al., 1994, Astron. Astrophys. 282, 663.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauFal03 to iauFapa03 and iauGmst06.
  IERS Conventions software FUNDARG.F, published test case.

EXAMPLE

tt = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([54465 0], "TT");
  fa = referenceSystem.fundamentalArguments(tt);
  assert(abs(fa.l - 2.291187512612069) < 1e-12);

KEYWORDS

fundamental arguments, Delaunay, Doodson, nutation, tides, SOFA, FUNDARG

See also CONVERTTIMESCALE, NORMALIZEEPOCH, TIMESCALE.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
