referenceSystem.earthRotationAngle

Earth rotation angle and its rate of advance.

SYNTAX

era = earthRotationAngle(epochUt1)
  [era, eraRate] = earthRotationAngle(epochUt1)
  [era, eraRate] = earthRotationAngle(epochUt1, 'LengthOfDay', lod)

  Returns the Earth rotation angle, the angle between the Celestial and the
  Terrestrial Intermediate Origin measured along the CIP equator, and its
  time derivative. ERA replaced Greenwich sidereal time as the measure of
  Earth rotation in the IAU 2000 resolutions: it is rigorously proportional
  to UT1, which sidereal time is not.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----  -----------
  epoch   1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries, scale UT1.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name          Size        Type     Default  Description
  ----          ----        ----     -------  -----------
  LengthOfDay   1x1 | Nx1   double   0        Excess length of day in
                                              seconds, the IERS quantity LOD.
                                              Corrects the rate; does not
                                              affect the angle.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name      Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----      ----   ----     -----    -----------
  era       Nx1    double   rad      Earth rotation angle in [0, 2*pi).
  eraRate   Nx1    double   rad/s    d(ERA)/dt, corrected for LOD.

METHOD

The angle comes from SOFA iauEra00, which evaluates the IAU 2000 defining
  expression exactly:

      ERA(Tu) = 2*pi * (0.7790572732640 + 1.00273781191135448 * Tu)

  with Tu the UT1 Julian date interval from 2451545.0. Because the expression
  is linear, the rate of advance is a constant of the definition and needs no
  differentiation.

  THAT CONSTANT IS NOT THE MEAN ROTATION RATE OF THE EARTH, and the two are
  easy to confuse because both are written omega. iersConstants supplies
  earthRotationRate = 7.292115e-5 rad/s, the nominal mean angular velocity of
  IERS Conventions Table 1.1. The rate of advance of ERA is
  7.2921151467e-5 rad/s. They differ by 1.47e-12 rad/s, a relative 2.0e-8,
  which is 10.3 micrometres per second of co-rotation velocity at a low Earth
  orbit radius of 7000 km. That is the same order as the LOD correction this
  function offers and two hundred times the polar-motion rate term, so using
  the wrong one is not a rounding question (decision D63).

  The rate is therefore not a literal here. It is measured once per session
  from iauEra00 itself, by differencing the angle over a 1000 s interval of
  UT1, and cached. The difference is exact apart from roundoff because the
  expression is linear, and it keeps SOFA as the single authority for the
  defining constant exactly as leapSecondTable keeps iauDat as the single
  authority for the leap seconds.

  LOD is the excess of the observed length of day over 86400 s. It reduces
  the rate in proportion:

      omega = omega_ERA * (1 - LOD / 86400)

  which reproduces the IERS relation 72921151.467064 - 0.843994809 * LOD
  picoradians per second with LOD in milliseconds, to the last digit of both
  coefficients. Over the supplied 20 C04 fixture LOD reaches 2.18 ms, worth
  12.5 micrometres per second at 7000 km, so the correction is applied by
  default wherever a rate is used in this toolbox.

  The angle is unaffected by LOD. LOD describes how fast the Earth is turning
  now; where it has turned to is already in UT1.

ACCURACY

The angle is that of iauEra00, exact to the last place of the defining
  expression given UT1, so its accuracy is entirely that of the supplied
  Delta UT1: of order 10 us for an IERS C04 series, which is 7.3e-10 rad of
  rotation, or 4.6 mm at the equator.

  The derived rate measures 7.292115146705669e-5 rad/s against the exact
  7.292115146706979e-5, a difference of 1.3e-18 rad/s carried by the
  1000 s differencing interval. At 7000 km that is 9 picometres per second,
  eleven orders below the LOD term it sits beside.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:earthRotationAngle:wrongScale
  referenceSystem:earthRotationAngle:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:earthRotationAngle:gatewayMissing

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.5.3 and Table 1.1.
  Capitaine, N., Guinot, B. and McCarthy, D.D., 2000, Astron. Astrophys.
    355, 398 (the definition of ERA).
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauEra00.

EXAMPLE

ut1 = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([54195 43199.927926315], "UT1");
  [era, rate] = referenceSystem.earthRotationAngle(ut1);
  assert(abs(rad2deg(era) - 13.318492966097) < 1e-10);

KEYWORDS

ERA, Earth rotation angle, UT1, CIO, TIO, length of day, LOD, SOFA

See also GREENWICHSIDEREALTIME, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, INTERPOLATEEOP.

Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-25 21:55 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.greenwichSiderealTime

Greenwich mean or apparent sidereal time.

SYNTAX

gmst = greenwichSiderealTime(epochUt1, epochTt)
  gast = greenwichSiderealTime(epochUt1, epochTt, 'Type', "apparent")

  Returns Greenwich sidereal time, the hour angle of the equinox at
  Greenwich, consistent with the IAU 2006 precession and the IAU 2000A
  nutation. Mean sidereal time is referred to the mean equinox of date,
  apparent sidereal time to the true equinox, the difference being the
  equation of the equinoxes.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----  -----------
  epochUt1   1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries, scale UT1.
  epochTt    1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries, scale TT, at the
                                    same instants as epochUt1.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----   ----   ----     -------   -----------
  Type   1x1    string   "mean"    "mean" for GMST or "apparent" for GAST.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  gst    Nx1    double   rad    Sidereal time in [0, 2*pi).

METHOD

SOFA iauGmst06 and iauGst06a, unmodified.

  BOTH TIME SCALES ARE REQUIRED AND NEITHER IS OPTIONAL. Sidereal time is
  not a function of one scale: UT1 carries the rotation of the Earth and TT
  carries the precession, and for apparent sidereal time also the nutation.
  That is precisely why ERA replaced sidereal time as the measure of Earth
  rotation in the IAU 2000 resolutions - ERA needs UT1 alone. Passing one
  epoch twice is therefore not a shortcut but an error of up to 0.9 s of
  rotation, and this function refuses it by demanding the scale of each
  argument explicitly.

  WHY THIS IS PUBLIC (risk R13b, decision D16). This toolbox transforms
  through the CIO and the Earth rotation angle, and no user-facing option
  selects the classical equinox route. Sidereal time is nevertheless a Tier 1
  quantity that users need in their own right - for hour angles, for
  telescope pointing, for reading older literature - and it is the ingredient
  of the equinox-versus-CIO cross-check that TestRotation runs continuously.
  Exposing it as a function is what turns that cross-check from a private
  assertion into something a user can reproduce.

  The equation of the origins, ERA - GAST, is the distance from the equinox
  to the CIO and is the bridge between the two conventions. It is obtained
  here as the difference of two public functions rather than as a third one.

ACCURACY

iauGmst06 and iauGst06a are exact evaluations of their published series
  given the epochs, so the error is that of the supplied Delta UT1, of order
  10 us for an IERS C04 series, that is 7.3e-10 rad.

  Consistency with the CIO route, measured over twelve epochs spanning 1990
  to 2050: forming Rz(-(ERA - GAST)) times the equinox-based nutation-
  precession-bias matrix reproduces the CIO-based GCRS to CIRS matrix to
  4.4e-16 rad worst element, which is 9.1e-5 microarcseconds. The two routes
  agree at the level of double-precision arithmetic and not merely at the
  1 microarcsecond the design asked for.

  Note that GAST here uses the unmodified IAU 2006/2000A nutation. Comparing
  it against a published value computed with observed pole offsets applied,
  as the SOFA cookbook section 5.4 does, differs by about 490
  microarcseconds at 2007 - that is the offsets, not a disagreement.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:greenwichSiderealTime:wrongScale
  referenceSystem:greenwichSiderealTime:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:greenwichSiderealTime:gatewayMissing

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.5.7.
  Capitaine, N., Wallace, P.T. and Chapront, J., 2003, Astron. Astrophys.
    412, 567.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauGmst06, iauGst06a.

EXAMPLE

ut1 = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([54195 43199.927926315], "UT1");
  tt  = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([54195 43265.184], "TT");
  gast = referenceSystem.greenwichSiderealTime(ut1, tt, 'Type', "apparent");
  era  = referenceSystem.earthRotationAngle(ut1);
  equationOfOrigins = era - gast;      % about -338.13 arcseconds

KEYWORDS

GMST, GAST, sidereal time, equinox, equation of the origins, SOFA

See also EARTHROTATIONANGLE, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, CONVERTTIMESCALE.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------

Created: 2026-07-25 22:05 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.equinoxNutationMatrix

Classical nutation-precession-bias matrix, IAU 2006/2000A.

SYNTAX

npb = equinoxNutationMatrix(epochTt)

  Returns the equinox-based rotation from the GCRS to the true equatorial
  system of date. This is the classical counterpart of the CIO-based
  rotationGcrsToCirs, and it exists as a VALIDATION ASSET rather than as an
  alternative transformation path.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----  -----------
  epoch   1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries, scale TT.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  npb    3x3xN    double   -      GCRS to true equator and equinox of date,
                                  one page per epoch. For N = 1 the result
                                  is 3x3.

METHOD

SOFA iauPnm06a, unmodified: frame bias, IAU 2006 precession and IAU 2000A
  nutation composed into one matrix.

  WHY THIS IS PUBLIC AND WHY IT IS NOT A TRANSFORMATION PATH (risk R13b,
  decision D16). The toolbox transforms through the CIO and the Earth
  rotation angle, and there is deliberately no user-facing 'Formulation'
  option choosing between the two conventions: two paths would mean two error
  budgets, two sets of tests and a silent choice for anyone who did not read
  the documentation.

  The classical route is kept for one purpose. Combined with
  greenwichSiderealTime it builds the same rotation by a route that shares
  almost no arithmetic with the CIO route, so differencing the two is an
  independent check that the CIO chain is right and not merely
  self-consistent. TestRotation runs that check over an epoch sweep on every
  test run. Exposing the ingredient as a function is what makes the check
  reproducible by a user rather than a private assertion.

  The relation between the two, for a caller who wants to reproduce it:

      Rz(-(ERA - GAST)) * npb  ==  rotationGcrsToCirs(X, Y, s)

  with ERA - GAST the equation of the origins, the distance from the equinox
  to the CIO.

  NO OBSERVED POLE OFFSETS ARE APPLIED HERE. In the equinox parameterisation
  the offsets are dPsi and dEpsilon rather than dX and dY, and converting
  between the two is a separate approximation that readEop owns under
  decision D56. Mixing it in here would confuse a conversion residual with a
  model inconsistency, which is exactly what the cross-check exists to
  separate.

ACCURACY

Agreement with the CIO route, measured over twelve epochs spanning 1990 to
  2050 with no observed offsets on either side: 4.4e-16 rad worst matrix
  element, that is 9.1e-5 microarcseconds. The two conventions agree at the
  level of double-precision arithmetic, three orders inside the 1
  microarcsecond the design asked for.

  Orthogonality better than 1.1e-16 in max |npb'npb - I|.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:equinoxNutationMatrix:wrongScale
  referenceSystem:equinoxNutationMatrix:gatewayMissing

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.4.5 and 5.5.7.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauPnm06a.
  SOFA Tools for Earth Attitude, sofa_pn_c.pdf, sections 5.4 and 5.5.

EXAMPLE

tt = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([54195 43265.184], "TT");
  npb = referenceSystem.equinoxNutationMatrix(tt);
  assert(norm(npb' * npb - eye(3)) < 1e-15);

KEYWORDS

nutation, precession, frame bias, equinox, NPB, cross-check, SOFA

See also ROTATIONGCRSTOCIRS, GREENWICHSIDEREALTIME, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-25 23:00 (CEST, UTC+02:00)

referenceSystem.rotationGcrsToCirs

Celestial-to-intermediate rotation from the CIP and CIO.

SYNTAX

Q = rotationGcrsToCirs(X, Y, s)

  Returns the rotation from the Geocentric Celestial Reference System to the
  Celestial Intermediate Reference System, built from the CIP coordinates and
  the CIO locator. This is the precession-nutation-bias factor of the full
  terrestrial-to-celestial rotation, in the CIO based form.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size        Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----        ----     -----  -----------
  X      Nx1         double   rad    CIP x-coordinate in the GCRS.
  Y      1x1 | Nx1   double   rad    CIP y-coordinate in the GCRS.
  s      1x1 | Nx1   double   rad    CIO locator.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  Q      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation GCRS to CIRS, one page per epoch.
                                  For N = 1 MATLAB drops the trailing
                                  singleton and the result is 3x3.

METHOD

SOFA iauC2ixys, unmodified, evaluated in C. The matrix is

      Q = R3(-(E + s)) * R2(d) * R3(E)

  with E and d the spherical angles of the CIP unit vector, X = sin d cos E
  and Y = sin d sin E. The construction is exact for any X and Y on the unit
  sphere; no small-angle approximation is made, which matters because the
  same function must serve epochs centuries from J2000.0 where the CIP has
  moved by degrees.

  The function takes X, Y and s rather than an epoch, deliberately. It is the
  geometry and nothing else, so it can be driven by the modelled CIP from
  cipCoordinates, by an observed one, or by a perturbed one in a partial
  derivative study, without any of those having to pretend to be a time. The
  epoch-driven convenience is rotationItrsToGcrs, which owns the whole chain.

  NO DIRECTION OPTION (decision D23). Q is orthogonal, so the CIRS to GCRS
  rotation is its transpose and needs no separate function, no flag and no
  recomputation: use invertRotation, or applyRotation with 'Inverse', true,
  which passes the transpose straight to pagemtimes at zero cost.

ACCURACY

Orthogonality of the returned matrix is better than 1.1e-16 in
  max |Q'Q - I|, measured over the epoch sweep of TestRotation, which is the
  accumulation of the three exact plane rotations SOFA composes.

  Against the SOFA cookbook worked example of 2007-04-05 (sofa_pn_c.pdf
  section 5.5) the matrix elements reproduce to better than 3e-15.

  Consistency with the classical equinox route, which is the independent
  check that this is the right matrix and not merely a well-conditioned one:
  Rz(-(ERA - GAST)) times the equinox-based nutation-precession-bias matrix
  agrees element by element to 4.4e-16, that is 9.1e-5 microarcseconds, over
  twelve epochs spanning 1990 to 2050.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationGcrsToCirs:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:rotationGcrsToCirs:gatewayMissing

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.4.4, Eq. 5.10.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauC2ixys.

EXAMPLE

tt = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([54195 43265.184], "TT");
  [X, Y, s] = referenceSystem.cipCoordinates(tt);
  Q = referenceSystem.rotationGcrsToCirs(X, Y, s);
  assert(norm(Q' * Q - eye(3)) < 1e-15);

KEYWORDS

GCRS, CIRS, CIP, CIO, precession, nutation, rotation matrix, SOFA

See also CIPCOORDINATES, ROTATIONTIRSTOITRS, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, APPLYROTATION.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-25 22:10 (CEST, UTC+02:00)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------

back to contents

referenceSystem.rotationTirsToItrs

Polar motion rotation from the pole coordinates.

SYNTAX

W = rotationTirsToItrs(xp, yp, sp)

  Returns the polar motion matrix, the rotation from the Terrestrial
  Intermediate Reference System to the International Terrestrial Reference
  System. It carries the motion of the CIP within the crust-fixed frame and
  the position of the TIO on the equator.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size        Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----        ----     -----  -----------
  xp     Nx1         double   rad    Pole coordinate x, IERS convention.
  yp     1x1 | Nx1   double   rad    Pole coordinate y, IERS convention.
  sp     1x1 | Nx1   double   rad    TIO locator s'.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  W      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation TIRS to ITRS, one page per epoch.
                                  For N = 1 the result is 3x3.

METHOD

SOFA iauPom00, unmodified, evaluated in C:

      W = R1(-yp) * R2(-xp) * R3(s')

  THIS IS THE TRANSPOSE OF THE MATRIX IERS TN36 EQUATION 5.3 CALLS W, and
  the difference is not cosmetic. Equation 5.3 writes

      W(IERS) = R3(-s') * R2(xp) * R1(yp)

  and equation 5.1 uses it as [GCRS] = Q R W(IERS) [ITRS], so the matrix
  equation 5.3 defines is the ITRS to TIRS rotation. This function is named
  for the direction it performs and returns the other one. The two differ by
  4.7e-06 at the pole rates of 2007, which is 30 metres at the surface: large
  enough to be obvious in a residual and small enough to survive every
  orthogonality and round-trip check, since a transpose is still a rotation.
  Revisions 1.0 to 1.72 of the handover printed equation 5.3 here unchanged
  and the code has always returned iauPom00; the code was right.

  RADIANS, NOT ARCSECONDS. The IERS publishes xp and yp in arcseconds and
  every EOP file carries them that way; readEop converts on the way in and
  every quantity inside this toolbox is SI. A caller who passes arcseconds
  here gets a matrix that is still orthogonal, still smooth and wrong by five
  orders of magnitude, so the unit is stated in the argument table, in the
  example and here.

  s' IS NOT OPTIONAL AND IS NOT ZERO, though it is very nearly so. The TIO
  locator is a secular quantity of about -47 microarcseconds per century
  times the Julian centuries since J2000.0, reaching -12 microarcseconds at
  2025, which is 0.37 mm at the equator. It comes from SOFA iauSp00 and is
  supplied by rotationItrsToGcrs automatically; a caller assembling the
  matrix directly must supply it or state deliberately that zero is good
  enough for the purpose.

  NO DIRECTION OPTION (decision D23). W is orthogonal, so the ITRS to TIRS
  rotation is its transpose: use invertRotation, or applyRotation with
  'Inverse', true.

ACCURACY

Orthogonality better than 1.1e-16 in max |W'W - I|.

  The rotation is exact given the pole coordinates, so its accuracy is that
  of the supplied EOP: about 30 microarcseconds formal error for an IERS C04
  series, which is 0.9 mm at the equator, and dominates every algorithmic
  term in this function by many orders.

  Time derivative, for callers assembling their own: measured over the
  supplied 20 C04 fixture the pole moves at up to 1.23 arcseconds per year,
  that is 1.9e-13 rad/s, a ratio of 2.6e-9 to the Earth rotation rate and
  worth 1.3 micrometres per second of velocity at a 7000 km orbit radius.
  The handover design table quotes 7.1e-9 and 3.6 micrometres per second;
  that is a conservative bound rather than a measurement, and the measured
  figure is smaller. rotationItrsToGcrs includes the term either way.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationTirsToItrs:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:rotationTirsToItrs:gatewayMissing

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.4.1, Eq. 5.3.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauPom00, iauSp00.

EXAMPLE

c = referenceSystem.iersConstants();
  xp = 0.0349282 * c.arcsecondsToRadians;
  yp = 0.4833163 * c.arcsecondsToRadians;
  W = referenceSystem.rotationTirsToItrs(xp, yp, 0);
  assert(norm(W' * W - eye(3)) < 1e-15);

KEYWORDS

polar motion, TIRS, ITRS, TIO locator, xp, yp, rotation matrix, SOFA

See also ROTATIONGCRSTOCIRS, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, INTERPOLATEEOP, APPLYROTATION.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-25 22:15 (CEST, UTC+02:00)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------

back to contents

referenceSystem.rotationItrsToGcrs

Terrestrial-to-celestial rotation and its derivatives.

SYNTAX

R = rotationItrsToGcrs(epoch, 'Eop', eop)
  [R, Rdot] = rotationItrsToGcrs(epoch, 'Eop', eop)
  [R, Rdot, Rddot, info] = rotationItrsToGcrs(epoch, 'EopFile', file)

  Returns the rotation from the International Terrestrial Reference System to
  the Geocentric Celestial Reference System, and on request its first two
  time derivatives, which are what a rotating frame demands of velocity and
  acceleration. The composition is

      R = (W * R3(ERA) * Q)'

  with Q the precession-nutation-bias rotation GCRS to CIRS, R3(ERA) the
  Earth rotation, and W the polar motion rotation TIRS to ITRS.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----  -----------
  epoch   1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct, N entries. Any scale; it is
                                 converted to UTC for the EOP lookup and to
                                 TT and UT1 for the rotation.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name                   Size  Type      Default        Description
  ----                   ----  ----      -------        -----------
  Eop                    1x1   struct    none           Series from readEop.
                                                        One of Eop or EopFile
                                                        is required.
  EopFile                1x1   string    none           File to read instead.
  EopFormat              1x1   string    auto           Format name for
                                                        EopFile.
  ApplyTidalCorrections  1x1   logical   true           Add the diurnal and
                                                        semidiurnal ocean
                                                        tide and libration
                                                        terms.
  ApplyLengthOfDay       1x1   logical   true           Correct the ERA rate
                                                        by the observed LOD.
  CipModel               1x1   string    from eop.meta  Frame of the pole
                                                        offsets. Overrides
                                                        the series metadata.
  Layout                 1x1   string    "pages"        "pages" for 3x3xN,
                                                        "rows" for the legacy
                                                        Nx9 form.
  Derivatives            1x1   double    -1             0, 1 or 2. Overrides
                                                        the output-count
                                                        inference; the default
                                                        -1 means "infer from
                                                        nargout". See METHOD.
  DifferenceStep         1x1   double    200            Central-difference
                                                        step in seconds. The
                                                        default is measured;
                                                        see ACCURACY.
  InterpolationMethod    1x1   string    "linear"       Passed to
                                                        interpolateEop.
  Interpolant            1x1   struct    none           Panels from
                                                        earthOrientationInterpolant.
                                                        Its tidal setting must
                                                        match this call's.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size            Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----            ----     -----    -----------
  R       3x3xN | Nx9     double   -        Rotation ITRS to GCRS.
  Rdot    3x3xN | Nx9     double   1/s      First time derivative. EMPTY if
                                            'Derivatives' suppressed it.
  Rddot   3x3xN | Nx9     double   1/s^2    Second time derivative. EMPTY if
                                            'Derivatives' suppressed it.
  info    1x1             struct   -        Diagnostics, fields below.
  .path              1x1  string   -        "direct" or "interpolated".
  .sampleSource      1x1  string   -        "series" (direct), "panels"
                                            (format-2 interpolant) or
                                            "panels+series" (format-1
                                            interpolant, LOD from series).
  .derivativeMethod  1x1  string   -        "none" or "centralDifference".
  .differenceStep    1x1  double   s        Step actually used, NaN if none.
  .interpolantResidual
                     1x1  double   uas      Fit residual of the interpolant,
                                            NaN on the direct path and for
                                            an interpolant read from a file,
                                            which carries no residual.
  .cipModel          1x1  string   -        Frame the offsets were read in.
  .tidalCorrections  1x1  logical  -        Whether they were applied.
  .lengthOfDayApplied
                     1x1  logical  -        Whether the ERA rate was corrected.
  .leapSecondSource  1x1  string   -        From leapSecondTable("state").
  .epochCount        1x1  double   -        N.
  .dubiousEpochCount 1x1  double   -        Epochs beyond the leap-second
                                            table's coverage.

METHOD

ONE ROTATION, NO DIRECTION FLAG (decision D23). This function returns the
  ITRS to GCRS rotation, full stop. The inverse is the transpose, exactly,
  because R is a product of orthogonal factors: use invertRotation for the
  matrices or applyRotation(..., 'Inverse', true) to apply it without forming
  them. There is no 'Direction' option and no partner function to keep in
  step.

  THE SIGNATURE IS [R, Rdot, Rddot, info] (decision D25). Earlier revisions
  of the design put the fundamental arguments in output slot 2, which made
  [R, Rdot] silently bind a struct to a variable named Rdot and fail three
  functions later inside pagemtimes. The fundamental arguments come from
  referenceSystem.fundamentalArguments, which is public and needed in its own
  right; taking the same epoch struct is what guarantees they are consistent
  with the rotation.

  OUTPUTS ARE GATED BY NARGOUT and the gating reaches the arithmetic, not
  just the return statement. A TILDE IS NOT A DISCARD as far as MATLAB is
  concerned: [R, ~, ~, info] reports nargout = 4, so the derivatives are
  computed and info honestly reports centralDifference. Requesting the
  diagnostics would therefore always cost two derivatives, which is about
  three times the dominant term for a struct of metadata. 'Derivatives'
  exists for exactly that call: [R, ~, ~, info] = f(..., 'Derivatives', 0)
  returns the rotation and the diagnostics for the price of one CIP
  evaluation. With one output the CIP series is evaluated once
  per epoch; with two or three it is evaluated three times, at t-h, t and
  t+h, and those same three evaluations yield both derivatives. Requesting
  Rdot therefore roughly triples the cost of the dominant term and requesting
  Rddot adds nothing further.

  ALL THREE DERIVATIVE TERMS ARE INCLUDED (decision D20):

      Rdot' = Wdot R3 Q + W R3dot Q + W R3 Qdot

  Measured magnitudes, as a ratio to the Earth rotation rate and as the
  velocity error at a 7000 km orbit radius if the term were dropped:

      precession-nutation Qdot            9.3e-8    47 um/s
      length-of-day correction to omega   2.5e-8    12.5 um/s
      polar motion Wdot                   2.6e-9    1.3 um/s

  Precession-nutation is the LARGEST of the three, not the smallest. All
  three are computed and the length-of-day correction defaults on.

  R3(ERA) IS DIFFERENTIATED ANALYTICALLY, not numerically: ERA is linear in
  UT1 by definition, so dR3/dt = omega dR3/dtheta in closed form, and that
  closed form is what the precision test compares the assembled Rdot against.
  Q and W are differentiated by central differences on the matrix elements
  at t-h, t and t+h. Analytic differentiation of the nutation series would be
  faster and was rejected: it requires a second copy of SOFA's coefficient
  tables, which is the two-sources-of-truth pattern this toolbox refuses
  everywhere else.

  Rddot keeps every second-order term of the product except one:

      Rddot' = Wddot R3 Q + W R3ddot Q + W R3 Qddot
               + 2(Wdot R3dot Q + Wdot R3 Qdot + W R3dot Qdot)

  with R3ddot = omega^2 d2R3/dtheta2. THE NEGLECTED TERM IS omegadot dR3/dt,
  the change in the Earth rotation rate itself. From the observed day-to-day
  change in length of day it is of order 1e-17 rad/s^2 against omega^2 of
  5.3e-9, nine orders down, and worth 7e-11 m/s^2 at 7000 km. Everything else
  is kept, including Qddot, which measures 2.0e-17 s^-2 and reaches
  1.4e-10 m/s^2 - at the GRACE accelerometer noise floor and therefore not
  negligible.

  THE EOP PIPELINE. The series is interpolated once for the whole arc, at
  three sample times per epoch when a derivative is wanted, so that Wdot is
  the derivative of the polar motion actually used and not of a frozen value.
  With ApplyTidalCorrections true the diurnal and semidiurnal terms from
  subdailyEopCorrection are added at the same three times. The zonal tide
  correction is NOT applied and cannot be applied here: it regularises UT1
  rather than supplying missing signal, and interpolateEop refuses it without
  an explicit 'Regularize' (decision D30).

  ONE WARNING PER CALL, NAMING THIS FUNCTION (decision D62). Epochs beyond
  the coverage of the leap-second table are dubious, and leapSecondTable
  raises its own warning when a UTC conversion crosses them. That warning
  names leapSecondTable, which is not the function the user called, and it
  fires once per internal conversion rather than once per call. The condition
  is therefore determined here, once, from leapSecondTable("validThrough"),
  the inner warning is suppressed for the duration of the internal
  conversions and restored afterwards, and one aggregated warning is raised
  naming this function and the epoch range. The count is also in
  info.dubiousEpochCount, so a caller who suppresses the warning can still
  see it. Note that the bundled SOFA table flags every epoch from 2029 onward
  as dubious.

  TWO MODES, DIRECT AND INTERPOLATED (decisions D17, D24; delivered by P19).
  On the direct path the precession-nutation series is evaluated at every
  epoch and the EOP come from the series. With 'Interpolant' the CIP and the
  pole come from Chebyshev panels instead, and info.path reads
  "interpolated". A panel is C-infinity inside itself, which removes the
  midnight slope discontinuity that piecewise linear EOP interpolation puts
  into a variable-step integrator's right-hand side. A format-2 interpolant
  also carries the excess length of day, so no series is read at all (P19c);
  info.sampleSource records which of the three cases ran.

  SINGLE-EPOCH PATH. With N = 1 the outputs are 3x3, because MATLAB drops a
  trailing singleton dimension, and no interpolation object, temporary array
  or vectorised setup is built. This is a branch inside one function and not
  a separate scalar entry point; section B.9 explains why separate
  scalar and vector functions were rejected.

ACCURACY

THE DIFFERENCE STEP IS MEASURED, NOT ASSUMED (decision D28). Earlier
  revisions of the design asserted h = 60 s giving 1e-7 relative on Qdot,
  a figure that had never been established. The h-refinement study run for
  this session, over twelve epochs spanning 1990 to 2050 against a Romberg
  reference that was itself validated against a second reference of different
  base step, gives:

      h [s]   dX/dt error    d2X/dt2 error   worst velocity   worst accel
      3000    1.9e-16        4.4e-22         1.3e-09 m/s      5.2e-15 m/s^2
       600    7.5e-18        1.8e-23         5.3e-11 m/s      5.5e-15 m/s^2
       200    8.4e-19        2.3e-22         6.0e-12 m/s      5.2e-14 m/s^2
        60    8.2e-20        1.2e-21         1.0e-11 m/s      5.1e-13 m/s^2
        10    7.5e-20        6.5e-20         7.4e-11 m/s      1.5e-11 m/s^2
         1    9.8e-19        4.5e-18         5.3e-10 m/s      1.6e-09 m/s^2

  The O(h^2) region runs from 3000 s down to about 200 s and the roundoff
  plateau begins below about 150 s, set by the intrinsic evaluation noise of
  the CIP series, which is 4.5e-18 rad rms in X and 5.2e-17 in Y.

  THE DEFAULT IS h = 200 s, the minimum of the combined error normalised
  against a budget of 1 nm/s in velocity and 1e-10 m/s^2 in acceleration,
  the latter being the GRACE accelerometer noise floor. At that step the
  normalised error is 6.0e-3, a margin of 167.

  The wider result is worth more than the number: EVERY STEP FROM 1 s TO
  1800 s MEETS BOTH BUDGETS, and only 3000 s fails, by a factor 1.3. The
  choice of h is therefore not critical, which is the opposite of what the
  unmeasured 60 s figure implied.

  Reproduction of the SOFA cookbook worked example of 2007-04-05
  (sofa_pn_c.pdf section 5.5), worst matrix element: 2.6e-15, that is
  5e-4 microarcseconds of rotation.

  Invariants, measured and independent of h:
      max |R'R - I|                  1.1e-16
      max |Rdot' R + R' Rdot|        4.2e-19 rad/s
  The second is the one that catches a sign or transposition error in Rdot,
  which no magnitude comparison would.

  Equinox-versus-CIO agreement (risk R13b): 4.4e-16 rad worst element, that
  is 9.1e-5 microarcseconds, over twelve epochs from 1990 to 2050.

  ABSOLUTE ACCURACY IS SET BY THE EOP AND NOT BY ANY OF THE ABOVE. A C04
  series carries formal errors of about 30 microarcseconds in polar motion
  and 10 us in Delta UT1, which is 0.9 to 1.5 mm at the Earth's surface. Every
  algorithmic figure quoted here is five or more orders below that. The
  tolerances in TestRotation bound algorithmic quantities and are never
  justified by EOP magnitudes (design principle 15).

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:eopRequired
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:conflictingEopSource
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:unknownCipModel
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:badInterpolant
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:tidalMismatch
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:outsideInterpolant
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:badDifferenceStep
  referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:gatewayMissing

WARNINGS

referenceSystem:rotationItrsToGcrs:dubiousYear

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, iauXys06a, iauC2ixys, iauEra00, iauSp00,
    iauPom00.
  SOFA Tools for Earth Attitude, sofa_pn_c.pdf, section 5.5.
  Montenbruck, O. and Gill, E., 2000, Satellite Orbits, Sec. 5.3.

EXAMPLE

eop = referenceSystem.readEop('eop20c04.txt');
  utc = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([57754 0], "UTC");
  [R, Rdot, ~, info] = referenceSystem.rotationItrsToGcrs(utc, 'Eop', eop);
  [rGcrs, vGcrs] = referenceSystem.applyRotation(R, Rdot, rItrs, vItrs);
  assert(info.path == "direct" && info.derivativeMethod == "centralDifference");

KEYWORDS

ITRS, GCRS, ECEF, ECI, Earth rotation, precession, nutation, polar motion,
  derivative, orbit integration, SOFA

See also CIPCOORDINATES, APPLYROTATION, INVERTROTATION, EARTHROTATIONANGLE,
  INTERPOLATEEOP, READEOP.

referenceSystem.applyRotation

Apply a rotation and its derivatives to a state.

SYNTAX

r2 = applyRotation(R, r)
  [r2, v2] = applyRotation(R, Rdot, r, v)
  [r2, v2, a2] = applyRotation(R, Rdot, Rddot, r, v, a)
  ... = applyRotation(..., 'Inverse', true)

  Rotates a position, a position and velocity, or a position, velocity and
  acceleration from one frame to another, carrying the frame-rate terms that
  a rotating frame demands. The call shape is chosen by the number of
  arguments: d rotation matrices followed by d vectors, for d = 1, 2 or 3.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size     Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----     ----     -----    -----------
  R       3x3xN    double   -        Rotation, one page per epoch, or 3x3 for
                                     a single epoch or one rotation applied
                                     to every column.
  Rdot    3x3xN    double   1/s      First time derivative, depth 2 and 3.
  Rddot   3x3xN    double   1/s^2    Second time derivative, depth 3.
  r       3xN      double   any      Position, one column per epoch.
  v       3xN      double   any/s    Velocity, depth 2 and 3.
  a       3xN      double   any/s^2  Acceleration, depth 3.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name      Size   Type      Default   Description
  ----      ----   ----      -------   -----------
  Inverse   1x1    logical   false     Apply the transposed rotation, that is
                                       the reverse transformation.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----    -----------
  r2     3xN    double   any      Rotated position.
  v2     3xN    double   any/s    Rotated velocity, depth 2 and 3.
  a2     3xN    double   any/s^2  Rotated acceleration, depth 3.

METHOD

The transformation of a state between frames in relative rotation is

      r2 = R r
      v2 = R v + Rdot r
      a2 = R a + 2 Rdot v + Rddot r

  THE FACTOR 2 ON THE MIDDLE TERM OF THE ACCELERATION is the classic error in
  this formula and it has its own test. It is the Coriolis-like term, and it
  carries a 2 because differentiating R v twice produces Rdot v from the
  product rule in two places. Dropping it, or writing 1 instead of 2, gives
  an acceleration that is wrong by roughly the Coriolis acceleration itself -
  about 0.1 m/s^2 at low Earth orbit, which is nine orders above the GRACE
  accelerometer noise floor and would nevertheless leave every orthogonality
  check in this toolbox perfectly happy.

  ONE FUNCTION, NOT TWO (decision D23). The inverse relations are the same
  formulas with every matrix transposed,

      r1 = R' r2,   v1 = R' v2 + Rdot' r2,   a1 = R' a2 + 2 Rdot' v2 + Rddot' r2

  so 'Inverse' does not select a second code path. It selects the 'transpose'
  flag of pagemtimes, which the BLAS honours without forming a transposed
  copy: zero extra flops and zero extra allocation. There is consequently no
  separate inverse implementation to test, only transposed inputs, and a
  round-trip test is a real check rather than a tautology.

  CALL SHAPE AND 'Inverse'. The number of arguments chooses the shape, and
  the shape is read from which optional slots were filled rather than from
  nargin, which counts a name-value pair as two arguments and therefore
  cannot distinguish applyRotation(R, r, 'Inverse', true) from
  applyRotation(R, Rdot, r, v). The first release of this function used
  nargin and silently returned the forward rotation for both round-trip
  calls; the failure is recorded in the handover revision log because the
  symptom - a plausible wrong state rather than an error - is the class of
  defect this toolbox exists to refuse.

  VECTORISATION. The multiplication is a single pagemtimes over all N epochs.
  A 3x3 R with a 3xN r applies one rotation to every column, which is the
  common case of a single epoch with many points; a 3x3xN R with a 3xN r
  applies page k to column k. Mixed shapes that mean neither of those are an
  error rather than a broadcast, because a silent broadcast of the wrong axis
  produces a full-size result that is wrong everywhere.

ACCURACY

The arithmetic is three inner products of length three per vector, so the
  relative error is a few units in the last place of the inputs, below
  1e-15 relative. Nothing here is approximated.

  A round trip through the forward and inverse transformation returns the
  original state to better than 1e-15 relative in position and velocity,
  measured over the epoch sweep in TestRotation.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:applyRotation:wrongArgumentCount
  referenceSystem:applyRotation:wrongMatrixSize
  referenceSystem:applyRotation:wrongVectorSize
  referenceSystem:applyRotation:sizeMismatch

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.1.
  Montenbruck, O. and Gill, E., 2000, Satellite Orbits, Sec. 5.3.

EXAMPLE

[R, Rdot] = referenceSystem.rotationItrsToGcrs(epoch, 'Eop', eop);
  [rGcrs, vGcrs] = referenceSystem.applyRotation(R, Rdot, rItrs, vItrs);
  [back, backRate] = referenceSystem.applyRotation(R, Rdot, rGcrs, vGcrs, ...
      'Inverse', true);

KEYWORDS

rotation, pagemtimes, velocity, Coriolis, frame transformation, state

See also ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, INVERTROTATION, ROTATIONGCRSTOCIRS.

referenceSystem.invertRotation

Inverse of a rotation and of its time derivatives.

SYNTAX

Rinv = invertRotation(R)
  [Rinv, RdotInv] = invertRotation(R, Rdot)
  [Rinv, RdotInv, RddotInv] = invertRotation(R, Rdot, Rddot)
  ... = invertRotation(..., 'Verify', true)

  Returns the transposed set. For an orthogonal rotation the transpose is the
  inverse exactly, and the derivative of the inverse is the transpose of the
  derivative, so no arithmetic beyond the transposition is needed or done.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size     Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----     ----     -----    -----------
  R       3x3xN    double   -        Rotation, one page per epoch.
  Rdot    3x3xN    double   1/s      First time derivative, optional.
  Rddot   3x3xN    double   1/s^2    Second time derivative, optional.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name     Size   Type      Default   Description
  ----     ----   ----      -------   -----------
  Verify   1x1    logical   false     Check max |R'R - I| and error if it
                                      exceeds 1e-12. Off by default; see
                                      METHOD.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size     Type     Units    Description
  ----       ----     ----     -----    -----------
  Rinv       3x3xN    double   -        Transposed rotation.
  RdotInv    3x3xN    double   1/s      Transposed first derivative.
  RddotInv   3x3xN    double   1/s^2    Transposed second derivative.

METHOD

R is a product of plane rotations, so R' R = I to about 1e-16 and R' is the
  inverse with nothing to solve. Differentiating R' R = I gives
  Rdot' R + R' Rdot = 0, from which d(R')/dt = (dR/dt)' - that is, the
  transposed derivative is the derivative of the transpose, and the same
  holds at second order. The whole function is therefore three pagetranspose
  calls, and the reason it exists at all is that writing R.' by hand for a
  3x3xN array transposes the wrong two dimensions.

  ORTHOGONALITY IS A PROPERTY OF HOW R WAS BUILT, NOT OF THIS FUNCTION. The
  rotations this toolbox returns are guaranteed orthogonal because section
  B.9.1 rule 1 forbids interpolating a rotation matrix: interpolation is done
  on the angles and the matrix is rebuilt, precisely so that this guarantee
  survives. A matrix a caller built some other way - averaged, interpolated
  element by element, read from a file, accumulated over an integration -
  carries no such guarantee, and for such a matrix the transpose is only an
  approximate inverse.

  'Verify' exists for that case and defaults OFF. It is useful in a test and
  wasteful in a hot loop, where it would add a full page product per call to
  check a property that holds by construction. When it is on, the tolerance
  is 1e-12 rather than 1e-15: the point is to catch a matrix that is not a
  rotation at all, not to re-measure the last bits of one that is.

  PREFER APPLYROTATION WHERE YOU CAN. If the inverse is wanted only in order
  to apply it, applyRotation(..., 'Inverse', true) passes the transposition
  to the BLAS as a flag and never forms the transposed array. This function
  is for callers who want the matrices themselves.

ACCURACY

Exact. A transposition moves values and does not compute, so the outputs
  are bit-identical to the inputs, rearranged.

  The claim that the transpose is the inverse is measured rather than
  asserted: over the epoch sweep in TestRotation, max |R*R' - I| measures
  4.4e-16, which is two units in the last place. That is the accumulation of
  a three-term dot product of numbers of order unity and is what an exact
  transpose of an orthogonal matrix is expected to give; the bound asserted
  in the test is 1e-15, a few ulp, and not a measured constant.

  An earlier revision of this block quoted 1.2e-16, imported from a C-level
  measurement of R'R at a single epoch. That is a different product in a
  different order over a different number of pages, and the test built on it
  failed on its first run. A tolerance is a claim about which error
  dominates (handover obligation O8): here the dominant error is
  floating-point summation, whose scale is ulp, not a number carried over
  from another experiment.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:invertRotation:wrongMatrixSize
  referenceSystem:invertRotation:notOrthogonal

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.1.

EXAMPLE

[R, Rdot] = referenceSystem.rotationItrsToGcrs(epoch, 'Eop', eop);
  [Rgcrs2itrs, RdotGcrs2itrs] = referenceSystem.invertRotation(R, Rdot);

KEYWORDS

rotation, inverse, transpose, orthogonality, pagetranspose

See also APPLYROTATION, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, ROTATIONGCRSTOCIRS.

L3 - Terrestrial frame realizations

referenceSystem.itrfParameters

Published Helmert parameters from ITRF2020 to a past ITRF.

SYNTAX

itrfInfo = itrfParameters(name) returns the 14 transformation parameters
  FROM ITRF2020 TO the named realization, in SI units.

  itrfTable = itrfParameters("list") returns a table with one row per
  supported realization and the same quantities as variables.

  Supported names, case sensitive: "ITRF2014", "ITRF2008", "ITRF2005",
  "ITRF2000", "ITRF97", "ITRF96", "ITRF94", "ITRF93", "ITRF92", "ITRF91",
  "ITRF90", "ITRF89", "ITRF88".

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  name   1x1    string   -      Realization name, or "list" for the table form.
                                A char row vector is accepted and converted.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name               Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----               ----   ----     -----    -----------
  itrfInfo           1x1    struct   -        Parameters of the named frame.
  .name              1x1    string   -        Realization name, echoed.
  .epoch             1x1    double   year     Reference epoch of the
                                              parameters, decimal year.
  .translation       1x3    double   m        Tx, Ty, Tz at .epoch.
  .translationRate   1x3    double   m/year   Their rates.
  .scale             1x1    double   1        Scale factor D at .epoch,
                                              dimensionless, NOT ppb.
  .scaleRate         1x1    double   1/year   Its rate.
  .rotation          1x3    double   rad      Rx, Ry, Rz at .epoch.
  .rotationRate      1x3    double   rad/year Their rates.
  .convention        1x1    string   -        "positionVector", the convention
                                              of the published model.
  .sourceUrl         1x1    string   -        Where the table was fetched from.
  .retrieved         1x1    string   -        Date it was fetched and checked.

  For name = "list" the output is a 13x12 table with the same variables, the
  vector quantities split into their three components.

METHOD

The table is the IGN publication Transfo-ITRF2020_TRFs.txt, transcribed
  verbatim and converted to SI once, here, so that nothing downstream ever
  sees millimetres, parts per billion or milliarcseconds. The published model
  is

      XS = X + T + M*X,   M = [  D  -Rz   Ry ;
                                 Rz   D  -Rx ;
                                -Ry  Rx   D  ]

  with X in ITRF2020 and XS in the named frame, every parameter linear in
  time: P(t) = P(epoch) + Pdot*(t - epoch). The epoch is 2015.0 for every
  current entry, which is why it is carried per entry rather than assumed.

  THE MODEL IS THE POSITION VECTOR CONVENTION. The sign of M's off-diagonal
  terms says so: a positive Rz turns the position vector, not the axes. The
  EPSG registry classifies these same parameters as "Time-dependent Position
  Vector transformation" for the same reason. Applying them under the
  Coordinate Frame convention reverses all three rotation signs, which for
  ITRF93 - the only entry with nonzero rotations - moves a surface point by
  about 27 cm. applyHelmert7 therefore requires the convention to be named and
  has no default.

  The values were re-fetched from the source URL and compared against the v1
  transcription line by line on 2026-07-26: all thirteen rows and all
  fourteen parameters agree exactly, and the unit conversion is correct. The
  published rotations are in units of 0.001 arcsecond, that is milliarcseconds,
  which is the one conversion in the table that is easy to lose by a factor of
  a thousand.

ACCURACY

The parameters are published to 0.1 mm, 0.01 ppb and 0.01 mas, and are
  stored here at exactly that precision; the SI conversion adds at most one
  double-precision rounding, below 1e-19 m. The uncertainty that matters is
  the physical one: the IGN notes state these parameters are derived from
  earlier published values and supersede them, and the ITRF2020 frame origin
  itself is stable at the 5 mm level with 0.5 mm/year of drift. A conversion
  between two realizations is therefore good to a few millimetres at best,
  whatever the arithmetic does.

  Note that the parameters are exact by definition of the transformation - the
  published numbers ARE the transformation - so a test may assert them to the
  last printed digit, and the reference test does.

ERRORS

MATLAB:validators:mustBeMember
      Raised by the arguments block for a name that is not supported.
  referenceSystem:itrfParameters:listNotSupportedInCodegen

REFERENCES

IGN, Transformation parameters from ITRF2020 to past ITRFs,
  https://itrf.ign.fr/docs/solutions/itrf2020/Transfo-ITRF2020_TRFs.txt
  Z. Altamimi, P. Rebischung, X. Collilieux, L. Metivier, K. Chanard (2023),
  ITRF2020: an augmented reference frame, Journal of Geodesy 97(47).
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 4.

EXAMPLE

p = referenceSystem.itrfParameters("ITRF2014");
  allFrames = referenceSystem.itrfParameters("list");

KEYWORDS

ITRF, ITRS, Helmert, transformation parameters, realization, IGN, IERS,
  position vector, datum

See also CONVERTITRF, APPLYHELMERT7, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS.

referenceSystem.convertItrf

Convert positions and velocities between ITRF realizations.

SYNTAX

cartesian = convertItrf(cartesian, fromFrame, toFrame, epoch) converts the
  Nx3 earth-fixed positions from one realization of the ITRS to another,
  evaluated at the given decimal year.

  [cartesian, velocity] = convertItrf(..., 'Velocity', v) also converts the
  Nx3 velocities in metres per year.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size          Type     Units   Description
  ----        ----          ----     -----   -----------
  cartesian   Nx3           double   m       Positions, one point per ROW.
  fromFrame   1x1           string   -       Source realization or IGS alias.
  toFrame     1x1           string   -       Target realization or IGS alias.
  epoch       1x1 or Nx1    double   year    DECIMAL YEAR at which the
                                             time-dependent parameters are
                                             evaluated, for example 2020.5.
                                             Not an epoch struct: L3 is the one
                                             layer whose time is a year.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----       ----   ----     -------   -----------
  Velocity   Nx3    double   []        Station velocities in m/year, converted
                                       with the exact time derivative of the
                                       position model.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units     Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----     -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m         Positions in toFrame.
  velocity    Nx3    double   m/year    Velocities in toFrame, or [] when none
                                        was supplied.

METHOD

The published parameters go from ITRF2020 to each past realization, so
  ITRF2020 IS THE PIVOT: a conversion between two past frames runs
  fromFrame -> ITRF2020 -> toFrame, and either leg is skipped when one of the
  frames is ITRF2020 itself. Chaining through the pivot rather than composing
  parameter sets keeps every leg exactly the published transformation.

      forward   XS = X + T + M*X
      inverse   X  = (I + M)\(XS - T)

  THE INVERSE IS SOLVED, NOT APPROXIMATED. The common first-order form
  X = XS - T - M*XS is accurate to about 1e-9 relative for parameters this
  small, but the exact solve costs one 3x3 factorisation for the whole array
  and removes an approximation that would otherwise have to be documented and
  re-argued every time the parameters grow. Measured on the ITRF93 entry, the
  only one with arcsecond-level rotations, the exact round trip closes to
  1.0e-09 m.

  VECTORISATION (finding M-3). The v1 function looped over points with a 3x3
  backslash inside the loop, which is unusable for the million-point arrays
  this sees. Here the parameters depend only on the epoch, so for a scalar
  epoch M is formed once and applied as a single matrix product over the whole
  array; for per-point epochs the points are GROUPED BY UNIQUE EPOCH and each
  group gets one matrix. A station list at one epoch, which is the common case,
  costs two matrix products regardless of N.

  VELOCITY IS EXACT, including both cross terms:

      VS = V + Tdot + Mdot*X + M*V

  Mdot*X is the rotation and scale rates acting on the position, M*V the
  parameters acting on the velocity. Both are far below any realistic velocity
  uncertainty - the largest is 0.2 mm/year from Tdot - but they cost nothing
  and a truncated derivative would be an approximation with no benefit. In the
  inverse direction the position in ITRF2020 is needed first and is used,
  never the input position.

  IGS ALIASES ARE IDENTITIES, WITH A CAVEAT. IGS20, IGb14 and the rest are
  constructed to have zero net Helmert transformation against the
  corresponding ITRF, so they are resolved to it and no arithmetic is done.
  They are not independent frames with their own published parameters. If a
  separately published IGS-minus-ITRF difference must be accounted for, apply
  it explicitly before or after this call; this function cannot know about it.
  The alias table is a validated registry that errors on an unknown name,
  replacing v1's struct-abused-as-a-map, which silently passed anything
  unrecognised through as if it were a canonical ITRF name (finding m-6).

ACCURACY

The arithmetic is exact to machine precision: agreement with an independent
  PROJ 9.5.1 pipeline over ITRF2014, ITRF2008, ITRF2000 and ITRF93 at epochs
  from 2000 to 2020 measures 1.0e-09 m, which is the accumulation of a few
  roundings on coordinates of order 5e6 m.

  The physical accuracy is set by the published parameters and by the frames
  themselves, a few millimetres at best; see itrfParameters. Extrapolating far
  from the 2015.0 parameter epoch is linear by construction and carries no
  warning, because the published model IS linear - but the frames' agreement
  is not, and a conversion evaluated decades away should be read as a
  definition rather than a measurement.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:convertItrf:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:convertItrf:unknownFrame
  referenceSystem:convertItrf:epochCountMismatch
  referenceSystem:convertItrf:velocitySizeMismatch

REFERENCES

IGN, Transformation parameters from ITRF2020 to past ITRFs,
  https://itrf.ign.fr/docs/solutions/itrf2020/Transfo-ITRF2020_TRFs.txt
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 4.

EXAMPLE

x2020 = referenceSystem.convertItrf(x2014, "ITRF2014", "ITRF2020", 2020.0);
  [x, v] = referenceSystem.convertItrf(x, "IGS14", "ITRF2020", 2020.0, ...
      'Velocity', v);

KEYWORDS

ITRF, ITRS, realization, Helmert, datum, IGS, pivot, station coordinates,
  plate motion

See also ITRFPARAMETERS, APPLYHELMERT7, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC.

referenceSystem.applyHelmert7

Seven-parameter similarity transformation, exact in both directions.

SYNTAX

cartesian = applyHelmert7(cartesian, parameters, 'Convention', c, ...
  'Units', u) applies the seven-parameter (Bursa-Wolf) transformation to the
  Nx3 earth-fixed positions. The convention and the units are both required:
  neither can be guessed and both change the answer.

  cartesian = applyHelmert7(..., 'Inverse', true) applies the exact inverse.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name          Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----          ----   ----     -----      -----------
  cartesian     Nx3    double   m          Positions, one point per ROW.
  parameters    1x1    struct   see Units  Transformation parameters.
  .translation  1x3    double   see Units  Tx, Ty, Tz.
  .rotation     1x3    double   see Units  Rx, Ry, Rz.
  .scale        1x1    double   see Units  Scale correction.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type      Default    Description
  ----         ----   ----      -------    -----------
  Convention   1x1    string    REQUIRED   "positionVector" or
                                           "coordinateFrame".
  Units        1x1    string    REQUIRED   "si" (m, rad, 1), "itrf"
                                           (mm, mas, ppb) or "datum"
                                           (m, arcsec, ppm).
  Inverse      1x1    logical   false      Solve the transformation instead of
                                           applying it.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m      Transformed positions.

METHOD

XS = T + (1 + s)*R*X

  with R the linearised small-angle rotation matrix. The two conventions
  differ in the sign of every rotation:

      positionVector    R = [  1  -rz   ry ;  rz   1  -rx ; -ry  rx   1 ]
      coordinateFrame   R = [  1   rz  -ry ; -rz   1   rx ;  ry -rx   1 ]

  THE CONVENTION HAS NO DEFAULT, and that is the point of the argument. Both
  are in daily use - EPSG, IERS and the IGN ITRF tables publish position
  vector, while several national and legacy datum sets publish coordinate
  frame - and choosing the wrong one is a pure sign error on the rotations
  that leaves the result looking entirely plausible. For the ITRF93
  parameters, the only ITRF entry with arcsecond-level rotations, the two
  conventions differ by about 0.27 m at the surface. A default here would be a
  silent 50 percent chance of that error, so there is none.

  THE UNITS HAVE NO DEFAULT EITHER, for the same reason at a larger scale:
  the same seven numbers mean a millimetre-level or a metre-level shift
  depending on the publication, and mistaking mas for arcsec is a factor of
  1000 on the rotations.

  THE INVERSE IS SOLVED EXACTLY, X = R'*(XS - T)/(1 + s), and NOT by negating
  the seven parameters (finding M-10). v1 carried two Helmert implementations
  that disagreed on this: one negated, one solved. Negation is a linearisation
  of the inverse and its error grows with the rotations and the scale. Measured
  on a 6.4e6 m position:

      parameters                     exact solve   parameter negation
      ITRF-scale (0 as, 0.4 ppb)     0.0e+00 m     1.2e-10 m
      modern datum (0.03 as, 1 ppm)  4.7e-10 m     5.0e-06 m
      legacy datum (3 as, 5 ppm)     0.0e+00 m     9.9e-04 m
      extreme (100 as, 20 ppm)       9.3e-10 m     1.4e+00 m

  So negation is sub-millimetre for modern parameters and wrong at the
  millimetre level for exactly the legacy datums it is traditionally used for.
  The exact solve costs one 3x3 factorisation for the whole array.

  THE SMALL-ANGLE FORM IS GUARDED. R is linearised, so it is a rotation only
  to first order in the angles; at 100 arcseconds the departure from
  orthogonality is 1.2e-7 relative, which is 0.8 m at the Earth's surface.
  Above that the function errors rather than silently applying a matrix that
  is not a rotation. Use a rigorous rotation from L4b if genuinely large
  angles are meant.

  All N points share one matrix, so the whole array costs one matrix product;
  v1 reshaped three separate X, Y and Z arrays with no check that they had the
  same shape (finding m-4), which this signature makes impossible.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision given the parameters. Agreement with an
  independent PROJ 9.5.1 helmert pipeline, both conventions, on a 6.4e6 m
  position: 0.0e+00 m, bit for bit. The forward-inverse round trip closes to
  4.7e-10 m for arcsecond-level rotations.

  The linearisation itself is an approximation of the published rotation
  matrix and not of this code: published parameter sets are DERIVED under the
  linearised model, so applying them with a rigorous rotation matrix would be
  less accurate, not more. That is why the guard is at 100 arcseconds rather
  than a switch to a rigorous form.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:applyHelmert7:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:applyHelmert7:conventionRequired
  referenceSystem:applyHelmert7:unitsRequired
  referenceSystem:applyHelmert7:missingParameter
  referenceSystem:applyHelmert7:rotationTooLarge

REFERENCES

IOGP Publication 373-7-2, Geomatics Guidance Note 7 part 2, Coordinate
  Conversions and Transformations including Formulas, Sect. 4.3.3.
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 4.

EXAMPLE

p = struct('translation', [0.06155 -0.01087 -0.04019], ...
      'rotation', [-0.0394924 -0.0327221 -0.0328979], 'scale', -0.009994);
  x = referenceSystem.applyHelmert7(x, p, 'Convention', "positionVector", ...
      'Units', "datum");

KEYWORDS

Helmert, Bursa-Wolf, seven parameter, similarity, datum, position vector,
  coordinate frame, ITRF, transformation

See also CONVERTITRF, ITRFPARAMETERS, APPLYMOLODENSKY.

referenceSystem.applyMolodensky

Molodensky datum transformation, a legacy compatibility path.

SYNTAX

geodetic = applyMolodensky(geodetic, shift) converts the Nx3 geodetic
  coordinates from one datum to another directly, without the round trip
  through Cartesian coordinates. THIS IS A LEGACY PATH kept for compatibility
  with published parameter sets; the Cartesian route through
  geodeticToCartesian, applyHelmert7 and cartesianToGeodetic is exact and is
  what new work should use.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                    Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----                    ----   ----     -----      -----------
  geodetic                Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Source coordinates,
                                                     [lat lon h], one per ROW.
  shift                   1x1    struct   -          Datum shift.
  .translation            1x3    double   m          Geocentric translation
                                                     source to target, so that
                                                     Xtarget = Xsource + this.
  .deltaSemiMajorAxis     1x1    double   m          a(target) - a(source).
  .deltaFlattening        1x1    double   1          f(target) - f(source).

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type            Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----            -------   -----------
  Ellipsoid   1x1    string|struct   "GRS80"   SOURCE ellipsoid, the one the
                                               input coordinates are referred
                                               to.
  AngleUnit   1x1    string          "rad"     Unit of the input and output
                                               angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----      -----------
  geodetic   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Coordinates on the target datum.

METHOD

The standard Molodensky formulas, with M the meridian and N the prime
  vertical radius of curvature on the SOURCE ellipsoid:

    dLat = [ -dX*sin(lat)*cos(lon) - dY*sin(lat)*sin(lon) + dZ*cos(lat)
             + N*e2*sin(lat)*cos(lat)*da/a
             + sin(lat)*cos(lat)*(M/(1-f) + N*(1-f))*df ] / (M + h)
    dLon = [ -dX*sin(lon) + dY*cos(lon) ] / ((N + h)*cos(lat))
    dh   = dX*cos(lat)*cos(lon) + dY*cos(lat)*sin(lon) + dZ*sin(lat)
           - (a/N)*da + N*(1-f)*sin(lat)^2*df

  THIS IS THE STANDARD FORM, NOT THE ABRIDGED ONE, and the distinction is
  worth stating because the v1 function's own help text called it abridged and
  the handover inherited that name. It is not: the abridged form replaces the
  two ellipsoid terms with (a*df + f*da)*sin(2*lat) and drops the h from both
  denominators. The two were separated by transcribing the abridged formula
  independently and comparing against the PROJ 9.5.1 molodensky operator with
  +abridged, which it reproduces to 0.0 mm while this form differs from it by
  76 mm in latitude at a mid-latitude test point. The heading of the function
  now says what the code does.

  THE INVERSE IS BY NEGATION, which is the standard reverse-Molodensky
  convention: call again with the target coordinates, the negated
  translation, the negated da and df, and the TARGET ellipsoid as
  'Ellipsoid'. It carries the same linearisation error as the forward step,
  and there is no exact inverse of this model because the model itself is
  linearised - which is the argument for the Cartesian route, not for a
  cleverer inverse.

ACCURACY

A LINEARISED APPROXIMATION, and the number to remember is centimetres. The
  error is measured against the exact Cartesian route for the ED50 to WGS84
  shift (89.5, 93.8, 123.1 m) over a global grid at 5 degree spacing:

      standard form (this function)   16 mm north, 15 mm east,  8 mm up
      abridged form (not used here)  165 mm north, 16 mm east, 84 mm up

  The error grows with the size of the shift and with the ellipsoid
  difference, so it must be re-measured for any parameter set far from that
  one. Handover approximation #10 quotes decimetre level; that figure
  describes the abridged form, and this correction is recorded rather than
  quietly improved upon.

  For any work below a centimetre, use the Cartesian route. The reason this
  function exists at all is that many national parameter sets are published
  for Molodensky and are DEFINED by it, so reproducing an official published
  coordinate requires the same approximation the publication used.

  At the poles dLon divides by cos(lat) and is unbounded; the formula is not
  usable within a few arcseconds of the pole and returns Inf or a very large
  value there rather than erroring, because the latitude and height remain
  meaningful. Use the Cartesian route near the poles.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:applyMolodensky:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:applyMolodensky:missingParameter
  referenceSystem:applyMolodensky:latitudeOutOfRange

REFERENCES

M. S. Molodensky, V. F. Eremeev, M. I. Yurkina (1962), Methods for Study of
  the External Gravitational Field and Figure of the Earth, Israel Program for
  Scientific Translations.
  R. E. Deakin (2004), The Standard and Abridged Molodensky Coordinate
  Transformation Formulae, RMIT University.
  IOGP Publication 373-7-2, Geomatics Guidance Note 7 part 2, Sect. 4.4.

EXAMPLE

s = struct('translation', [89.5 93.8 123.1], ...
      'deltaSemiMajorAxis', -251.0, 'deltaFlattening', -1.41927022558e-05);
  wgs = referenceSystem.applyMolodensky(ed50, s, ...
      'Ellipsoid', "Hayford1924", 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

Molodensky, datum, geodetic shift, legacy, ED50, abridged, standard,
  approximation

See also APPLYHELMERT7, GEODETICTOCARTESIAN, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC.

L4 - Coordinate representations

referenceSystem.geodeticToCartesian

Ellipsoidal geodetic coordinates to Cartesian coordinates.

SYNTAX

cartesian = geodeticToCartesian(geodetic) converts the Nx3 geodetic
  coordinates [latitude longitude height] to the Nx3 Cartesian coordinates
  [X Y Z] on the same earth-fixed frame.

  cartesian = geodeticToCartesian(geodetic, 'Ellipsoid', "WGS84")
  cartesian = geodeticToCartesian(geodetic, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----      -----------
  geodetic   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  One point per ROW: column 1 geodetic
                                         latitude, column 2 longitude, column 3
                                         ellipsoidal height. Height is metres
                                         whatever 'AngleUnit' says.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type            Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----            -------   -----------
  Ellipsoid   1x1    string|struct   "GRS80"   Ellipsoid name accepted by
                                                ellipsoidParameters, or the
                                                struct it returns.
  AngleUnit   1x1    string          "rad"     "rad" or "deg" for columns 1-2.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m      One point per row: X, Y, Z.

METHOD

The exact closed form, with N the radius of curvature in the prime vertical:

      N = a/sqrt(1 - e2*sin(lat)^2)
      X = (N + h)*cos(lat)*cos(lon)
      Y = (N + h)*cos(lat)*sin(lon)
      Z = (N*(1 - e2) + h)*sin(lat)

  THIS LAYER IS FRAME-AGNOSTIC BY DESIGN. The relation holds in any earth-
  fixed frame given an ellipsoid, so the function is named by coordinate
  representation and not by frame: it is the same arithmetic whether the
  Cartesian triple is in ITRF2020, in ITRF2014 or in a local realization, and
  naming it ecef2geod would imply a frame commitment L4 does not make. The
  terrestrial frame commonly called ECEF is the ITRS, realized by an ITRF; the
  inertial frame commonly called ECI is the GCRS. Changing between ITRF
  realizations is convertItrf, and ITRS to GCRS is rotationItrsToGcrs.

  ONE POINT PER ROW, NOT PER COLUMN. L3 and L4 take Nx3 arrays, one row per
  station, which is how geodetic tables are written; L2 takes 3xN, one column
  per epoch, which is how rotations page-multiply. The boundary between the
  two is a transpose and it is stated in both places. A 3x3 input is read as
  THREE POINTS, never as one transposed triple - the ambiguity of a square
  array is resolved by the documented rule and never by inspection, which is
  finding M-1's lesson applied to a different shape.

  THE DEFAULT ELLIPSOID IS GRS80, not WGS84 as in the v1 toolbox. GRS80 is the
  ellipsoid of the ITRS and of every ITRF product this toolbox reads (IERS
  Conventions 2010, Table 1.2). The two share a and differ in flattening; see
  ACCURACY for what that costs. State the ellipsoid explicitly for any work
  below the millimetre.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision: a few correctly rounded operations, no
  iteration, no series. Measured against PROJ 9.5.1 for a station at 49.14 N,
  12.88 E, 665 m on GRS80 the three components agree to 0.0e+00 m, that is
  bit for bit.

  The round trip through cartesianToGeodetic returns the height to 4.0e-10 m
  and the latitude to 0.0e+00 rad at that station; see cartesianToGeodetic
  for the behaviour at the poles and near the geocentre.

  GRS80 AGAINST WGS84 for the same geodetic triple: 3.8e-05 m in X, 8.8e-06 m
  in Y and 1.1e-04 m in Z at that station, growing to 1.05e-04 m in Z at the
  pole. That is above the sub-millimetre level this toolbox targets, so the
  ellipsoid is a choice and not a detail.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:geodeticToCartesian:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:geodeticToCartesian:latitudeOutOfRange

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Table 1.2.
  W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

xyz = referenceSystem.geodeticToCartesian([49.144969 12.877940 665], ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");
  xyz = referenceSystem.geodeticToCartesian(latLonH, 'Ellipsoid', "WGS84");

KEYWORDS

geodetic, cartesian, ECEF, ITRS, ellipsoid, latitude, longitude, height,
  prime vertical

See also CARTESIANTOGEODETIC, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS, CARTESIANTOENU.

referenceSystem.cartesianToGeodetic

Cartesian coordinates to ellipsoidal geodetic coordinates.

SYNTAX

geodetic = cartesianToGeodetic(cartesian) converts the Nx3 Cartesian
  coordinates [X Y Z] to the Nx3 geodetic coordinates
  [latitude longitude height] on the same earth-fixed frame.

  geodetic = cartesianToGeodetic(cartesian, 'Ellipsoid', "WGS84")
  geodetic = cartesianToGeodetic(cartesian, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m      One point per ROW: X, Y, Z. A 3x3 array
                                     is three points.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type            Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----            -------   -----------
  Ellipsoid   1x1    string|struct   "GRS80"   Name or ellipsoidParameters
                                                struct.
  AngleUnit   1x1    string          "rad"     Unit of the output angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----      -----------
  geodetic   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Column 1 geodetic latitude in
                                         [-pi/2, pi/2], column 2 longitude in
                                         (-pi, pi], column 3 ellipsoidal
                                         height in metres.

METHOD

Vermeille's closed form (2002), which solves the quartic exactly rather than
  iterating. It is best in class for this problem: non-iterative, so the cost
  is fixed and there is no convergence criterion to tune, and accurate at the
  poles, on the equator and at large negative and positive heights, where the
  classical Bowring and Hedgehog iterations degrade.

      p = (X^2 + Y^2)/a^2                q = (1 - e2)*Z^2/a^2
      r = (p + q - e2^2)/6               s = e2^2*p*q/(4*r^3)
      t = cbrt(1 + s + sqrt(s*(2 + s)))  u = r*(1 + t + 1/t)
      v = sqrt(u^2 + e2^2*q)             w = e2*(u + v - q)/(2*v)
      k = sqrt(u + v + w^2) - w          D = k*sqrt(X^2 + Y^2)/(k + e2)
      lat = 2*atan2(Z, D + sqrt(D^2 + Z^2))
      lon = atan2(Y, X)
      h   = (k + e2 - 1)/k*sqrt(D^2 + Z^2)

  TWO DEGENERACIES, BOTH HANDLED EXPLICITLY RATHER THAN LEFT TO NaN.

  On the rotation axis, X = Y = 0, the longitude is not defined by the
  geometry. It is returned as exactly 0 - the value atan2(0, 0) gives - and
  the latitude and height are exact, so the point round trips. This is the
  documented convention, not an accident of the formula.

  Near the geocentre the formulation fails rather than degrades: r becomes
  negative and s*(2 + s) becomes negative, so the square root would be
  complex. The region is the interior of a surface roughly 43 km across
  (|X| below a*e2^2 in the equatorial plane), which contains no point any
  geodetic or orbital calculation can produce. The condition is tested and
  raises nearGeocentre naming the offending row, because returning a complex
  or NaN latitude there would propagate silently - v1 returned complex
  numbers without a word.

  Row layout as geodeticToCartesian: one point per row, Nx3.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Round trip against geodeticToCartesian over
  latitudes -90 to 90 degrees and heights from -10 km to 40000 km reproduces
  the latitude to 1.1e-16 rad (0.7 mm at the surface would be 1.1e-10 rad, so
  this is six orders inside it), the longitude to 2.2e-16 rad and the height
  to 4.1e-10 m. The worst height error over that sweep is at the highest
  altitude, where the ellipsoid is least relevant.

  At the exact pole the height is exact and the latitude is exactly pi/2. At
  a height of -6000 km, well inside the ellipsoid but outside the degenerate
  region, the round trip still holds to 1e-9 m.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:cartesianToGeodetic:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:cartesianToGeodetic:nearGeocentre

REFERENCES

H. Vermeille (2002), Direct transformation from geocentric coordinates to
  geodetic coordinates, Journal of Geodesy 76, 451-454.
  H. Vermeille (2011), An analytical method to transform geocentric into
  geodetic coordinates, Journal of Geodesy 85, 105-117.

EXAMPLE

llh = referenceSystem.cartesianToGeodetic(xyz, 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

cartesian, geodetic, ECEF, ITRS, Vermeille, closed form, latitude, height,
  pole, degeneracy

See also GEODETICTOCARTESIAN, CARTESIANTOSPHERICAL, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS.
