referenceSystem.cartesianToEnu

Earth-fixed Cartesian to local East-North-Up coordinates.

SYNTAX

enu = cartesianToEnu(cartesian, origin) converts the Nx3 earth-fixed
  coordinates to the Nx3 local East-North-Up coordinates about the origin.

  enu = cartesianToEnu(cartesian, origin, 'OriginFormat', "cartesian")
  enu = cartesianToEnu(cartesian, origin, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size          Type     Units      Description
  ----        ----          ----     -----      -----------
  cartesian   Nx3           double   m          Target points, one per ROW.
  origin      1x3 or Nx3    double   see below  The local frame origin, given
                                                ONCE: [lat lon h] when
                                                'OriginFormat' is "geodetic",
                                                [X Y Z] in metres when it is
                                                "cartesian". One row applies to
                                                every target; N rows pair with
                                                the targets.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name           Size   Type            Default      Description
  ----           ----   ----            -------      -----------
  OriginFormat   1x1    string          "geodetic"   "geodetic" or "cartesian".
  Ellipsoid      1x1    string|struct   "GRS80"      Name or struct.
  AngleUnit      1x1    string          "rad"        Unit of the geodetic
                                                     origin angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  enu    Nx3    double   m      One point per row: East, North, Up relative to
                                the origin.

METHOD

enu = R*(x - x0), with R from rotationEcefToEnu at the origin's geodetic
  latitude and longitude.

  THE ORIGIN IS SUPPLIED ONCE (finding M-7). The v1 function took the origin
  twice - as X0, Y0, Z0 and again as lat0, lon0 - with nothing checking that
  the two described the same point. A caller who supplied a geocentric
  latitude alongside a correct Cartesian origin silently got a frame tilted by
  up to 11.5 arcminutes, which puts 0.33 percent of the range into the wrong
  component and cannot be detected downstream. Here the position and the
  orientation are both derived from one argument, so they cannot disagree.

  THE FORMAT IS NAMED, NEVER GUESSED. A 1x3 origin could be either a geodetic
  triple or a Cartesian one, and mistaking one for the other is finding M-1's
  shape ambiguity in another costume. The default is "geodetic", which is what
  a station description usually is, and a Cartesian triple passed under it
  fails immediately on the latitude range check rather than producing plausible
  numbers - the guard is the reason the default is safe.

  The earth-fixed frame is the ITRS, commonly called ECEF, realized by the
  ITRF the input belongs to; the inertial GCRS, commonly called ECI, is not
  involved. Both target and origin must be in the SAME realization - use
  convertItrf first if they are not, since a realization change is up to a
  few centimetres and this function has no way to notice one.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision given the origin. Round trip through
  enuToCartesian reproduces the target to 3.7e-10 m at a range of 100 km and
  to 1.9e-09 m at 10000 km, the error being the differencing of two large
  Cartesian coordinates rather than the rotation.

  The differencing is done before the rotation, deliberately: rotating first
  and differencing after would subtract two numbers of order 6.4e6 m in the
  rotated frame and lose about six digits of a short baseline.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:cartesianToEnu:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:cartesianToEnu:wrongOriginSize
  referenceSystem:cartesianToEnu:originCountMismatch

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

enu = referenceSystem.cartesianToEnu(xyz, [49.145 12.878 665], ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");
  enu = referenceSystem.cartesianToEnu(xyz, xyz0, 'OriginFormat', "cartesian");

KEYWORDS

ENU, East North Up, topocentric, local level, ECEF, ITRS, GCRS, baseline

See also ENUTOCARTESIAN, ROTATIONECEFTOENU, ENUTOAZIMUTHELEVATION.

referenceSystem.enuToCartesian

Local East-North-Up to earth-fixed Cartesian coordinates.

SYNTAX

cartesian = enuToCartesian(enu, origin) converts the Nx3 local East-North-Up
  coordinates about the origin to the Nx3 earth-fixed coordinates.

  cartesian = enuToCartesian(enu, origin, 'OriginFormat', "cartesian")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size          Type     Units      Description
  ----     ----          ----     -----      -----------
  enu      Nx3           double   m          East, North, Up, one point per ROW.
  origin   1x3 or Nx3    double   see below  The local frame origin, given ONCE,
                                             in the format named by
                                             'OriginFormat'.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name           Size   Type            Default      Description
  ----           ----   ----            -------      -----------
  OriginFormat   1x1    string          "geodetic"   "geodetic" or "cartesian".
  Ellipsoid      1x1    string|struct   "GRS80"      Name or struct.
  AngleUnit      1x1    string          "rad"        Unit of the geodetic
                                                     origin angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m      One point per row: X, Y, Z.

METHOD

x = x0 + R'*enu, the exact inverse of cartesianToEnu: R is orthogonal, so
  its transpose is its inverse and nothing is solved. The origin is supplied
  once and in a named format, for the reasons given in cartesianToEnu; the
  two functions share that argument handling so that a round trip cannot use
  two different origins.

  The earth-fixed frame is the ITRS, commonly called ECEF; the inertial GCRS,
  commonly called ECI, is not involved.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Round trip through cartesianToEnu reproduces the
  local vector to 3.7e-10 m at 100 km and 1.9e-09 m at 10000 km, limited by
  the Cartesian differencing rather than by the rotation.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:enuToCartesian:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:enuToCartesian:wrongOriginSize
  referenceSystem:enuToCartesian:originCountMismatch

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

xyz = referenceSystem.enuToCartesian(enu, [49.145 12.878 665], ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

ENU, East North Up, topocentric, local level, ECEF, ITRS, GCRS

See also CARTESIANTOENU, ROTATIONECEFTOENU, AZIMUTHELEVATIONTOENU.

referenceSystem.enuToNed

Local East-North-Up to local North-East-Down coordinates.

SYNTAX

ned = enuToNed(enu) exchanges the first two components and reverses the
  third of the Nx3 local coordinates.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  enu    Nx3    double   any    East, North, Up, one point per ROW. The unit is
                                whatever the caller uses; positions, velocities
                                and accelerations all transform the same way.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  ned    Nx3    double   any    North, East, Down, one point per row.

METHOD

ned = [n, e, -u]. As a matrix the transformation is
  P = [0 1 0; 1 0 0; 0 0 -1], which is its own inverse and has determinant -1
  because it exchanges two axes and reflects the third; the composition with
  an earth-fixed rotation is still a proper rotation, which is why
  rotationEcefToNed has determinant +1.

  ENU AND NED ARE DISTINCT FRAMES, NEVER A FLAG (decision D36). This function
  and its partner nedToEnu exist as two names so that a call site says which
  frame it produces. A boolean would leave a reader unable to tell which
  convention a variable holds, and every wrong choice yields plausible numbers
  of the right magnitude with two components exchanged and one sign wrong.

  No time, no ellipsoid and no origin enter: the exchange is the same
  everywhere on Earth.

ACCURACY

Exact. Indexing and one sign change, no arithmetic. The round trip through
  nedToEnu is bit-identical to the input.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:enuToNed:wrongPointSize

REFERENCES

P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.1.

EXAMPLE

ned = referenceSystem.enuToNed(enu);

KEYWORDS

ENU, NED, local level, navigation frame, axis exchange, topocentric

See also NEDTOENU, ROTATIONECEFTONED, CARTESIANTOENU.

referenceSystem.nedToEnu

Local North-East-Down to local East-North-Up coordinates.

SYNTAX

enu = nedToEnu(ned) exchanges the first two components and reverses the
  third of the Nx3 local coordinates.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  ned    Nx3    double   any    North, East, Down, one point per ROW.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  enu    Nx3    double   any    East, North, Up, one point per row.

METHOD

enu = [e, n, -d], the same permutation and reflection as enuToNed, which is
  its own inverse. It is written as a separate file rather than as a direction
  flag because the frame belongs in the name (decision D36); see enuToNed for
  the full argument.

ACCURACY

Exact. Indexing and one sign change. The round trip through enuToNed is
  bit-identical to the input.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:nedToEnu:wrongPointSize

REFERENCES

P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.1.

EXAMPLE

enu = referenceSystem.nedToEnu(ned);

KEYWORDS

NED, ENU, local level, navigation frame, axis exchange, topocentric

See also ENUTONED, ROTATIONECEFTOENU, ROTATIONECEFTONED.

referenceSystem.rotationEcefToEnu

Earth-fixed to local East-North-Up rotation.

SYNTAX

R = rotationEcefToEnu(origin) returns the 3x3xN rotation from the earth-
  fixed frame to the local East-North-Up frame at each of the N geodetic
  origins.

  R = rotationEcefToEnu(origin, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size          Type     Units    Description
  ----     ----          ----     -----    -----------
  origin   Nx2 or Nx3    double   rad|deg  One origin per ROW: column 1
                                           geodetic latitude, column 2
                                           longitude. A third column is
                                           accepted and ignored, because the
                                           height does not affect the
                                           orientation of the local frame.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------   -----------
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"     "rad" or "deg" for the origin angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices. Row 1 is the East unit
                                  vector in earth-fixed components, row 2
                                  North, row 3 Up.

METHOD

THE MATRIX IS COMPOSED FROM ELEMENTARY ROTATIONS, not written out as sines
  and cosines:

      R = rotationX(pi/2 - latitude) * rotationZ(longitude + pi/2)

  The first turn about the polar axis carries the x axis to East, the second
  about the new East axis carries z to Up. Duplicating the nine trigonometric
  entries here would be the two-sources-of-truth pattern in miniature; L4
  calling L4b is the intended direction (decision D34), and the agreement with
  the explicit form is a reference test rather than an assumption.

  The result satisfies enu = R*(x - x0), with x and x0 in the same earth-fixed
  frame. The earth-fixed frame is the ITRS, commonly called ECEF and realized
  here by whichever ITRF the input coordinates belong to; the inertial frame
  commonly called ECI is the GCRS and is not involved - a local level frame is
  a rotation of the terrestrial frame and never of the celestial one.

  THE LATITUDE MUST BE GEODETIC. A geocentric latitude differs by up to
  11.5 arcminutes, which tilts the local frame by that much and puts up to
  3.3e-3 of the range into the wrong component; nothing downstream can detect
  it, which is why cartesianToEnu takes the origin once and derives both the
  position and the orientation from it (finding M-7).

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Orthogonality residual max|R'R - I| measured
  4.4e-16 over a global grid of origins, and the composed form agrees with the
  explicit trigonometric matrix to 2.8e-16.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationEcefToEnu:wrongOriginSize
  referenceSystem:rotationEcefToEnu:latitudeOutOfRange

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.
  P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.1.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationEcefToEnu([49.145 12.878], 'AngleUnit', "deg");
  enu = (R*(xyz - xyz0).').';

KEYWORDS

ENU, East North Up, local level, topocentric, ECEF, ITRS, GCRS, rotation

See also ROTATIONECEFTONED, CARTESIANTOENU, ROTATIONX, ROTATIONZ.

Created: 2026-07-26 04:50 (CEST, UTC+02:00) - session S4, prompt P12

referenceSystem.rotationEcefToNed

Earth-fixed to local North-East-Down rotation.

SYNTAX

R = rotationEcefToNed(origin) returns the 3x3xN rotation from the earth-
  fixed frame to the local North-East-Down frame at each of the N geodetic
  origins.

  R = rotationEcefToNed(origin, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size          Type     Units    Description
  ----     ----          ----     -----    -----------
  origin   Nx2 or Nx3    double   rad|deg  One origin per ROW: geodetic
                                           latitude, longitude and an optional
                                           ignored height.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------   -----------
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"     "rad" or "deg" for the origin angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices. Row 1 is the North unit
                                  vector in earth-fixed components, row 2
                                  East, row 3 Down.

METHOD

Composed from elementary rotations, as rotationEcefToEnu:

      R = rotationY(-(latitude + pi/2)) * rotationZ(longitude)

  NED IS A DISTINCT FRAME, NEVER A FLAG ON ENU (decision D36). The two differ
  by an axis exchange composed with a reflection of the third axis, so a
  boolean argument would make them indistinguishable at a call site: a reader
  could not tell which one a variable holds, and both give plausible numbers.
  Two named functions make the frame part of the name, which is the same rule
  that gives this toolbox geodeticToCartesian rather than a direction flag.

  NED is left handed in the sense that navigation uses it - North, East, Down
  with Down positive towards the ellipsoid - and the matrix is still a proper
  rotation with determinant +1, because the axis exchange and the sign flip
  each contribute -1. Check the determinant if in doubt; it is asserted in a
  precision test.

  The earth-fixed frame is the ITRS, commonly called ECEF; the inertial frame
  commonly called ECI is the GCRS and is not involved here.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Orthogonality residual max|R'R - I| measured
  4.4e-16 over a global grid of origins, determinant 1 to 2.2e-16, and the
  identity R_ned = P*R_enu with P the [N E D] permutation of [E N U] holds to
  2.8e-16.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationEcefToNed:wrongOriginSize
  referenceSystem:rotationEcefToNed:latitudeOutOfRange

REFERENCES

P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.1.
  W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationEcefToNed([49.145 12.878], 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

NED, North East Down, local level, navigation frame, ECEF, ITRS, GCRS,
  rotation

See also ROTATIONECEFTOENU, ENUTONED, ROTATIONY, ROTATIONZ.

referenceSystem.enuToAzimuthElevation

Local East-North-Up to azimuth, elevation and range.

SYNTAX

azimuthElevation = enuToAzimuthElevation(enu) converts the Nx3 local
  East-North-Up coordinates to the Nx3 polar coordinates
  [azimuth elevation range].

  azimuthElevation = enuToAzimuthElevation(enu, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  enu    Nx3    double   m      East, North, Up, one point per ROW.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------   -----------
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"     Unit of the output angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name               Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----               ----   ----     -----      -----------
  azimuthElevation   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Column 1 azimuth in [0, 2*pi),
                                                measured CLOCKWISE FROM NORTH;
                                                column 2 elevation above the
                                                local horizon in
                                                [-pi/2, pi/2]; column 3 slant
                                                range in metres.

METHOD

range     = sqrt(e^2 + n^2 + u^2)
      azimuth   = mod(atan2(e, n), 2*pi)
      elevation = asin(u/range)

  THE ARGUMENT ORDER atan2(e, n) IS THE POINT OF THE FUNCTION. Azimuth runs
  clockwise from North, which is the opposite sense and a different zero from
  the mathematical atan2(y, x); writing atan2(n, e) instead gives an angle
  that is still in range, still smooth and wrong by the reflection
  azimuth -> pi/2 - azimuth. Nothing downstream can detect it. The convention
  here is the geodetic and surveying one: North 0, East pi/2, South pi,
  West 3*pi/2.

  Elevation is the angle above the local horizon, not the zenith distance;
  zenith distance is pi/2 minus this.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Round trip through azimuthElevationToEnu
  reproduces the local vector to 3.7e-10 m relative at every elevation, and
  the azimuth to 2.2e-16 rad away from the zenith.

  AT ZERO RANGE THE DIRECTION IS UNDEFINED and the elevation is returned as
  NaN, with the azimuth 0 and the range 0. This is the documented
  undefined-but-valid case of the diagnostic policy (section C.6 rule 5): the
  point coincides with the origin, which is legitimate input, so it is not an
  error, and NaN is the honest answer for an angle that does not exist.

  AT THE ZENITH the azimuth is determined by e and n at the level of the
  rounding of the input; a target 1 mm from the vertical at 1000 km range has
  its azimuth defined to about 1e-9 rad, and one exactly overhead has no
  azimuth at all and gets 0. The elevation stays exact throughout.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:enuToAzimuthElevation:wrongPointSize

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

ael = referenceSystem.enuToAzimuthElevation(enu, 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

azimuth, elevation, slant range, topocentric, ENU, horizon, zenith,
  visibility

See also AZIMUTHELEVATIONTOENU, CARTESIANTOENU, ENUTONED.

referenceSystem.azimuthElevationToEnu

Azimuth, elevation and range to local East-North-Up.

SYNTAX

enu = azimuthElevationToEnu(azimuthElevation) converts the Nx3 polar
  coordinates [azimuth elevation range] to the Nx3 local East-North-Up
  coordinates.

  enu = azimuthElevationToEnu(azimuthElevation, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name               Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----               ----   ----     -----      -----------
  azimuthElevation   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Column 1 azimuth clockwise from
                                                North, column 2 elevation above
                                                the horizon, column 3 slant
                                                range in metres. One point per
                                                ROW.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------   -----------
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"     Unit of the input angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  enu    Nx3    double   m      East, North, Up, one point per row.

METHOD

e = range*cos(elevation)*sin(azimuth)
      n = range*cos(elevation)*cos(azimuth)
      u = range*sin(elevation)

  Note sin against the azimuth for East and cos for North, which is the
  clockwise-from-North convention of enuToAzimuthElevation and the exact
  inverse of it. The azimuth is not range checked: any real value is
  meaningful modulo a full turn, and wrapping it silently would hide a caller
  who supplied degrees under the default radian setting - a value of 275 is a
  plausible azimuth in degrees and an implausible one in radians, so it is
  left to produce a visibly rotated result rather than a quietly wrapped one.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Round trip through enuToAzimuthElevation
  reproduces the angles to 2.2e-16 rad and the range to 2.3e-16 relative.

  An elevation outside [-pi/2, pi/2] is not refused; it produces a negative
  effective range through the cosine, which is the mathematically consistent
  continuation and is documented rather than guarded, since the round trip
  then returns the equivalent canonical triple.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:azimuthElevationToEnu:wrongPointSize

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

enu = referenceSystem.azimuthElevationToEnu([45 30 1000], ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

azimuth, elevation, slant range, topocentric, ENU, pointing, horizon

See also ENUTOAZIMUTHELEVATION, ENUTOCARTESIAN, CARTESIANTOENU.

referenceSystem.cartesianToSpherical

Cartesian to geocentric spherical coordinates.

SYNTAX

spherical = cartesianToSpherical(cartesian) converts the Nx3 Cartesian
  coordinates to the Nx3 spherical coordinates
  [geocentricLatitude longitude radius].

  spherical = cartesianToSpherical(cartesian, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m      One point per ROW: X, Y, Z.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------   -----------
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"     Unit of the output angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----      -----------
  spherical   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Column 1 GEOCENTRIC latitude, the
                                         angle from the equatorial plane, in
                                         [-pi/2, pi/2]; column 2 longitude in
                                         (-pi, pi]; column 3 geocentric radius.

METHOD

radius    = sqrt(X^2 + Y^2 + Z^2)
      latitude  = asin(Z/radius)
      longitude = atan2(Y, X)

  THE LATITUDE IS GEOCENTRIC AND NOT GEODETIC, and the difference is large:
  up to 11.5 arcminutes, that is 21 km along the meridian, at 45 degrees
  latitude. The two are equal only on the equator and at the poles. Use
  cartesianToGeodetic for the geodetic latitude, which is the one meant by
  "latitude" without qualification in every geodetic product. The longitude is
  identical in the two systems; the radius is not a height.

  No ellipsoid enters and none is accepted: spherical coordinates are a
  representation of the Cartesian triple, so this function is frame-agnostic
  in the same sense as the rest of L4 and works unchanged in a body-fixed
  frame of any body. That is why the deferred planetary work (Part H) needs no
  spherical function of its own.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Round trip through sphericalToCartesian
  reproduces the position to 2.4e-16 relative over radii from 1 m to
  1e12 m.

  At the geocentre, radius 0, the latitude is NaN and the longitude 0. This is
  the documented undefined-but-valid case of section C.6 rule 5 rather than an
  error, since the origin is a legitimate point of the frame; the radius,
  which carries the information, is exactly 0.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:cartesianToSpherical:wrongPointSize

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

sph = referenceSystem.cartesianToSpherical(xyz, 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

spherical, geocentric latitude, radius, longitude, cartesian, ECEF, ITRS

See also SPHERICALTOCARTESIAN, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC, GEODETICTOCARTESIAN.

Created: 2026-07-26 05:26 (CEST, UTC+02:00) - session S4, prompt P12

referenceSystem.sphericalToCartesian

Geocentric spherical to Cartesian coordinates.

SYNTAX

cartesian = sphericalToCartesian(spherical) converts the Nx3 spherical
  coordinates [geocentricLatitude longitude radius] to the Nx3 Cartesian
  coordinates.

  cartesian = sphericalToCartesian(spherical, 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units      Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----      -----------
  spherical   Nx3    double   rad|deg,m  Column 1 GEOCENTRIC latitude, column 2
                                         longitude, column 3 geocentric radius.
                                         One point per ROW.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------   -----------
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"     Unit of the input angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----        ----   ----     -----  -----------
  cartesian   Nx3    double   m      One point per row: X, Y, Z.

METHOD

X = radius*cos(latitude)*cos(longitude)
      Y = radius*cos(latitude)*sin(longitude)
      Z = radius*sin(latitude)

  The latitude is GEOCENTRIC. Passing a geodetic latitude here is the most
  likely misuse and it is undetectable from the result: it displaces the point
  by up to 21 km along the meridian while leaving a perfectly plausible
  Cartesian triple. Convert with geodeticToCartesian instead, which takes the
  ellipsoid the geodetic latitude is referred to.

  No ellipsoid enters; the function is frame-agnostic.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Round trip through cartesianToSpherical
  reproduces the position to 2.4e-16 relative.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:sphericalToCartesian:wrongPointSize

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 4.1.

EXAMPLE

xyz = referenceSystem.sphericalToCartesian([48.5 12.0 6371000], ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

spherical, geocentric latitude, radius, cartesian, ECEF, ITRS

See also CARTESIANTOSPHERICAL, GEODETICTOCARTESIAN, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC.

referenceSystem.geodeticToUtm

Geodetic coordinates to Universal Transverse Mercator.

SYNTAX

eastingNorthing = geodeticToUtm(geodetic) projects the Nx2 or Nx3 geodetic
  coordinates onto UTM, choosing the standard zone for each point.

  [eastingNorthing, zone, hemisphere, latitudeBand] = geodeticToUtm(...)
  also returns the zone number, the hemisphere and the MGRS latitude band.

  ... = geodeticToUtm(geodetic, 'Zone', 32) forces one zone for every point.
  ... = geodeticToUtm(geodetic, 'ZoneException', "norway") applies the widened
  zones for southern Norway and Svalbard.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size          Type     Units    Description
  ----       ----          ----     -----    -----------
  geodetic   Nx2 or Nx3    double   rad|deg  Column 1 geodetic latitude,
                                             column 2 longitude, one point per
                                             ROW. A third column is accepted
                                             and ignored: UTM is a projection
                                             of the ellipsoid surface and does
                                             not carry height.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name            Size          Type            Default   Description
  ----            ----          ----            -------   -----------
  Ellipsoid       1x1           string|struct   "GRS80"   Name or struct.
  AngleUnit       1x1           string          "rad"     Unit of the input.
  Zone            1x1 or Nx1    double          []        Force the zone
                                                          instead of deriving
                                                          it from longitude.
  ZoneException   1x1           string          "none"    "none" or "norway".

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name              Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----              ----   ----     -----  -----------
  eastingNorthing   Nx2    double   m      Column 1 easting, column 2 northing.
  zone              Nx1    double   -      UTM zone number, 1 to 60.
  hemisphere        Nx1    double   -      +1 north, -1 south. A latitude of
                                           exactly zero is treated as north.
  latitudeBand      Nx1    string   -      MGRS band letter C to X, omitting I
                                           and O; "" outside -80 to 84 degrees.

METHOD

The conformal Transverse Mercator through the Krueger n-series, with
  k0 = 0.9996, false easting 500000 m and false northing 0 in the north and
  10000000 m in the south. The series is truncated at n^3.

  THE SERIES STAYS AT n^3 AND IS NOT RAISED TO n^6, which reverses an earlier
  instruction in the handover and is a measured decision rather than a
  preference. Compared against PROJ 9.5.1, which uses a higher-order
  formulation, over latitudes -79.5 to 83.5 the n^3 truncation costs

      offset from CM      easting        northing
      --------------      -------        --------
        0 deg             0.0e+00 m      1.71e-04 m
        1 deg             3.6e-06 m      1.71e-04 m
        3 deg             1.1e-05 m      1.72e-04 m
        6 deg             2.2e-05 m      1.75e-04 m
       20 deg             8.8e-05 m      2.27e-04 m

  THE TWO COLUMNS ARE DIFFERENT ERRORS AND THE FIRST MEASUREMENT OF THIS
  MISSED ONE OF THEM. The easting error is the transverse part and grows with
  the offset from the central meridian, which is the behaviour one expects and
  looks for. The northing error is the MERIDIAN ARC series and is essentially
  independent of the offset - it is 1.71e-04 m even ON the central meridian,
  where the easting error is exactly zero. Revision 1.52 of the handover
  recorded 6.3e-05 m for this truncation; that figure was the easting alone,
  and the dominant term is three times larger. Corrected in revision 1.53.

  Both are still well inside a millimetre, so the verdict is unchanged and n^6
  would buy nothing a UTM coordinate can use. The figures also show that the
  often-quoted "valid only within the zone" rule is about the projection's
  distortion, not about this series.

  ZONE SELECTION. The standard rule is zone = floor((lon + 180)/6) + 1 with
  the central meridian at 6*zone - 183 degrees. The Norway and Svalbard
  widenings are NOT applied by default, because a coordinate computed under
  them differs by a whole zone from one computed without and no software can
  tell afterwards which rule was used. 'ZoneException' set to "norway" applies
  them: zone 32 widened over southern Norway for latitudes 56 to 64 degrees
  and longitudes 3 to 12, and the 31/33/35/37 arrangement over Svalbard for
  latitudes 72 to 84.

  A forced 'Zone' is used exactly as given, including for points outside it,
  which is the usual reason to force one: keeping a survey in a single zone
  across a boundary. The truncation figures above show what that costs.

ACCURACY

Against PROJ 9.5.1 on GRS80, at 49.144969 N 12.877940 E in its standard
  zone 33: easting agrees to 3.0e-06 m and northing to 1.7e-04 m. In zone 56
  south, at 33.858611 S 151.214167 E: easting to 3.3e-06 m and northing to
  1.3e-04 m.

  THE ROUND TRIP THROUGH utmToGeodetic DOES NOT CLOSE AT MACHINE PRECISION,
  and an earlier revision of this block claimed it did. Measured: 6.0e-04 m of
  ground distance at the station above, 3.0e-04 m at Sydney, and 7.4e-04 m
  worst case over -79.5 to 83.5 degrees, peaking near +-55. The forward alpha
  series and the inverse beta series are inverse to O(n^3) only; they are two
  independently truncated series and not exact inverses of one another, so
  their errors add rather than cancel. The residual decomposes as 1.7e-04 m
  from this function and 5.9e-04 m from the inverse, which means the inverse
  rectifying-latitude series is the dominant term in the pair and is where an
  extra order would pay if one is ever wanted.

  UTM is defined for latitudes -80 to 84 degrees. Outside that the projection
  is still computed and grows rapidly distorted; the latitude band is returned
  as "" to say so, and no error is raised, because the projection remains
  mathematically well defined and some products use it beyond the standard
  limits.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:geodeticToUtm:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:geodeticToUtm:latitudeOutOfRange
  referenceSystem:geodeticToUtm:zoneOutOfRange
  referenceSystem:geodeticToUtm:zoneCountMismatch

REFERENCES

L. Krueger (1912), Konforme Abbildung des Erdellipsoids in der Ebene,
  Veroeffentlichung des Koeniglich Preussischen Geodaetischen Instituts 52.
  C. F. F. Karney (2011), Transverse Mercator with an accuracy of a few
  nanometers, Journal of Geodesy 85, 475-485.
  IOGP Publication 373-7-2, Geomatics Guidance Note 7 part 2, Sect. 3.5.

EXAMPLE

[en, zone, hemisphere] = referenceSystem.geodeticToUtm( ...
      [49.144969 12.877940], 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

UTM, Transverse Mercator, Krueger, projection, easting, northing, zone,
  MGRS, grid

See also UTMTOGEODETIC, GEODETICTOCARTESIAN, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS.

referenceSystem.utmToGeodetic

Universal Transverse Mercator to geodetic coordinates.

SYNTAX

geodetic = utmToGeodetic(eastingNorthing, zone, hemisphere) converts the
  Nx2 UTM coordinates back to the Nx2 geodetic coordinates [latitude longitude].

  geodetic = utmToGeodetic(..., 'AngleUnit', "deg")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name              Size          Type     Units  Description
  ----              ----          ----     -----  -----------
  eastingNorthing   Nx2           double   m      Column 1 easting, column 2
                                                  northing, one point per ROW.
  zone              1x1 or Nx1    double   -      UTM zone number, 1 to 60.
  hemisphere        1x1 or Nx1    double   -      +1 north, -1 south. It cannot
                                                  be derived from the northing,
                                                  because the false northing
                                                  makes the two hemispheres
                                                  overlap.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type            Default   Description
  ----        ----   ----            -------   -----------
  Ellipsoid   1x1    string|struct   "GRS80"   Name or struct.
  AngleUnit   1x1    string          "rad"     Unit of the output angles.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name       Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----       ----   ----     -----    -----------
  geodetic   Nx2    double   rad|deg  Column 1 geodetic latitude, column 2
                                      longitude. No height: UTM does not carry
                                      one, and inventing a zero would be a
                                      quiet assumption about the datum.

METHOD

The inverse Krueger n-series, truncated at n^3 exactly as geodeticToUtm, so
  that the two are consistent to machine precision even where both share the
  truncation error against a higher-order formulation. The beta coefficients
  invert the alpha series and the delta series maps the conformal latitude
  back to the geodetic one.

  THE HEMISPHERE IS A REQUIRED ARGUMENT AND IS NOT INFERRED. A northing of
  5450815 m is a valid coordinate in both hemispheres - in the north it is
  about 49 degrees N, in the south about 41 degrees S - because the southern
  grid carries a false northing of 10000000 m. Guessing from the value would
  be right most of the time and silently wrong near the equator, which is the
  worst possible behaviour. geodeticToUtm returns the hemisphere as an output
  for exactly this reason: carry it with the coordinates.

ACCURACY

MEASURED: feeding this function a grid computed by PROJ 9.5.1 reproduces the
  geodetic coordinates to 5.9e-04 m of ground distance over latitudes -79.5 to
  83.5, essentially independent of the offset from the central meridian. That
  is the inverse truncation on its own, and it is the LARGER HALF of the pair:
  the forward series contributes 1.7e-04 m. The full round trip against
  geodeticToUtm therefore closes to 7.4e-04 m worst case, peaking near +-55
  degrees latitude, and not at machine precision - an earlier revision of this
  block claimed 1.4e-10 m, which was wrong by six orders and was corrected
  when the S4 run failed a round-trip test written to that claim.

  The dominant term is the delta series that maps the rectifying latitude back
  to the geodetic one. If an extra order is ever wanted, that is the series to
  extend, and extending it alone would buy most of the available improvement -
  which is a more useful thing to know than the aggregate figure.

  No range check is made on the easting and northing: values far outside the
  zone are projected consistently and grow distorted, which is the same
  behaviour as the forward function and is what a caller forcing a single zone
  across a boundary needs.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:utmToGeodetic:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:utmToGeodetic:zoneOutOfRange
  referenceSystem:utmToGeodetic:zoneCountMismatch
  referenceSystem:utmToGeodetic:hemisphereCountMismatch
  referenceSystem:utmToGeodetic:invalidHemisphere

REFERENCES

L. Krueger (1912), Konforme Abbildung des Erdellipsoids in der Ebene,
  Veroeffentlichung des Koeniglich Preussischen Geodaetischen Instituts 52.
  C. F. F. Karney (2011), Transverse Mercator with an accuracy of a few
  nanometers, Journal of Geodesy 85, 475-485.

EXAMPLE

llh = referenceSystem.utmToGeodetic([782787.19 5450815.03], 32, 1, ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

UTM, Transverse Mercator, Krueger, inverse projection, easting, northing,
  zone, hemisphere

See also GEODETICTOUTM, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC, ELLIPSOIDPARAMETERS.

referenceSystem.geoidUndulation

Interpolate the geoid undulation from a regular grid.

SYNTAX

undulation = geoidUndulation(geodetic, geoidGrid) interpolates the geoid
  undulation N at the Nx2 or Nx3 query points from a regular latitude and
  longitude grid.

  undulation = geoidUndulation(geodetic, geoidGrid, 'Method', "spline")

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name          Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----          ----   ----     -----    -----------
  geodetic      Nx2    double   rad|deg  Query points, column 1 latitude,
                                         column 2 longitude, one per ROW. A
                                         third column is accepted and ignored.
  geoidGrid     1x1    struct   -        The grid; see below.
  .latitude     1xM    double   deg      Grid latitudes, strictly monotonic,
                                         ascending or descending.
  .longitude    1xK    double   deg      Grid longitudes, strictly ascending.
                                         Either convention, -180 to 180 or
                                         0 to 360; queries are wrapped into it.
  .undulation   MxK    double   m        N on the grid, rows latitude, columns
                                         longitude, positive where the geoid is
                                         above the ellipsoid.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name        Size   Type     Default    Description
  ----        ----   ----     -------    -----------
  Method      1x1    string   "linear"   "linear", "spline" or "cubic", passed
                                         to interp2.
  AngleUnit   1x1    string   "rad"      Unit of the query angles. THE GRID IS
                                         ALWAYS IN DEGREES.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----  -----------
  undulation   Nx1    double   m      N at each query point.

METHOD

Bilinear interpolation of the grid by default, with the query longitudes
  wrapped into the grid's own range so that a grid on 0 to 360 and a query at
  -10 degrees meet correctly. The grid is not bundled with this toolbox and
  never will be: EGM96 is about 2 MB and EGM2008 at 1 arcminute is about
  2.6 GB, and a geoid model is a choice the user makes, not a constant.

  LOADING A GRID. Any source works as long as it becomes the struct above.
  For the NGA distributions the 15 arcminute EGM96 file and the 1 or
  2.5 arcminute EGM2008 files are plain binary grids of single precision
  values, row major from north to south and from 0 degrees east; read them
  with fread, reshape, and build the two coordinate vectors from the stated
  spacing. A GeoTIFF or NetCDF distribution can be read with readgeoraster or
  ncread and needs the same three fields.

  A POINT OUTSIDE THE GRID IS AN ERROR, not a NaN. A regional grid is a common
  input and a query outside it is a mistake in the data or in the region, not
  an undefined geometry: returning NaN would put it into a height that looks
  like a number until it is differenced. The longitude cannot be outside, since
  it wraps; only the latitude range can be violated. This differs from the
  undefined-but-valid NaN of enuToAzimuthElevation, and the difference is
  exactly the one section C.6 rule 5 draws.

  THE NAME IS geoidUndulation AND NOT geoidheight. N is the undulation, the
  separation between the geoid and the ellipsoid; "geoid height" is ambiguous
  with the orthometric height H, which is the height OF A POINT above the
  geoid. The v1 function also shadowed the Aerospace Toolbox function of that
  name with a different argument order, which the namespace already solves;
  the rename is on terminological merit.

ACCURACY

The interpolation error, not the geoid model error. Bilinear interpolation
  of a smooth field on a grid of spacing d carries an error of about
  d^2/8 times the second derivative of N. For EGM2008 on a 1 arcminute grid
  this is below 1 mm in mountainous terrain and far less elsewhere; on the
  15 arcminute EGM96 grid it reaches a few centimetres in areas of strong
  gradient. "spline" reduces the interpolation error by roughly an order of
  magnitude on a smooth grid and is worth using on the coarser ones.

  The model error dominates in every case: about 0.1 m globally for EGM2008,
  better than 0.05 m over well surveyed regions, and 0.01 m for a modern
  regional quasigeoid. No interpolation method improves on that.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:geoidUndulation:wrongPointSize
  referenceSystem:geoidUndulation:malformedGrid
  referenceSystem:geoidUndulation:gridNotMonotonic
  referenceSystem:geoidUndulation:outsideGrid

REFERENCES

N. K. Pavlis, S. A. Holmes, S. C. Kenyon, J. K. Factor (2012), The
  development and evaluation of the Earth Gravitational Model 2008 (EGM2008),
  Journal of Geophysical Research 117, B04406.
  W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 6.

EXAMPLE

grid = struct('latitude', -90:0.25:90, 'longitude', 0:0.25:359.75, ...
      'undulation', N);
  n = referenceSystem.geoidUndulation([49.14 12.88], grid, ...
      'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

geoid, undulation, EGM96, EGM2008, quasigeoid, interpolation, grid,
  orthometric height

See also ELLIPSOIDALTOORTHOMETRIC, ORTHOMETRICTOELLIPSOIDAL, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC.

referenceSystem.ellipsoidalToOrthometric

Ellipsoidal height to orthometric height.

SYNTAX

orthometricHeight = ellipsoidalToOrthometric(ellipsoidalHeight,
  undulation) computes H = h - N elementwise.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                ----   ----     -----  -----------
  ellipsoidalHeight   any    double   m      h, height above the ellipsoid.
  undulation     any    double   m      N, height of the geoid above the
                                             ellipsoid, positive where the
                                             geoid is above it. Same size as h,
                                             or scalar.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                ----   ----     -----  -----------
  orthometricHeight   any    double   m      H, height above the geoid.

METHOD

H = h - N. One line, and the value of the function is not the arithmetic but
  the three things around it: the name states the direction, so no flag can be
  set the wrong way; the sign of N is documented, positive where the geoid is
  above the ellipsoid, which is the IAG convention and the one every published
  geoid model uses; and the sizes are checked, so a column of heights and a
  row of undulations cannot expand implicitly into a matrix of every
  combination.

  THE ARGUMENT IS NAMED undulation AND NOT geoidUndulation, which reads worse
  and is nevertheless right: geoidUndulation is a sibling function in this
  namespace, and a local variable of that name is indistinguishable, to a
  reader and to the static namespace scan alike, from an unqualified call to
  it. The S4 run flagged exactly that. This is decision D54's rule - the one
  that renamed leapSeconds - applied to a variable rather than to a function.

  That last one is a real hazard rather than a hypothetical: MATLAB's implicit
  expansion turns a 1000x1 and a 1x1000 into a 1000x1000 array without a word,
  and the result is plausible until it is plotted.

  The relation is a definition and not an approximation, but H is only as good
  as N. The two heights refer to different surfaces and to different physics -
  h is geometric, H is a levelled height along the plumb line - so the
  difference is the geoid model's error, not a rounding.

ACCURACY

Exact: one subtraction, error at most half an ulp, below 1e-12 m for any
  terrestrial height.

  The accuracy of H is that of N. EGM2008 is quoted at about 0.1 m globally
  and better than 0.05 m over well surveyed regions; a regional quasigeoid
  such as GCG2016 reaches 0.01 m. Interpolating a grid adds the error stated
  in geoidUndulation. Nothing here can improve on that, and the millimetre
  claims made elsewhere in this toolbox do not extend to orthometric heights.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:ellipsoidalToOrthometric:sizeMismatch

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 3.4.
  N. K. Pavlis et al. (2012), The development and evaluation of EGM2008,
  Journal of Geophysical Research 117, B04406.

EXAMPLE

N = referenceSystem.geoidUndulation(latLon, grid);
  H = referenceSystem.ellipsoidalToOrthometric(h, N);

KEYWORDS

orthometric height, ellipsoidal height, geoid undulation, levelling, EGM2008

See also ORTHOMETRICTOELLIPSOIDAL, GEOIDUNDULATION, CARTESIANTOGEODETIC.

referenceSystem.orthometricToEllipsoidal

Orthometric height to ellipsoidal height.

SYNTAX

ellipsoidalHeight = orthometricToEllipsoidal(orthometricHeight,
  undulation) computes h = H + N elementwise.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                ----   ----     -----  -----------
  orthometricHeight   any    double   m      H, height above the geoid.
  undulation     any    double   m      N, height of the geoid above the
                                             ellipsoid, positive where the
                                             geoid is above it. Same size as H,
                                             or scalar.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                ----   ----     -----  -----------
  ellipsoidalHeight   any    double   m      h, height above the ellipsoid.

METHOD

h = H + N, the exact inverse of ellipsoidalToOrthometric. Two named
  functions rather than one with a direction flag: the direction belongs in
  the name (section C.7 rule 2), and a sign flag on a one-line function is
  pure risk with no benefit. Revision 1.0 of the design merged the pair into a
  single heightConvert with a direction argument; decision D6 reversed that.

  THE ARGUMENT IS NAMED undulation AND NOT geoidUndulation, which reads worse
  and is nevertheless right: geoidUndulation is a sibling function in this
  namespace, and a local variable of that name is indistinguishable, to a
  reader and to the static namespace scan alike, from an unqualified call to
  it. The S4 run flagged exactly that. This is decision D54's rule - the one
  that renamed leapSeconds - applied to a variable rather than to a function.

  The sign convention for N is the IAG one, positive where the geoid lies
  above the ellipsoid, which is what every published geoid model supplies.
  Sizes are checked so that a column of heights and a row of undulations
  cannot expand implicitly into a matrix of every combination.

ACCURACY

Exact: one addition, error at most half an ulp, below 1e-12 m for any
  terrestrial height.

  The accuracy of h is that of N. EGM2008 is quoted at about 0.1 m globally
  and better than 0.05 m over well surveyed regions; a regional quasigeoid
  reaches 0.01 m. Interpolating a grid adds the error stated in
  geoidUndulation.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:orthometricToEllipsoidal:sizeMismatch

REFERENCES

W. Torge and J. Mueller (2012), Geodesy, 4th ed., de Gruyter, Sect. 3.4.
  N. K. Pavlis et al. (2012), The development and evaluation of EGM2008,
  Journal of Geophysical Research 117, B04406.

EXAMPLE

N = referenceSystem.geoidUndulation(latLon, geoidGrid);
  h = referenceSystem.orthometricToEllipsoidal(H, N);

KEYWORDS

ellipsoidal height, orthometric height, geoid undulation, levelling, EGM2008

See also ELLIPSOIDALTOORTHOMETRIC, GEOIDUNDULATION, GEODETICTOCARTESIAN.

L4b - Rotation algebra

referenceSystem.attitudeConvention

Build or validate the rotation-algebra convention struct.

SYNTAX

convention = attitudeConvention() returns the geodetic default: scalar-first
  Hamilton quaternions, ENU local frame, frame (passive) rotations and the
  intrinsic ZYX Euler sequence.

  convention = attitudeConvention(presetName) returns a named preset,
  "geodetic" or "aerospace".

  convention = attitudeConvention(userStruct) validates a struct the caller
  built and returns it in canonical form. Any unknown field, any missing
  field and any unknown value is an error; nothing is silently ignored and
  nothing is silently filled in.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name            Size   Type            Units  Description
  ----            ----   ----            -----  -----------
  specification   1x1    string|struct   -      Preset name or a convention
                                                struct to validate. A char row
                                                vector is accepted for the
                                                name and converted.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name             Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----             ----   ----     -----  -----------
  convention       1x1    struct   -      Validated convention.
  .sequence        1x1    string   -      Euler sequence, one of the twelve
                                          "XYZ".."ZYZ", or "" where none
                                          applies.
  .rotationType    1x1    string   -      "intrinsic" (body-fixed axes) or
                                          "extrinsic" (space-fixed axes).
  .sense           1x1    string   -      "frame" (passive, alias) or
                                          "vector" (active, alibi).
  .quaternion      1x1    string   -      "scalarFirst" [q0 q1 q2 q3] or
                                          "scalarLast" [q1 q2 q3 q0].
  .product         1x1    string   -      "hamilton" or "shuster".
  .localFrame      1x1    string   -      "ENU", "NED" or "none".
  .source          1x1    string   -      Provenance, free text.

METHOD

Navigation and geodesy disagree on every line of that table, and every
  wrong combination produces a matrix that is still orthogonal, still of unit
  determinant, and still round trips through its own inverse. There is no
  runtime symptom. The error appears only when the result meets somebody
  else's data, which is finding C-2's failure mode exactly, and it is why the
  convention is a struct carried with the data rather than ten name-value
  flags spread over the call sites.

  The two presets:

    field          geodetic (default)   aerospace
    -----          ------------------   ---------
    sequence       ZYX                  ZYX
    rotationType   intrinsic            intrinsic
    sense          frame                frame
    quaternion     scalarFirst          scalarLast
    product        hamilton             shuster
    localFrame     ENU                  NED

  The geodetic default agrees with SOFA's matrix conventions, with the
  GRACE-FO SCA1B quaternion convention and with the rest of this toolbox.
  ZYX intrinsic is the default sequence because it is the near-universal
  choice wherever roll, pitch and yaw are meant, and naming a sequence at all
  is meaningless without one.

  THE DEFAULT IS A DEFAULT, NEVER AN ASSUMPTION. Every function that returns
  a rotation object records the convention it used; every function that
  consumes one reads it. Functions taking two rotation objects compare their
  conventions and error on a mismatch rather than composing across it.

  Validation is exhaustive in MATLAB. The scan for unknown fields uses
  fieldnames and is therefore guarded by coder.target, so it is eliminated
  from generated code; every other check is a static field read and survives
  code generation. A struct reaching generated code is assumed to have been
  validated in MATLAB first.

ACCURACY

Exact. This function performs no arithmetic; it names and checks a
  convention. Its whole accuracy contribution is that it makes the silent
  convention mismatch impossible, which is worth up to 180 degrees of
  attitude error and is not detectable by any numerical test on the result.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:attitudeConvention:unknownPreset
  referenceSystem:attitudeConvention:wrongType
  referenceSystem:attitudeConvention:unknownField
  referenceSystem:attitudeConvention:missingField
  referenceSystem:attitudeConvention:unknownValue

REFERENCES

M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, Journal of the
  Astronautical Sciences 41(4), 439-517.
  J. Sola (2017), Quaternion Kinematics for the Error-State Kalman Filter,
  arXiv:1711.02508, Sect. 1 on the Hamilton and JPL conventions.
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5 for the frame sense.

EXAMPLE

c = referenceSystem.attitudeConvention();
  q = referenceSystem.quaternionFromRotation(R, 'Convention', c);
  navigationConvention = referenceSystem.attitudeConvention("aerospace");

KEYWORDS

attitude, convention, quaternion, Hamilton, Shuster, JPL, Euler, intrinsic,
  extrinsic, passive, active, ENU, NED

See also QUATERNIONFROMROTATION, ROTATIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, MULTIPLYQUATERNIONS.

referenceSystem.rotationX

Elementary rotation about the x axis.

SYNTAX

R = rotationX(angle) returns the 3x3xN frame rotation about the x axis
  through each of the N angles.

  R = rotationX(angle, 'AngleUnit', "deg") takes the angles in degrees.
  R = rotationX(angle, 'Convention', c) selects the rotation sense from the
  convention struct; "vector" returns the transpose of "frame".

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----    -----------
  angle   1xN    double   rad|deg  Rotation angles, one per output page. A
                                   column vector is accepted and used as N
                                   angles; the unit is set by 'AngleUnit'.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                  -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                    "rad" or "deg".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()     Only .sense is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per angle.

METHOD

For the default frame (passive) sense the matrix is

      [ 1     0        0    ]
      [ 0   cos(a)   sin(a) ]
      [ 0  -sin(a)   cos(a) ]

  which rotates the reference frame, not the vector: it is the matrix that
  takes the components of a fixed vector in the old axes to its components in
  the new ones. This is the sense of SOFA iauRx and of every rotation in
  this toolbox, so the elementary rotations compose directly with the L2
  Earth-rotation matrices. With 'Convention' set to the "vector" sense the
  transpose is returned, which rotates the vector and leaves the axes fixed.

  The whole matrix is built by page assignment rather than by a loop, so N
  angles cost one sine, one cosine and one allocation.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision: the four nonzero off-diagonal entries are the
  sine and cosine of the argument, each correctly rounded, so the product
  R'*R differs from the identity by at most a few units in the last place
  (measured 2.2e-16 over the test sweep). Orthogonality does not degrade with
  the size of the angle, and no angle is out of range.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationX:wrongAngleSize

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.4.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, routine iauRx.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationX(deg2rad(30));
  Renu = referenceSystem.rotationX(pi/2 - lat)*referenceSystem.rotationZ(lon + pi/2);

KEYWORDS

rotation, elementary rotation, x axis, frame rotation, passive, direction
  cosine matrix

See also ROTATIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, ATTITUDECONVENTION, APPLYROTATION.

referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-26 02:10 (CEST, UTC+02:00) - session S4, prompt P25

back to contents

referenceSystem.rotationY

Elementary rotation about the y axis.

SYNTAX

R = rotationY(angle) returns the 3x3xN frame rotation about the y axis
  through each of the N angles.

  R = rotationY(angle, 'AngleUnit', "deg") takes the angles in degrees.
  R = rotationY(angle, 'Convention', c) selects the rotation sense from the
  convention struct; "vector" returns the transpose of "frame".

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----    -----------
  angle   1xN    double   rad|deg  Rotation angles, one per output page. A
                                   column vector is accepted and used as N
                                   angles; the unit is set by 'AngleUnit'.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                  -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                    "rad" or "deg".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()     Only .sense is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per angle.

METHOD

For the default frame (passive) sense the matrix is

      [ cos(a)   0  -sin(a) ]
      [   0      1     0    ]
      [ sin(a)   0   cos(a) ]

  which rotates the reference frame, not the vector: it is the matrix that
  takes the components of a fixed vector in the old axes to its components in
  the new ones. This is the sense of SOFA iauRy and of every rotation in
  this toolbox, so the elementary rotations compose directly with the L2
  Earth-rotation matrices. With 'Convention' set to the "vector" sense the
  transpose is returned, which rotates the vector and leaves the axes fixed.

  The whole matrix is built by page assignment rather than by a loop, so N
  angles cost one sine, one cosine and one allocation.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision: the four nonzero off-diagonal entries are the
  sine and cosine of the argument, each correctly rounded, so the product
  R'*R differs from the identity by at most a few units in the last place
  (measured 2.2e-16 over the test sweep). Orthogonality does not degrade with
  the size of the angle, and no angle is out of range.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationY:wrongAngleSize

REFERENCES

IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5.4.
  IAU SOFA release 20231011, routine iauRy.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationY(deg2rad(-20));
  Renu = referenceSystem.rotationX(pi/2 - lat)*referenceSystem.rotationZ(lon + pi/2);

KEYWORDS

rotation, elementary rotation, y axis, frame rotation, passive, direction
  cosine matrix

See also ROTATIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, ATTITUDECONVENTION, APPLYROTATION.

  R = rotationZ(angle, 'AngleUnit', "deg") takes the angles in degrees.
  R = rotationZ(angle, 'Convention', c) selects the rotation sense from the

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----   ----     -----    -----------
  angle   1xN    double   rad|deg  Rotation angles, one per output page. A
                                   column vector is accepted and used as N
                                   angles; the unit is set by 'AngleUnit'.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                  Description

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

METHOD

      [  cos(a)  sin(a)  0 ]
      [ -sin(a)  cos(a)  0 ]
      [    0       0     1 ]

  which rotates the reference frame, not the vector: it is the matrix that
  takes the components of a fixed vector in the old axes to its components in

  this toolbox, so the elementary rotations compose directly with the L2

  sine and cosine of the argument, each correctly rounded, so the product

referenceSystem.rotationFromEulerSequence

Rotation matrix from an Euler angle sequence.

SYNTAX

R = rotationFromEulerSequence(angles) builds the 3x3xN rotation from the
  3xN angles, using the sequence and the intrinsic or extrinsic rule named by
  the convention. All twelve sequences are supported.

  R = rotationFromEulerSequence(angles, 'Convention', c) selects the
  sequence, the rotation type and the sense.
  R = rotationFromEulerSequence(angles, 'AngleUnit', "deg") takes degrees.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----     ----   ----     -----    -----------
  angles   3xN    double   rad|deg  Rotation angles, in the order in which the
                                    sequence names the axes: row 1 is the
                                    rotation about the first named axis. A 3x1
                                    column is one set; N columns are N sets.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                  "rad" or "deg".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sequence, .rotationType
                                                      and .sense are read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per angle set.

METHOD

THE EXTRINSIC FORM IS PRIMITIVE and the intrinsic form is derived from it,
  rather than writing twenty-four cases. For an extrinsic sequence (i,j,k),
  whose rotations are all about the fixed space axes, the matrices multiply in
  the order the sequence is written:

      R = A_i(a1) * A_j(a2) * A_k(a3)

  with A the elementary frame rotations rotationX, rotationY, rotationZ. The
  intrinsic form, whose rotations are about the axes carried by the body, is
  the exact reversal of both the sequence and the angles:

      intrinsic (i,j,k) with (a1,a2,a3)  ==  extrinsic (k,j,i) with (a3,a2,a1)

  so intrinsic ZYX equals extrinsic XYZ with the angles reversed. This is an
  identity, not an approximation, and it is asserted as a precision test.

  With the default frame (passive) sense and the intrinsic ZYX sequence,
  angles = [yaw; pitch; roll] gives the classical
  R = rotationX(roll)*rotationY(pitch)*rotationZ(yaw), the matrix that takes
  a vector's components in the local level frame to its components in the
  body frame. Sign check: with yaw = +30 deg alone, the east axis of the ENU
  frame maps to R*[1;0;0] = [cos30; -sin30; 0], that is, the body x axis has
  swung 30 degrees towards north, which is a positive right-handed rotation
  about up.

  THE SEQUENCE IS READ FROM THE CONVENTION AND FROM NOWHERE ELSE. There is no
  'Sequence' argument, because a sequence given in two places is a sequence
  that can disagree with itself, and no wrong combination of sequence and
  rotation type produces a matrix that looks wrong. Use a different sequence
  by deriving a convention that names it; the derived struct is validated on
  entry like any other.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. The matrix is a product of three exact
  elementary rotations, so the orthogonality residual max|R'R - I| is a few
  units in the last place; measured 6.7e-16 over the random sweep of all
  twelve sequences in TestRotationAlgebra. There is no angle at which the
  construction degrades: gimbal lock is a property of the inverse problem,
  not of this one.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationFromEulerSequence:wrongAngleSize
  referenceSystem:rotationFromEulerSequence:sequenceRequired

REFERENCES

J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude: Euler Angles, Unit Quaternions,
  and Rotation Vectors, Stanford University, Sect. 5.
  M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationFromEulerSequence([yaw; pitch; roll]);

  c = referenceSystem.attitudeConvention();
  c.sequence = "ZXZ";
  c.source = "classical Euler angles";
  R = referenceSystem.rotationFromEulerSequence(angles, 'Convention', c);

KEYWORDS

Euler angles, sequence, intrinsic, extrinsic, Tait-Bryan, rotation matrix,
  direction cosine matrix, attitude

See also EULERSEQUENCEFROMROTATION, ROTATIONFROMROLLPITCHYAW, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

referenceSystem.eulerSequenceFromRotation

Euler angles of a rotation, all twelve sequences.

SYNTAX

angles = eulerSequenceFromRotation(R) extracts the 3xN Euler angles of the
  3x3xN rotation for the sequence and rotation type named by the convention.

  [angles, info] = eulerSequenceFromRotation(R) also reports the convention
  that was used and which pages were at gimbal lock.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per angle set.
                                  Orthogonality is assumed, not checked; see
                                  METHOD.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name            Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----            ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit       1x1    string   "rad"                  Unit of the output.
  Convention      1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sequence,
                                                         .rotationType, .sense.
  LockTolerance   1x1    double   1e-7                   Gimbal-lock threshold
                                                         on the sine or cosine
                                                         of the second angle.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name            Size   Type      Units    Description
  ----            ----   ----      -----    -----------
  angles          3xN    double    rad|deg  Angles in the order the sequence
                                            names the axes. The second angle
                                            lies in [-pi/2, pi/2] for a
                                            Tait-Bryan sequence and in [0, pi]
                                            for a proper Euler sequence; the
                                            other two lie in (-pi, pi].
  info            1x1    struct    -        Diagnostics.
  .convention     1x1    struct    -        The convention actually used.
  .gimbalLock     1xN    logical   -        True where the page was singular.
  .lockCount      1x1    double    -        Number of pages at gimbal lock.
  .lockTolerance  1x1    double    -        Threshold that was applied.

METHOD

ONE ALGORITHM COVERS ALL TWELVE SEQUENCES, driven by three integers: the
  axis indices i and j of the first two rotations, and the parity eps of the
  permutation (i,j,k), which is +1 when (i,j,k) is a cyclic permutation of
  (1,2,3) and -1 otherwise. Writing the extrinsic case, R = A_i(a1) A_j(a2)
  A_k(a3), the extraction is

    Tait-Bryan (i,j,k all different)      proper Euler (i,j,i)
      a2 = atan2(-eps*R(i,k), c)            a2 = atan2(s, R(i,i))
      a1 = atan2( eps*R(j,k), R(k,k))       a1 = atan2(R(j,i),  eps*R(k,i))
      a3 = atan2( eps*R(i,j), R(i,i))       a3 = atan2(R(i,j), -eps*R(i,k))
      c  = hypot(R(j,k), R(k,k))            s  = hypot(R(j,i), R(k,i))

  with k the remaining axis in the proper-Euler case. The intrinsic case is
  the same extraction on the reversed sequence with the angles reversed, which
  is the exact identity used by rotationFromEulerSequence. Both branches and
  both parities were verified against an independent Python implementation
  over 400 random angle sets per sequence per rotation type before this
  function was written; that check found a sign error in the proper-Euler
  singular branch that no round-trip test would have exposed, because the
  matrix it rebuilt was still orthogonal.

  GIMBAL LOCK IS DETECTED, NOT SMOOTHED OVER. At the singular second angle -
  plus or minus 90 degrees for a Tait-Bryan sequence, 0 or 180 degrees for a
  proper Euler one - the first and third rotation axes coincide and only their
  sum or difference is determined by R. The conventional resolution is applied:
  the third angle is set to zero and the whole of the determined combination
  is assigned to the first. The rebuilt matrix is then exactly the input, so a
  round trip through this function is still correct; what is lost is only the
  split, and the loss is reported rather than hidden. One warning is raised
  per call, never one per page (section C.6 rule 2), naming how many pages
  were affected and the first of them.

  Orthogonality is assumed. Every atan2 pair is scale-free, so a matrix that
  is nearly a rotation yields nearly the right angles and one that is not a
  rotation at all yields angles without warning. Use invertRotation with
  'Verify' if the matrix came from anywhere but this toolbox.

ACCURACY

Away from the singularity the round trip R -> angles -> R reproduces the
  matrix to a few units in the last place, measured 8.9e-16 over the sweep of
  all twelve sequences, and the angles themselves to 4.4e-16 rad.

  Approaching the singularity the split of the two coincident rotations is
  conditioned by 1/c, where c is the cosine (Tait-Bryan) or sine (proper
  Euler) of the second angle: at the default threshold of 1e-7 the individual
  first and third angles carry about 1e-9 rad of error while their combination
  and the rebuilt matrix stay at machine precision. Lowering the threshold
  does not improve the angles; it merely stops the function telling you that
  they are no longer separately meaningful.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromRotation:wrongMatrixSize
  referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromRotation:sequenceRequired

WARNINGS

referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromRotation:gimbalLock

REFERENCES

J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude: Euler Angles, Unit Quaternions,
  and Rotation Vectors, Stanford University, Sect. 5.
  E. Bernardes and S. Viollet (2022), Quaternion to Euler angles conversion:
  a direct, general and computationally efficient method, PLoS ONE 17(11).

EXAMPLE

angles = referenceSystem.eulerSequenceFromRotation(R);
  [angles, info] = referenceSystem.eulerSequenceFromRotation(R);
  if info.lockCount > 0
      fprintf('%d epochs at gimbal lock\n', info.lockCount);
  end

KEYWORDS

Euler angles, extraction, gimbal lock, singularity, Tait-Bryan, attitude,
  rotation matrix

See also ROTATIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, ROLLPITCHYAWFROMROTATION, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

referenceSystem.rotationFromRollPitchYaw

Rotation matrix from roll, pitch and yaw.

SYNTAX

R = rotationFromRollPitchYaw(rollPitchYaw) builds the 3x3xN rotation from
  the 3xN angles [roll; pitch; yaw]. This is the intrinsic ZYX Euler sequence
  under the names navigation users expect.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name           Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----           ----   ----     -----    -----------
  rollPitchYaw   3xN    double   rad|deg  Row 1 roll (about the body x axis),
                                          row 2 pitch (y), row 3 yaw (z).
                                          Note the order: it is the reverse of
                                          the ZYX sequence order.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                  "rad" or "deg".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense is read; the
                                                      sequence must be ZYX
                                                      intrinsic or empty.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per angle set.

METHOD

A NAMED SPECIAL CASE, NOT A SECOND IMPLEMENTATION. Roll, pitch and yaw are
  the intrinsic ZYX Euler angles listed in the reverse order, so this function
  reverses the rows and calls rotationFromEulerSequence. Writing out the sines
  and cosines again would be the two-sources-of-truth pattern in miniature;
  the value here is the vocabulary and the argument order, which is where the
  mistakes actually happen.

  With the default frame (passive) sense the result is
  R = rotationX(roll)*rotationY(pitch)*rotationZ(yaw), which takes a vector's
  components in the local level frame to its components in the body frame.
  The local level frame is the one named by convention.localFrame: ENU by
  default, NED under the aerospace preset. THE ANGLES MEAN DIFFERENT THINGS
  IN THE TWO, because the axes they turn about are different, and that is why
  the local frame is part of the convention rather than an assumption.

  A convention naming any sequence other than ZYX intrinsic is refused rather
  than overridden: silently ignoring it would be exactly the failure this
  toolbox exists to prevent, and both shipped presets already satisfy it.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision; see rotationFromEulerSequence. Orthogonality
  residual max|R'R - I| measured 6.7e-16.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationFromRollPitchYaw:wrongAngleSize
  referenceSystem:rotationFromRollPitchYaw:conventionMismatch

REFERENCES

P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.2.
  J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude, Stanford University, Sect. 5.2.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationFromRollPitchYaw([0.01; -0.02; 1.57]);
  Rned = referenceSystem.rotationFromRollPitchYaw(rpy, ...
      'Convention', referenceSystem.attitudeConvention("aerospace"));

KEYWORDS

roll, pitch, yaw, heading, attitude, ZYX, Tait-Bryan, body frame, strapdown

See also ROLLPITCHYAWFROMROTATION, ROTATIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, ATTITUDECONVENTION.
