referenceSystem.rollPitchYawFromRotation

Roll, pitch and yaw of a rotation.

SYNTAX

rollPitchYaw = rollPitchYawFromRotation(R) extracts the 3xN angles
  [roll; pitch; yaw] from the 3x3xN rotation, that is, the intrinsic ZYX Euler
  angles in the order navigation users expect.

  [rollPitchYaw, info] = rollPitchYawFromRotation(R) also reports the
  convention used and which pages were at gimbal lock.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per angle set.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name            Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----            ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit       1x1    string   "rad"                  Unit of the output.
  Convention      1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense is read; the
                                                         sequence must be ZYX
                                                         intrinsic or empty.
  LockTolerance   1x1    double   1e-7                   Gimbal-lock threshold.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name           Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----           ----   ----     -----    -----------
  rollPitchYaw   3xN    double   rad|deg  Row 1 roll, row 2 pitch, row 3 yaw.
                                          Pitch lies in [-pi/2, pi/2]; roll and
                                          yaw lie in (-pi, pi].
  info           1x1    struct   -        As eulerSequenceFromRotation.

METHOD

The rows of eulerSequenceFromRotation's ZYX intrinsic output, reversed. See
  that function for the extraction itself; nothing is recomputed here.

  GIMBAL LOCK IS AT PITCH = PLUS OR MINUS 90 DEGREES, where the roll and yaw
  axes coincide and only their difference is determined. The convention there
  is yaw = 0 with the whole of the determined combination carried by roll, and
  one warning is raised per call stating how many pages were affected. A
  vehicle passing through the vertical therefore produces a warning and a
  discontinuity in roll and yaw, which is a property of the parameterisation
  and not of this code; integrate attitude as a quaternion or a matrix and
  convert for display only.

ACCURACY

Round trip to a few units in the last place away from the singularity,
  measured 8.9e-16 on the matrix and 4.4e-16 rad on the angles. Within the
  default 1e-7 threshold of vertical pitch, roll and yaw individually carry
  about 1e-9 rad while their difference and the rebuilt matrix stay exact.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rollPitchYawFromRotation:wrongMatrixSize
  referenceSystem:rollPitchYawFromRotation:conventionMismatch

WARNINGS

referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromRotation:gimbalLock

REFERENCES

P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.2.

EXAMPLE

rpy = referenceSystem.rollPitchYawFromRotation(R, 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

roll, pitch, yaw, heading, attitude, gimbal lock, ZYX, body frame

See also ROTATIONFROMROLLPITCHYAW, EULERSEQUENCEFROMROTATION, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

referenceSystem.rotationFromQuaternion

Rotation matrix of a unit quaternion.

SYNTAX

R = rotationFromQuaternion(q) returns the 3x3xN rotation of the 4xN
  quaternions.

  [R, convention] = rotationFromQuaternion(q) also returns the convention the
  matrices were built under.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q      4xN    double   -      Quaternions, one column each. Row order is set
                                by the convention: [q0; q1; q2; q3] for
                                scalarFirst, [q1; q2; q3; q0] for scalarLast.
                                The norm need not be exactly one; see METHOD.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense and .quaternion
                                                      are read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R            3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per column.
  convention   1x1      struct   -      The convention used.

METHOD

For the default frame (passive) sense and a unit quaternion,

      R = (q0^2 - v'v) I + 2 v v' - 2 q0 [v]x,   v = [q1; q2; q3],

  written out element by element so that no 3x3 temporary is formed per page.
  The "vector" (active) sense returns the transpose, which is the same
  expression with the sign of the [v]x term reversed. Sign check: the
  quaternion [cos(t/2); 0; 0; sin(t/2)] gives exactly rotationZ(t).

  THE QUATERNION IS NORMALISED ON ENTRY rather than assumed unit. Quaternions
  arriving from a file or from an integrator are typically unit to about
  1e-9, and a quaternion of norm 1+e produces a matrix scaled by (1+e)^2,
  which is not a rotation at all and whose transpose is not its inverse.
  Normalising costs one square root per page and removes the failure mode; the
  sign is left alone here, since it does not affect R.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision. Orthogonality residual max|R'R - I| measured
  2.6e-16 over random quaternions, and the round trip q -> R -> q reproduces
  the quaternion to 4.4e-16 including within 1e-8 rad of a 180 degree
  rotation. The double cover is exact: q and -q give bit-identical matrices,
  because every term is quadratic in q.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationFromQuaternion:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:rotationFromQuaternion:zeroQuaternion

REFERENCES

M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).
  J. Sola (2017), Quaternion Kinematics for the Error-State Kalman Filter,
  arXiv:1711.02508, Sect. 2.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationFromQuaternion(q);
  Raerospace = referenceSystem.rotationFromQuaternion(qJpl, ...
      'Convention', referenceSystem.attitudeConvention("aerospace"));

KEYWORDS

quaternion, rotation matrix, direction cosine matrix, attitude, Hamilton,
  double cover

See also QUATERNIONFROMROTATION, MULTIPLYQUATERNIONS, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

-------------------------------------------------------------------------
referenceSystem v2.1
Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-26 03:05 (CEST, UTC+02:00) - session S4, prompt P25

referenceSystem.quaternionFromRotation

Unit quaternion of a rotation, by Shepperd's method.

SYNTAX

q = quaternionFromRotation(R) returns the 4xN unit quaternions of the 3x3xN
  rotation, scalar first and with a non-negative scalar part.

  [q, convention] = quaternionFromRotation(R) also returns the convention the
  quaternions are expressed in, so that it can travel with them.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per quaternion.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense and .quaternion
                                                      are read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q            4xN    double   -      Unit quaternions. Row order is
                                      [q0; q1; q2; q3] for the scalarFirst
                                      convention and [q1; q2; q3; q0] for
                                      scalarLast. The sign is fixed by
                                      q0 >= 0.
  convention   1x1    struct   -      The convention used, for tagging.

METHOD

SHEPPERD'S METHOD: the four candidate denominators

      1 + tr(R),  1 + 2R11 - tr(R),  1 + 2R22 - tr(R),  1 + 2R33 - tr(R)

  sum to 4, so the largest is at least 1 and the component derived from it is
  at least 1/2. That component is taken from a square root and the other three
  from the off-diagonal differences and sums divided by it. The branch is
  chosen per page, so every page uses a well-conditioned divisor.

  The naive q0 = sqrt(1 + tr(R))/2 form is a special case of the first branch
  and is the reason the branching exists: at a rotation of 180 degrees the
  trace is -1, that branch divides by zero, and near 180 degrees it loses
  half the significant digits. Attitude data lives there after a large slew,
  so the branch is not a refinement.

  For the default frame (passive) sense the relations used are
  4*q0*q1 = R23 - R32, 4*q0*q2 = R31 - R13, 4*q0*q3 = R12 - R21, together with
  the symmetric 4*q1*q2 = R12 + R21, 4*q2*q3 = R23 + R32, 4*q3*q1 = R31 + R13.
  For the "vector" (active) sense the antisymmetric group changes sign, which
  is handled by transposing the input first: one set of formulae, one place to
  be wrong.

  THE SIGN IS FIXED, because q and -q are the same rotation but not the same
  number, and an unmanaged flip in the middle of a series destroys any
  interpolation through it. The rule is q0 >= 0, applied here and by
  normalizeQuaternion.

ACCURACY

Round trip R -> q -> R to a few units in the last place at every angle:
  measured 4.4e-16 over random rotations and 4.4e-16 within 1e-8 rad of a
  180 degree rotation, where the unbranched formula measures 1.5e-8 - eight
  digits lost, which is the whole reason for this implementation. The returned
  norm differs from unity by at most 2.2e-16.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:quaternionFromRotation:wrongMatrixSize

REFERENCES

S. W. Shepperd (1978), Quaternion from rotation matrix, Journal of Guidance
  and Control 1(3), 223-224.
  M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).

EXAMPLE

[q, c] = referenceSystem.quaternionFromRotation(R);
  tagged = struct('quaternion', q, 'convention', c);

KEYWORDS

quaternion, Shepperd, attitude, rotation matrix, Hamilton, scalar first

See also ROTATIONFROMQUATERNION, NORMALIZEQUATERNION, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

referenceSystem.rotationFromAxisAngle

Rotation matrix from an axis and an angle, Rodrigues.

SYNTAX

R = rotationFromAxisAngle(axis, angle) returns the 3x3xN rotation about each
  3xN axis through each of the N angles. The axis need not be a unit vector.

  [R, convention] = rotationFromAxisAngle(axis, angle) also returns the
  convention used.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size          Type     Units    Description
  ----    ----          ----     -----    -----------
  axis    3xN or 3x1    double   -        Rotation axes, one column each. A
                                          single column is used for every
                                          angle. Normalised internally.
  angle   1xN or 1x1    double   rad|deg  Rotation angles. A single angle is
                                          used for every axis.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                  "rad" or "deg".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R            3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page per pair.
  convention   1x1      struct   -      The convention used.

METHOD

Rodrigues' formula in the frame (passive) sense,

      R = cos(t) I + (1 - cos(t)) a a' - sin(t) [a]x,

  written out element by element. The "vector" (active) sense is the
  transpose, obtained by reversing the sign of the [a]x term. Sign check: the
  axis [0; 0; 1] gives exactly rotationZ(t).

  THE (1 - cos t) FACTOR IS EVALUATED AS 2 sin(t/2)^2 and never as 1 - cos(t).
  That is an identity, exact at every angle, and it removes the cancellation
  that costs about half the significant digits of that term near t = 0: at
  t = 1e-6 rad, 1 - cos(t) has an absolute error of about 1e-16 against a
  value of 5e-13, that is three digits, while 2 sin(t/2)^2 is correctly
  rounded.

  The handover asked for a series expansion below a threshold. It is not used,
  and the reason is recorded here rather than in a commit message: the
  half-angle identity is exact over the whole range, so it needs no threshold,
  no branch and no tuning, and a threshold that must be chosen is a parameter
  that can be chosen wrong. The series and the identity agree to 1e-16 for
  t below 1e-3 rad, which is the region the series was proposed for.

ACCURACY

Orthogonality residual max|R'R - I| measured 1.8e-15 over random axes and
  angles. The round trip axis-angle -> R -> axis-angle reproduces the angle to
  4.4e-16 rad and the axis direction to 5.6e-16 at angles from 1e-9 rad to
  pi - 1e-9 rad.

  At t = 0 exactly the rotation is the identity for every axis and the axis is
  not recoverable; that is a property of the parameterisation and is reported
  by axisAngleFromRotation, not here.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationFromAxisAngle:wrongAxisSize
  referenceSystem:rotationFromAxisAngle:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:rotationFromAxisAngle:zeroAxis

REFERENCES

O. Rodrigues (1840), Des lois geometriques qui regissent les deplacements
  d'un systeme solide, J. Math. Pures Appl. 5, 380-440.
  J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude, Stanford University, Sect. 4.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationFromAxisAngle([0; 0; 1], deg2rad(30));
  R = referenceSystem.rotationFromAxisAngle(rotationVector./vecnorm(rotationVector), ...
      vecnorm(rotationVector));

KEYWORDS

axis angle, Rodrigues, rotation vector, exponential map, attitude,
  small angle

See also AXISANGLEFROMROTATION, ROTATIONFROMQUATERNION, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

referenceSystem.axisAngleFromRotation

Rotation axis and angle of a rotation matrix.

SYNTAX

[axis, angle] = axisAngleFromRotation(R) returns the 3xN unit rotation axes
  and the 1xN angles in [0, pi] of the 3x3xN rotation.

  [axis, angle, convention] = axisAngleFromRotation(R) also returns the
  convention used.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page each.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                  Unit of the output angle.
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----    -----------
  axis         3xN    double   -        Unit rotation axes. At a zero rotation
                                        the axis is undefined and is reported
                                        as [0; 0; 1]; see ACCURACY.
  angle        1xN    double   rad|deg  Rotation angles in [0, pi]. Together
                                        with the axis sign this covers every
                                        rotation once.
  convention   1x1    struct   -        The convention used.

METHOD

THROUGH THE QUATERNION, not through the trace. The obvious route,
  angle = acos((tr(R) - 1)/2) with the axis from the antisymmetric part, is
  ill conditioned at both ends: acos has infinite slope at its limits, so a
  rotation near 0 or near pi loses half its digits, and at exactly pi the
  antisymmetric part vanishes and the axis is lost entirely.

  quaternionFromRotation already solves that problem with Shepperd's branch,
  so this function uses it and then reads off

      angle = 2*atan2(|v|, q0),      axis = v/|v|,

  which is well conditioned everywhere: atan2 of the two half-angle terms
  never differentiates a near-flat function. With the sign convention q0 >= 0
  the angle lands in [0, pi] automatically.

  Reusing the quaternion is also the reason there is no second Shepperd
  implementation in this file. The two-sources-of-truth pattern is cheaper to
  avoid than to detect.

ACCURACY

Round trip R -> axis-angle -> R reproduces the matrix to 4.4e-16 at angles
  from 1e-9 rad to pi - 1e-9 rad, and the angle itself to 4.4e-16 rad. The
  trace route measures 1.5e-8 rad of error at pi - 1e-8 rad, eight digits
  worse, which is what the quaternion route buys.

  AT A ZERO ROTATION THE AXIS IS GENUINELY UNDEFINED: every axis satisfies
  R = I. The angle returned is 0, which is exact, and the axis is reported as
  [0; 0; 1] rather than NaN, because a NaN axis would propagate into a
  rotation vector axis*angle that ought to be exactly zero. The convention is
  documented here and asserted in a contract test; a caller who needs to know
  should test the angle, which is the quantity that carries the information.

  THE any(turning) GUARD IS NOT DEFENSIVE PADDING. When no page is turning -
  the single-page identity being the obvious case - the logical index selects
  a 3x0 numerator and an empty denominator whose shapes do not conform, and
  the elementwise division raises rather than returning an empty result. The
  S4 run found this through axisAngleFromRotation(eye(3)), which is about as
  reachable a call as exists in this file. Note that it CANNOT occur when any
  page turns, so a mixed array hides it completely: the failing case is the
  uniform one, which is the reverse of the usual expectation.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:axisAngleFromRotation:wrongMatrixSize

REFERENCES

S. W. Shepperd (1978), Quaternion from rotation matrix, J. Guidance and
  Control 1(3), 223-224.
  J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude, Stanford University, Sect. 4.

EXAMPLE

[axis, angle] = referenceSystem.axisAngleFromRotation(R);
  rotationVector = axis.*angle;

KEYWORDS

axis angle, rotation vector, logarithmic map, attitude, Shepperd,
  conditioning

See also ROTATIONFROMAXISANGLE, QUATERNIONFROMROTATION, EULERSEQUENCEFROMROTATION.

referenceSystem.quaternionFromEulerSequence

Quaternion from an Euler angle sequence.

SYNTAX

q = quaternionFromEulerSequence(angles) returns the 4xN quaternions of the
  3xN Euler angles, without forming a rotation matrix.

  [q, convention] = quaternionFromEulerSequence(angles) also returns the
  convention used.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----     ----   ----     -----    -----------
  angles   3xN    double   rad|deg  Angles in the order the sequence names the
                                    axes, as for rotationFromEulerSequence.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"                  "rad" or "deg".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sequence, .rotationType,
                                                      .sense, .quaternion and
                                                      .product are read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q            4xN    double   -      Unit quaternions, scalar part >= 0, in
                                      the row order of the convention.
  convention   1x1    struct   -      The convention used.

METHOD

THE DIRECT PATH: each elementary rotation about axis i through angle a is
  the quaternion [cos(a/2); sin(a/2)*e_i], and the three are composed with
  multiplyQuaternions in the order the convention's product demands. No
  rotation matrix is built, so the cost is three sines, three cosines and two
  quaternion products instead of three 3x3 matrices and two page products.

  The composition order follows from the frame sense: with the Hamilton
  product a frame rotation composes as R(a*b) = R(b) R(a), so an extrinsic
  sequence (i,j,k) whose matrix is A_i A_j A_k is the quaternion
  q_k * q_j * q_i. The intrinsic case reverses the sequence and the angles, as
  in rotationFromEulerSequence, so the two functions cannot disagree about
  what a sequence means.

  The elementary quaternions are built here and composed through the sibling
  product, which is why the four combinations of sense and product need no
  separate code: the product function already knows them.

ACCURACY

Agrees with the matrix route quaternionFromRotation(rotationFromEuler...)
  to 5.0e-16 over 4000 random angle sets and all twelve sequences, which is
  asserted as a precision test rather than assumed. Half-angle arguments are
  exact, so there is no accuracy penalty near any singularity: gimbal lock is
  a property of extracting angles, not of building from them.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:quaternionFromEulerSequence:wrongAngleSize
  referenceSystem:quaternionFromEulerSequence:sequenceRequired

REFERENCES

J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude, Stanford University, Sect. 5.
  M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).

EXAMPLE

q = referenceSystem.quaternionFromEulerSequence([yaw; pitch; roll]);

KEYWORDS

quaternion, Euler angles, sequence, intrinsic, extrinsic, attitude,
  direct conversion

See also EULERSEQUENCEFROMQUATERNION, ROTATIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, MULTIPLYQUATERNIONS.

referenceSystem.eulerSequenceFromQuaternion

Euler angles of a quaternion, all twelve sequences.

SYNTAX

angles = eulerSequenceFromQuaternion(q) extracts the 3xN Euler angles of the
  4xN quaternions for the sequence named by the convention.

  [angles, info] = eulerSequenceFromQuaternion(q) also reports the convention
  used and which quaternions were at gimbal lock.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q      4xN    double   -      Quaternions, one column each, in the row order
                                named by the convention.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name            Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----            ----   ----     -------                -----------
  AngleUnit       1x1    string   "rad"                  Unit of the output.
  Convention      1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sequence,
                                                         .rotationType, .sense,
                                                         .quaternion.
  LockTolerance   1x1    double   1e-7                   Gimbal-lock threshold.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----     ----   ----     -----    -----------
  angles   3xN    double   rad|deg  Angles in the order the sequence names the
                                    axes, ranges as eulerSequenceFromRotation.
  info     1x1    struct   -        As eulerSequenceFromRotation.

METHOD

The quaternion is turned into a rotation matrix and the angles are extracted
  from it by eulerSequenceFromRotation. THE ROUND TRIP IS DELIBERATE AND IT IS
  EXACT: a matrix built from a unit quaternion is orthogonal to 2.6e-16, and
  the extraction is a ratio of matrix elements, so nothing is lost.

  The alternative - extracting the angles from the quaternion components
  directly - would need its own copy of the twelve-sequence index and parity
  logic, and that logic is the part of this family that is hard to get right:
  the Python cross-check written before this code found a sign error in one
  branch of it. A second copy would be a second place for that error to live,
  which is the two-sources-of-truth pattern (findings M-2, C-2, decision D27).
  The nine multiplications saved are not worth it. quaternionFromEulerSequence
  does take the direct path in the other direction, because building is short
  and shares no logic with the extraction.

ACCURACY

Agrees with the matrix route to 4.4e-16 rad, which is the accuracy of the
  extraction itself; see eulerSequenceFromRotation for the conditioning near
  gimbal lock and for what the reported lock means.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromQuaternion:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromQuaternion:sequenceRequired

WARNINGS

referenceSystem:eulerSequenceFromRotation:gimbalLock

REFERENCES

E. Bernardes and S. Viollet (2022), Quaternion to Euler angles conversion,
  PLoS ONE 17(11), e0276302.
  J. Diebel (2006), Representing Attitude, Stanford University, Sect. 5.

EXAMPLE

angles = referenceSystem.eulerSequenceFromQuaternion(q, 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

quaternion, Euler angles, extraction, gimbal lock, attitude, sequence

See also QUATERNIONFROMEULERSEQUENCE, EULERSEQUENCEFROMROTATION, ROTATIONFROMQUATERNION.

referenceSystem.multiplyQuaternions

Product of two quaternion series, conventions compared.

SYNTAX

q = multiplyQuaternions(first, second) returns the 4xN quaternion product.
  Each operand is either a 4xN array or a tagged struct with fields
  .quaternion and .convention; when both carry a convention the two are
  compared and a mismatch is an error, never a silent composition.

  [q, convention] = multiplyQuaternions(first, second) also returns the
  convention the product is expressed in.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name     Size        Type           Units  Description
  ----     ----        ----           -----  -----------
  first    4xN or 1x1  double|struct  -      Left operand. A struct must have
                                             .quaternion (4xN) and .convention
                                             (1x1 struct).
  second   4xN or 1x1  double|struct  -      Right operand, same forms. One
                                             operand may be 4x1 and is then
                                             applied to every column of the
                                             other.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------   -----------
  Convention   1x1    struct   absent    Convention for untagged operands. If
                                         omitted it is taken from the tagged
                                         operands, or defaults to
                                         attitudeConvention() when neither is
                                         tagged.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q            4xN    double   -      Product, unit norm, scalar part >= 0.
  convention   1x1    struct   -      The convention used, for tagging.

METHOD

With a = [a0; av] and b = [b0; bv], both scalar first,

      Hamilton   a*b = [a0*b0 - av.bv ;  a0*bv + b0*av + av x bv]
      Shuster    a*b = [a0*b0 - av.bv ;  a0*bv + b0*av - av x bv]

  The two differ only in the sign of the cross term, and the Shuster (JPL)
  product is the Hamilton product with the operands exchanged. THIS IS WHY THE
  COMPOSITION ORDER DIFFERS, and it is the single most common error in
  attitude software:

      sense       product     matrix of the product
      -----       -------     ---------------------
      frame       hamilton    R(a*b) = R(b) R(a)
      frame       shuster     R(a*b) = R(a) R(b)
      vector      hamilton    R(a*b) = R(a) R(b)
      vector      shuster     R(a*b) = R(b) R(a)

  All four rows are asserted as precision tests. Nothing here can detect a
  caller who wanted the other order: every one of the four is a valid rotation
  and none is distinguishable from the result.

  CONVENTION COMPARISON IS THE STRUCTURAL PROTECTION (decision D35), not the
  flag. The fields compared are .quaternion, .product, .sense and .localFrame,
  which are the four that change the answer. The .sequence and .rotationType
  fields are deliberately NOT compared: they do not enter a quaternion product
  at all, and two quaternions legitimately derived through different Euler
  sequences are the same kind of object.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision, sixteen products and twelve sums per pair. The
  result is renormalised, so composing a long chain does not drift off the
  unit sphere: over 10000 successive products the norm stays within 4.4e-16 of
  one, against 2e-12 of accumulated drift without the renormalisation.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:multiplyQuaternions:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:multiplyQuaternions:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:multiplyQuaternions:conventionMismatch
  referenceSystem:multiplyQuaternions:malformedOperand

REFERENCES

M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).
  J. Sola (2017), Quaternion Kinematics for the Error-State Kalman Filter,
  arXiv:1711.02508, Sect. 1.4 on the Hamilton and JPL conventions.

EXAMPLE

q = referenceSystem.multiplyQuaternions(qa, qb);

  [qa, ca] = referenceSystem.quaternionFromRotation(Ra);
  taggedA = struct('quaternion', qa, 'convention', ca);
  q = referenceSystem.multiplyQuaternions(taggedA, taggedB);

KEYWORDS

quaternion, product, Hamilton, Shuster, JPL, composition, attitude,
  convention mismatch

See also INVERTQUATERNION, NORMALIZEQUATERNION, ATTITUDECONVENTION.

referenceSystem.invertQuaternion

Inverse of a unit quaternion, its conjugate.

SYNTAX

q = invertQuaternion(q) returns the inverse of each of the 4xN quaternions,
  that is the quaternion of the inverse rotation.

  [q, convention] = invertQuaternion(q) also returns the convention.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q      4xN    double   -      Quaternions, one column each, in the row order
                                named by the convention.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .quaternion is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q            4xN    double   -      Inverse quaternions, unit norm, scalar
                                      part non-negative.
  convention   1x1    struct   -      The convention used.

METHOD

For a unit quaternion the inverse is the conjugate: the scalar part is kept
  and the vector part is negated. The input is normalised first, so a
  quaternion that has drifted off the unit sphere gets its true inverse
  q*/|q|^2 rather than a conjugate that is only nearly an inverse.

  THE CONJUGATE IS THE SAME UNDER BOTH PRODUCTS. Hamilton and Shuster differ
  in the sign of the cross-product term of the product, not in the conjugate,
  so this function needs no product branch - which is worth stating because it
  is the one place in this family where the two conventions agree and a reader
  expecting a branch will look for a missing one.

  Negating the vector part flips the sign convention when the scalar part is
  zero, so normalizeQuaternion's tie-break is applied again on the way out and
  the result is always in canonical form.

ACCURACY

Exact: negation and division by a norm. The round trip
  q -> inverse -> inverse reproduces the input to 2.2e-16, and
  multiplyQuaternions(q, invertQuaternion(q)) gives the identity quaternion
  to 2.8e-16.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:invertQuaternion:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:invertQuaternion:zeroQuaternion

REFERENCES

M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).

EXAMPLE

qBodyToNav = referenceSystem.invertQuaternion(qNavToBody);

KEYWORDS

quaternion, inverse, conjugate, attitude, Hamilton, Shuster

See also MULTIPLYQUATERNIONS, NORMALIZEQUATERNION, INVERTROTATION.

referenceSystem.normalizeQuaternion

Unit norm and a consistent sign for a quaternion series.

SYNTAX

q = normalizeQuaternion(q) scales each of the 4xN quaternions to unit norm
  and fixes the sign so that the scalar part is non-negative.

  [q, convention] = normalizeQuaternion(q) also returns the convention.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q      4xN    double   -      Quaternions, one column each, in the row order
                                named by the convention.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .quaternion is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----         ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q            4xN    double   -      Unit quaternions with scalar part >= 0.
  convention   1x1    struct   -      The convention used.

METHOD

Two separate jobs, both of which have to be done somewhere and neither of
  which is arithmetic on the rotation.

  UNIT NORM. A quaternion drifts off the unit sphere through integration or
  through interpolation. The rotation it then represents is scaled by the
  square of the norm, so the matrix is no longer orthogonal and its transpose
  is no longer its inverse.

  CONSISTENT SIGN. q and -q are the same rotation, so nothing downstream that
  only builds a matrix can tell them apart - but everything that treats the
  quaternion as a point in R4 can. A sign flip in the middle of a series puts
  a 180 degree discontinuity into any interpolation, any difference and any
  filter innovation, and it is invisible in the attitude itself. The rule
  applied here is q0 >= 0, the same rule quaternionFromRotation applies, so
  the two agree by construction.

  The rule cannot fix a series that is already discontinuous in a different
  way; it makes every quaternion lie in the same half of the sphere, which is
  what an interpolator needs. It is applied per column and is exact: negating
  a double changes only the sign bit.

ACCURACY

The norm after scaling differs from unity by at most one unit in the last
  place, measured 2.2e-16. The direction is unchanged to the same order; the
  rotation the quaternion represents is not touched.

  A quaternion with q0 exactly zero is a rotation of exactly 180 degrees and
  is the one case where the sign rule cannot choose. The tie is broken on the
  first nonzero component in the order q1, q2, q3, which is documented rather
  than arbitrary so that two callers agree.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:normalizeQuaternion:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:normalizeQuaternion:zeroQuaternion

REFERENCES

M. D. Shuster (1993), A Survey of Attitude Representations, JAS 41(4).

EXAMPLE

q = referenceSystem.normalizeQuaternion(qFromFile);

KEYWORDS

quaternion, normalisation, unit norm, sign convention, double cover,
  attitude series

See also QUATERNIONFROMROTATION, INVERTQUATERNION, MULTIPLYQUATERNIONS.

L5 - Orbit, attitude and navigation

referenceSystem.rotationGcrsToRsw

Inertial to orbit frame rotation from a satellite state.

SYNTAX

R = rotationGcrsToRsw(r, v) returns the 3x3xN rotation from the geocentric
  celestial frame to the radial / along-track / cross-track frame defined by
  each of the N inertial states.

  R = rotationGcrsToRsw(r, v, 'Frame', "LVLH") returns the local-vertical
  local-horizontal frame instead; "NTW" gives the velocity-aligned frame.

  [R, info] = rotationGcrsToRsw(r, v) also returns what each row means and
  how far the state is from the degenerate radial case.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  r      3xN    double   m      Inertial position, one state per COLUMN. The
                                column layout is L2's and applyRotation's, not
                                L4's row layout (decision D65).
  v      3xN    double   m/s    Inertial velocity, one state per column. A
                                single column is applied to every position.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name    Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----    ----   ----     -------   -----------
  Frame   1x1    string   "RSW"     "RSW", "LVLH" or "NTW".

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                 Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----                 ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R                    3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices. Row k holds
                                                the k-th axis of the orbit
                                                frame in celestial components.
  info                 1x1      struct   -      Diagnostics.
  .frame               1x1      string   -      The frame that was built.
  .rowAxis             1x3      string   -      What each row of R is.
  .sineOfPlaneAngle    1xN      double   -      |r x v| / (|r| |v|), the margin
                                                against the degenerate case.
                                                1 for a circular orbit.

METHOD

The GCRS is the geocentric celestial reference system, commonly called ECI;
  the earth-fixed frame commonly called ECEF is the ITRS and is not involved
  here. This function makes no statement about how r and v were obtained: it
  builds an orthonormal triad from the state it is given, so the frame it
  produces is inertial exactly to the extent that its input is.

  Three unit vectors are formed from the state, and all three frames are
  permutations and sign changes of them:

      radial      = r / |r|
      crossTrack  = (r x v) / |r x v|      the orbit normal, along h
      alongTrack  = crossTrack x radial    in plane, in the sense of motion
      tangential  = v / |v|

  ALONGTRACK IS NOT THE VELOCITY DIRECTION except on a circular orbit. It is
  the in-plane direction perpendicular to the radius, and it differs from the
  velocity by the flight path angle, which reaches 4.7 degrees at e = 0.1 and
  is zero only at apsis. Choosing RSW when NTW was meant is therefore a small,
  plausible and entirely silent error, which is why the two frames are named
  options and not a default.

  Row layout of the three frames:

      frame   row 1        row 2        row 3        convention
      -----   -----        -----        -----        ----------
      RSW     radial       alongTrack   crossTrack   Vallado; also called RTN
      LVLH    alongTrack  -crossTrack  -radial       x forward, z nadir
      NTW     normal       tangential   crossTrack   y along velocity

  with normal = tangential x crossTrack, which equals radial on a circular
  orbit. Every row triple is right handed and every matrix has determinant +1;
  the sign changes in LVLH come in pairs for exactly that reason.

  R is a frame rotation in the sense of attitudeConvention: it re-expresses a
  celestial vector in orbit-frame components, x_rsw = R * x_gcrs. Apply it
  with applyRotation, and invert it with invertRotation, like any other
  rotation in this toolbox.

  DEGENERACY. r x v vanishes when the velocity is purely radial, which is a
  rectilinear trajectory rather than an orbit, and the cross-track direction
  is then undefined rather than merely ill conditioned. The function errors
  instead of returning a plausible triad built on rounding noise. The margin
  is reported in info.sineOfPlaneAngle so a caller integrating towards that
  condition can see it coming.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision: three normalisations and two cross products, no
  series and no iteration. Measured against an independent Python
  implementation over the Vallado test state, max|R'R - I| = 3.3e-16 and
  det(R) - 1 = 0 for all three frames. The residual is that of the
  normalisation, one to two units in the last place.

  The accuracy of the FRAME, as opposed to the matrix, is that of the state.
  A 1 mm error in r at 7000 km tilts the radial axis by 1.4e-10 rad, which
  moves a point on the satellite by 0.1 nm; a 1 mm/s velocity error tilts the
  orbit normal by about 1.3e-7 rad near a 7.5 km/s speed.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationGcrsToRsw:wrongStateSize
  referenceSystem:rotationGcrsToRsw:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:rotationGcrsToRsw:zeroState
  referenceSystem:rotationGcrsToRsw:degenerateOrbitPlane

REFERENCES

D. A. Vallado (2013), Fundamentals of Astrodynamics and Applications,
  4th ed., Sect. 3.4, the RSW and NTW satellite coordinate systems.
  O. Montenbruck and E. Gill (2000), Satellite Orbits, Sect. 7.1.
  GRACE-FO Level-1 Data Product User Handbook, JPL D-56935, on the
  line-of-sight and orbit frames used for the KBR and accelerometer products.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationGcrsToRsw(rGcrs, vGcrs);
  residualRsw = referenceSystem.applyRotation(R, rGcrs - rReference);

KEYWORDS

RSW, RTN, LVLH, NTW, orbit frame, radial, along-track, cross-track, nadir,
  GCRS, ECI, GRACE, satellite

See also APPLYROTATION, INVERTROTATION, ROTATIONITRSTOGCRS, INTERPOLATEQUATERNIONS.

referenceSystem.interpolateQuaternions

Interpolate an attitude series to a vector of epochs.

SYNTAX

q = interpolateQuaternions(nodeQuaternion, nodeEpoch, queryEpoch)
  interpolates the 4xM attitude series tagged by the M node epochs to the N
  query epochs, by spherical linear interpolation.

  q = interpolateQuaternions(..., 'Method', "squad") uses the cubic SQUAD
  interpolant instead, which is continuous in the angular rate at the nodes.

  [q, convention, info] = interpolateQuaternions(...) also returns the
  convention the result is expressed in and what the interpolation did.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name             Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----             ----   ----     -----  -----------
  nodeQuaternion   4xM    double   -      Attitude at the nodes, one column
                                          each, rows ordered by the convention.
                                          Need not be of unit norm.
  nodeEpoch        1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct holding M epochs,
                                          strictly increasing.
  queryEpoch       1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct holding N epochs in the
                                          SAME time scale, each inside the node
                                          range.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Method       1x1    string   "slerp"                "slerp" or "squad".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   Only .quaternion is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                   Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                   ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q                      4xN    double   -      Interpolated unit quaternions,
                                                SIGN CONTINUOUS along the
                                                series, not forced to q0 >= 0.
  convention             1x1    struct   -      The convention used.
  info                   1x1    struct   -      Diagnostics.
  .method                1x1    string   -      The method that ran.
  .nodeCount             1x1    double   -      M.
  .signFlipCount         1x1    double   -      Nodes whose sign was reversed
                                                to make the series continuous.
  .maximumNodeAngle      1x1    double   rad    Largest rotation between two
                                                consecutive nodes.
  .linearFallbackCount   1x1    double   -      Query points whose bracketing
                                                pair was too close to separate,
                                                evaluated by normalised linear
                                                interpolation instead.

METHOD

SIGN CONTINUITY FIRST, INTERPOLATION SECOND. A quaternion and its negative
  are the same rotation, so a series read from a file or produced by an
  estimator may flip sign between one record and the next with no change in
  attitude whatsoever. Interpolating across such a flip sends the result the
  long way round the sphere - up to 360 degrees of entirely fictitious motion -
  while every individual node remains correct. The series is therefore made
  continuous before anything else happens: with s(1) = 1 and
  s(k) = s(k-1)*sign(q(:,k)'*q(:,k-1)), each node is multiplied by its s(k).
  The rule is cumulative, exact (it changes only sign bits) and vectorised as
  a cumulative product. info.signFlipCount reports how many nodes it moved.

  THE OUTPUT IS NOT PUT INTO THE q0 >= 0 CANONICAL FORM, and that is a
  deliberate departure from normalizeQuaternion's rule. Canonicalising the
  output would reintroduce exactly the discontinuity just removed, at every
  point where the series passes through a 180 degree rotation. The unit norm
  still comes from normalizeQuaternion, so there is one implementation of it;
  the sign it imposes is then undone where it disagrees with the interpolated
  value. Apply normalizeQuaternion afterwards if the canonical form is wanted.

  SLERP. With cos(w) = q_a'*q_b and u the fractional position in the interval,

      q(u) = (sin((1-u)w)*q_a + sin(u*w)*q_b) / sin(w)

  which is the constant-rate great-circle path. It is exact at u = 0 and
  u = 1: sin(w)/sin(w) is one and sin(0) is zero, so the nodes come back
  unchanged. Where the pair is closer than 1e-6 rad the quotient loses
  significance and normalised linear interpolation is used instead, which
  agrees with the great circle to O(w^2) and is therefore better there than
  the formula it replaces; the count is reported.

  SQUAD. SLERP is only C0: the angular rate jumps at every node, which is
  invisible in the attitude and obvious the moment the series is
  differentiated for a rate. SQUAD adds the Shoemake control points

      s_i = q_i (x) exp( -( log(q_i^-1 (x) q_i+1) + log(q_i^-1 (x) q_i-1) )/4 )

  with s_1 = q_1 and s_M = q_M, and evaluates

      squad(u) = slerp( slerp(q_i, q_i+1, u), slerp(s_i, s_i+1, u), 2u(1-u) )

  The nodes are still reproduced exactly, because u = 0 and u = 1 make the
  blend weight 2u(1-u) vanish.

  ONLY .quaternion OF THE CONVENTION ENTERS. Interpolation is a geometric
  operation on the unit 3-sphere, so it depends on which row carries the
  scalar and on nothing else: .sense does not change a geodesic, and .product
  cancels out of the control-point construction, which is symmetric under the
  operand reversal that distinguishes Hamilton from Shuster. That is measured,
  not assumed - the two constructions were computed independently and agree to
  5.6e-17. The quaternion products are therefore taken through
  multiplyQuaternions with a fixed scalar-first Hamilton convention, and the
  rows are reordered at entry and exit if the caller's convention is
  scalar last.

  The exponential and logarithm of a unit quaternion are local functions here.
  They are the exponential map of the rotation group rather than quaternion
  algebra, they exist in no L4b function, and they are used by nothing but
  SQUAD (decision D73).

  TIME. The interval fractions come from secondsBetween, so the node and query
  epochs must share a time scale and the leap-second rule of that function
  applies unchanged: a UTC or GLONASS series that CROSSES a leap second is
  refused rather than silently mis-spaced by one second. Convert the series to
  TAI, TT or GPS first, which is what an attitude product should be tagged
  with in any case.

ACCURACY

At the nodes the result reproduces the input to 1.1e-16, which is the
  renormalisation of an already-unit quaternion and not an interpolation
  error. Between them the error is that of the interpolant against whatever
  the true motion was, and no interpolator can do better than the sampling:
  measured against a smoothly turning reference sampled every second over
  eight seconds, the worst deviation is 1.2e-2 rad for SLERP and 7.4e-3 rad
  for SQUAD.

  THE DIFFERENCE THAT MATTERS IS IN THE RATE, NOT IN THE ATTITUDE. On the same
  case the jump in the numerical derivative across an interior node measures
  3.9e-2 for SLERP and 1.3e-6 for SQUAD, a factor of 3e4. A series
  differentiated for angular rate will show the SLERP corners as rate spikes
  at exactly the node spacing, and they are easy to mistake for a sensor
  artefact.

  SQUAD's control points are the classical uniform-spacing construction. With
  markedly non-uniform nodes the curve still passes through every node exactly
  and stays C0, but the rate continuity degrades in proportion to the spacing
  ratio. Nothing here detects that; keep the sampling regular if the rate is
  going to be used.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:interpolateQuaternions:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:interpolateQuaternions:tooFewNodes
  referenceSystem:interpolateQuaternions:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:interpolateQuaternions:nodesNotIncreasing
  referenceSystem:interpolateQuaternions:queryOutOfRange
  referenceSystem:secondsBetween:scaleMismatch
      Raised on this function's behalf when the node and query epochs are in
      different time scales.
  referenceSystem:addSeconds:leapSecondCrossed
      Raised on this function's behalf for a leap-bearing scale whose span
      crosses a leap second.

REFERENCES

K. Shoemake (1985), Animating Rotation with Quaternion Curves, SIGGRAPH
  Computer Graphics 19(3), 245-254.
  E. B. Dam, M. Koch and M. Lillholm (1998), Quaternions, Interpolation and
  Animation, University of Copenhagen DIKU TR-98/5, Sect. 6 on SQUAD.
  GRACE-FO Level-1 Data Product User Handbook, JPL D-56935, on the SCA1B
  star-camera attitude product this function is shaped for.

EXAMPLE

sca = referenceSystem.mjdToEpoch(mjdOfRecord, 'GPS');
  kbr = referenceSystem.mjdToEpoch(mjdOfSample, 'GPS');
  q = referenceSystem.interpolateQuaternions(qSca1b, sca, kbr, ...
      'Method', "squad");

KEYWORDS

quaternion, attitude, interpolation, SLERP, SQUAD, sign continuity, double
  cover, GRACE, SCA1B, star camera

See also NORMALIZEQUATERNION, MULTIPLYQUATERNIONS, INVERTQUATERNION, SECONDSBETWEEN.

referenceSystem.rotationNavToBody

Navigation-frame to body-frame rotation from roll, pitch and yaw.

SYNTAX

R = rotationNavToBody(rollPitchYaw, 'Convention', convention) returns the
  3x3xN rotation from the local navigation frame to the body frame. The
  convention is REQUIRED and has no default, because the ENU-versus-NED
  choice changes what pitch and yaw MEAN, not merely their sign.

  [R, info] = rotationNavToBody(...) also returns which frames the rotation
  runs between and the local-level frame the angles were read against.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name           Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----           ----   ----     -----    -----------
  rollPitchYaw   3xN    double   rad|deg  Row 1 roll (about the body x axis),
                                          row 2 pitch (y), row 3 yaw (z), one
                                          attitude per COLUMN. The order is
                                          the reverse of the ZYX sequence
                                          order, as in
                                          rotationFromRollPitchYaw.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default   Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------   -----------
  Convention   1x1    struct   none      REQUIRED. .localFrame, .sequence,
                                         .rotationType and .sense are read.
  AngleUnit    1x1    string   "rad"     "rad" or "deg".

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name          Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----          ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R             3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page each.
  info          1x1      struct   -      Diagnostics.
  .fromFrame    1x1      string   -      "NAV".
  .toFrame      1x1      string   -      "BODY".
  .localFrame   1x1      string   -      "ENU" or "NED", from the convention.
  .angleOrder   1x3      string   -      What each input row is.

METHOD

THE DELIVERABLE IS THE NAMING, NOT THE ARITHMETIC. Roll, pitch and yaw are
  the intrinsic ZYX Euler angles by definition, so this function delegates
  wholly to rotationFromRollPitchYaw and adds only the statement of which two
  frames the result runs between. That statement is what a navigation user
  needs and what a bare Euler-angle function cannot give: NAV and BODY are
  frames whose realization the caller supplies (a body frame is defined by
  its platform, a nav frame by its origin), so the toolbox validates the
  names and requires the defining parameters explicitly rather than
  defaulting them (section B.3).

  WHY THE CONVENTION IS REQUIRED HERE AND OPTIONAL ELSEWHERE. Under ENU the
  third axis is Up and yaw increases from East towards North; under NED it is
  Down and yaw is the familiar heading clockwise from North. The two differ
  by more than a sign: the same three numbers describe two different
  attitudes, both orthogonal, both plausible, and nothing at runtime
  distinguishes them. That is decision D35's argument at its sharpest, so
  this is the one place the default is withdrawn. Pass
  attitudeConvention("aerospace") for NED, attitudeConvention("geodetic")
  for ENU, and say which you mean.

  The earth-fixed frame underlying the nav frame is whichever the caller
  supplied to rotationEcefToEnu or rotationEcefToNed - rigorously the ITRS if
  it came from rotationItrsToGcrs, and the GCRS is not involved at all. L4
  and L5 make no frame commitment of their own (section C.7).

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision; the accuracy is that of rotationFromRollPitchYaw,
  whose orthogonality residual max|R'R - I| measures 4.4e-16. Verified against
  an independent Python implementation at roll 10, pitch 20, yaw 30 degrees,
  intrinsic ZYX in the frame sense, agreeing to 2.2e-16 elementwise with
  determinant 1 to one unit in the last place.

  This function performs no arithmetic of its own, so it adds no error.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:rotationNavToBody:conventionRequired
  referenceSystem:rotationNavToBody:localFrameRequired
  referenceSystem:rotationNavToBody:wrongAngleSize

REFERENCES

D. H. Titterton and J. L. Weston (2004), Strapdown Inertial Navigation
  Technology, 2nd ed., Sect. 3.5 and 3.6.
  P. D. Groves (2013), Principles of GNSS, Inertial and Multisensor
  Integrated Navigation Systems, 2nd ed., Sect. 2.2.

EXAMPLE

convention = referenceSystem.attitudeConvention("aerospace");
  R = referenceSystem.rotationNavToBody([10; 20; 30], ...
      'Convention', convention, 'AngleUnit', "deg");

KEYWORDS

strapdown, attitude, roll, pitch, yaw, heading, body frame, navigation
  frame, NED, ENU, inertial navigation

See also ROTATIONFROMROLLPITCHYAW, ROTATIONECEFTONED, ROTATIONECEFTOENU,
  ANGULARVELOCITYTOROTATIONRATE.

referenceSystem.angularVelocityToRotationRate

Rotation rate from a body-relative-to-navigation angular velocity.

SYNTAX

Rdot = angularVelocityToRotationRate(R, omega) returns the time derivative
  of the 3x3xN rotation R for the angular velocity of the BODY frame relative
  to the NAVIGATION frame, RESOLVED IN THE BODY FRAME. Which frame omega is
  resolved in is the single most common error in strapdown code, so it is
  named here and not left to the reader.

  Rdot = angularVelocityToRotationRate(R, omega, 'Resolution', "reference")
  takes omega resolved in the navigation frame instead.

  [Rdot, qdot] = angularVelocityToRotationRate(R, omega) also returns the
  quaternion rate of the same motion. It costs one quaternion extraction and
  one product, so it is computed only when it is asked for.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name    Size          Type     Units  Description
  ----    ----          ----     -----  -----------
  R       3x3xN         double   -      Rotation matrices, one page each. Read
                                        in the sense the convention names.
  omega   3xN or 3x1    double   rad/s  Angular velocity, one column each. A
                                        single column applies to every page.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Resolution   1x1    string   "body"                 "body" or "reference":
                                                      the frame omega is
                                                      resolved in.
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense and .quaternion
                                                      are read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name         Size     Type     Units    Description
  ----         ----     ----     -----    -----------
  Rdot         3x3xN    double   1/s      Time derivative of R.
  qdot         4xN      double   1/s      Time derivative of the quaternion of
                                          R, in the convention's row order.
  convention   1x1      struct   -        The convention used.

METHOD

R is the frame (passive) rotation from the navigation frame to the body
  frame, x_body = R * x_nav, and omega is the angular velocity of the body
  relative to the navigation frame. With Omega the skew-symmetric matrix of
  omega, Omega*u = cross(omega, u),

      resolution   Rdot            because
      ----------   ----            -------
      body         -Omega * R      omega is expressed in the frame R maps TO
      reference    -R * Omega      [R*w]x = R [w]x R' for a proper rotation

  Both satisfy Rdot' * R + R' * Rdot = 0 identically, which is the same
  invariant asserted for rotationItrsToGcrs in P9 and the cheapest available
  check that omega is resolved in the frame this help text claims.

  THE TWO SENSES DIFFER BY A TRANSPOSE AND NOTHING ELSE. The vector (active)
  matrix is the transpose of the frame one, so its derivative is the transpose
  of the frame derivative: for a body-resolved omega the vector-sense relation
  reads Rdot = R * Omega, which is the form quoted in most navigation texts
  and the reason two texts can print formulae of opposite sign and both be
  right. omega itself is untouched by the choice, because omega is a physical
  rate and not a matrix convention.

  QUATERNION FORM. With the Hamilton product and the scalar-first order,

      qdot = 0.5 * q (x) [0; omega]        omega resolved in the body frame

  and the quaternion is obtained from quaternionFromRotation and the product
  from multiplyQuaternions, never rebuilt here (seam item 5): the quaternion
  algebra of this toolbox lives in L4b and L5 consumes it.

  For a reference-resolved omega the quaternion rule becomes
  qdot = 0.5 * [0; omega] (x) q, the operands exchanged, which is the same
  left-versus-right distinction as the matrix form above.

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision: one cross-product matrix and one page product.
  Measured against an independent Python implementation on a closed-form
  trajectory, |Rdot - analytic| = 2.3e-10 against a numerical derivative of
  step 1e-6 s, which is the step's own truncation and not this function's
  error; the exact invariant |Rdot'R + R'Rdot| measures 0 to the last bit.
  The round trip omega -> Rdot -> omega through angularVelocityFromRotationRate
  reproduces omega to 1.4e-17 rad/s.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:angularVelocityToRotationRate:wrongRotationSize
  referenceSystem:angularVelocityToRotationRate:wrongVelocitySize
  referenceSystem:angularVelocityToRotationRate:sizeMismatch

REFERENCES

D. H. Titterton and J. L. Weston (2004), Strapdown Inertial Navigation
  Technology, 2nd ed., Sect. 3.6 and 11.2.
  P. G. Savage (1998), Strapdown Inertial Navigation Integration Algorithm
  Design Part 1: Attitude Algorithms, J. Guidance 21(1), 19-28.

EXAMPLE

R = referenceSystem.rotationNavToBody([0.01; 0.02; 0.03], ...
      'Convention', referenceSystem.attitudeConvention("aerospace"));
  Rdot = referenceSystem.angularVelocityToRotationRate(R, [0.1; 0; 0], ...
      'Convention', referenceSystem.attitudeConvention("aerospace"));

KEYWORDS

strapdown, gyro, angular velocity, kinematics, attitude, rotation rate,
  skew symmetric, body frame, navigation frame, IMU

See also ANGULARVELOCITYFROMROTATIONRATE, INTEGRATEATTITUDE, ROTATIONNAVTOBODY.

Developed by Matthias Weigelt with the assistance of Claude Opus 5 (Anthropic)
Created: 2026-07-26 22:15 (CEST, UTC+02:00) - session S5, prompt P26

referenceSystem.angularVelocityFromRotationRate

Body-relative-to-navigation angular velocity from a rotation rate.

SYNTAX

omega = angularVelocityFromRotationRate(R, Rdot) returns the angular
  velocity of the BODY frame relative to the NAVIGATION frame, RESOLVED IN
  THE BODY FRAME, that produces the given rate of the 3x3xN rotation R.

  omega = angularVelocityFromRotationRate(R, Rdot, 'Resolution', "reference")
  resolves it in the navigation frame instead.

  [omega, info] = angularVelocityFromRotationRate(R, Rdot) also returns how
  far the supplied pair was from a consistent rotation and rate.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name   Size     Type     Units  Description
  ----   ----     ----     -----  -----------
  R      3x3xN    double   -      Rotation matrices, one page each.
  Rdot   3x3xN    double   1/s    Time derivative of R, one page each. A
                                  single page applies to every rotation.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name         Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----         ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Resolution   1x1    string   "body"                 "body" or "reference".
  Convention   1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .sense is read.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                   Size   Type     Units    Description
  ----                   ----   ----     -----    -----------
  omega                  3xN    double   rad/s    Angular velocity, one column
                                                  per page.
  info                   1x1    struct   -        Diagnostics.
  .resolution            1x1    string   -        The frame omega is in.
  .symmetricResidual     1xN    double   1/s      Norm of the symmetric part
                                                  of Rdot*R', which is zero
                                                  for a consistent pair and
                                                  is DISCARDED, not used.
  .orthogonalityResidual 1xN    double   -        max|R'R - I| per page.
  convention             1x1    struct   -        The convention used.

METHOD

Inverting the relation of angularVelocityToRotationRate. With Omega the
  skew-symmetric matrix of omega and the frame (passive) sense,

      resolution   from            Omega recovered as
      ----------   ----            ------------------
      body         Rdot = -Omega R  -Rdot * R'
      reference    Rdot = -R Omega  -R' * Rdot

  and omega is read out of the skew matrix as [S(3,2); S(1,3); S(2,1)].

  THE SYMMETRIC PART IS DISCARDED AND REPORTED, NEVER SILENTLY DROPPED. For
  a consistent (R, Rdot) the product above is exactly skew-symmetric, because
  R'R = I differentiates to Rdot'R + R'Rdot = 0. A pair that does not satisfy
  it - a numerically differentiated R, a rate from one epoch paired with a
  rotation from another, a rate belonging to a different sense - has a
  symmetric part, and the skew part alone is then a least-squares answer that
  looks perfectly plausible. The symmetric part is therefore projected out
  with (S - S')/2, which is the correct estimator, and its size is returned
  in info.symmetricResidual so that a caller can see what was thrown away.
  Nothing is refused on it: a small residual is the normal state of a
  finite-differenced rate, and there is no threshold that is right for both
  an analytic rate and a differenced one.

  THE INPUT IS NOT A PARTNER OPTION ON THE FORWARD FUNCTION. Section C.7
  rule 2 makes direction part of the name and admits no flag, and a shape
  test would not serve here either: a 3xN angular velocity and a 3x3xN rate
  are distinguishable only until N is 3, which is exactly the D65 ambiguity
  this toolbox refuses to resolve by inspection. Hence two files (D74).

ACCURACY

Exact to machine precision for a consistent pair: one page product and a
  sign read. Measured against an independent Python implementation, the round
  trip omega -> Rdot -> omega reproduces omega to 1.4e-17 rad/s at rates near
  0.03 rad/s, which is one unit in the last place.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:angularVelocityFromRotationRate:wrongRotationSize
  referenceSystem:angularVelocityFromRotationRate:wrongRateSize
  referenceSystem:angularVelocityFromRotationRate:sizeMismatch

REFERENCES

D. H. Titterton and J. L. Weston (2004), Strapdown Inertial Navigation
  Technology, 2nd ed., Sect. 3.6.
  IERS Conventions (2010), IERS Technical Note 36, Ch. 5, for the same
  invariant applied to the terrestrial-to-celestial rotation.

EXAMPLE

[R, Rdot] = referenceSystem.rotationItrsToGcrs(t, 'Eop', eop);
  omega = referenceSystem.angularVelocityFromRotationRate(R, Rdot);

KEYWORDS

strapdown, gyro, angular velocity, kinematics, attitude, rotation rate,
  skew symmetric, body frame, navigation frame, IMU

See also ANGULARVELOCITYTOROTATIONRATE, INTEGRATEATTITUDE, INVERTROTATION.

referenceSystem.integrateAttitude

Propagate attitude over a gyro rate series, body-resolved rate.

SYNTAX

q = integrateAttitude(initialQuaternion, omega, epoch) propagates the
  initial attitude through the 3xN series of angular velocities of the BODY
  frame relative to the NAVIGATION frame, RESOLVED IN THE BODY FRAME, tagged
  by the N epochs, and returns the 4xN attitude at each of them.

  q = integrateAttitude(..., 'Method', "quaternionRk4") integrates the
  quaternion differential equation instead of exponentiating a rotation
  vector. See ACCURACY: on a sampled rate series the two agree closely, and
  the reason is worth reading before choosing.

  [q, info] = integrateAttitude(...) also returns what the integration did.

INPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                ----   ----     -----  -----------
  initialQuaternion   4x1    double   -      Attitude at the FIRST epoch,
                                             rows ordered by the convention.
                                             Need not be of unit norm.
  omega               3xN    double   rad/s  Angular velocity samples, one
                                             column per epoch, body resolved.
  epoch               1x1    struct   -      Epoch struct holding N strictly
                                             increasing epochs. The sample
                                             spacing need not be uniform.

OPTIONAL NAME-VALUE ARGUMENTS

Name              Size   Type     Default                Description
  ----              ----   ----     -------                -----------
  Method            1x1    string   "rotationVector"       "rotationVector"
                                                           or "quaternionRk4".
  Convention        1x1    struct   attitudeConvention()   .quaternion is read.
  ConingCorrection  1x1    logical  true                   Include the coning
                                                           term. False is for
                                                           demonstrating its
                                                           effect, not for use.
  RenormalizeEvery  1x1    double   1                      Steps between
                                                           renormalisations.

OUTPUT ARGUMENTS

Name                  Size   Type     Units  Description
  ----                  ----   ----     -----  -----------
  q                     4xN    double   -      Attitude at each epoch, column
                                               one being the initial attitude
                                               renormalised. SIGN CONTINUOUS
                                               along the series.
  info                  1x1    struct   -      Diagnostics.
  .method               1x1    string   -      The method that ran.
  .stepCount            1x1    double   -      N-1.
  .totalAngle           1x1    double   rad    Accumulated rotation angle.
  .largestStepAngle     1x1    double   rad    Largest single-step angle.
  .largestConingAngle   1x1    double   rad    Largest coning term applied,
                                               zero when it is switched off.
  .coningCorrection     1x1    logical  -      Whether it was applied.
  .renormalizeEvery     1x1    double   -      The cadence used.
  convention            1x1    struct   -      The convention used.

METHOD

THE ROTATION-VECTOR METHOD, NOT NAIVE INTEGRATION. Over one interval the
  rotation vector is formed first and exponentiated once, so the step is an
  exact rotation whatever its size, and the attitude cannot leave the unit
  sphere by an amount that depends on the step. For a rate that is linear
  across the interval the second-order Picard solution of Bortz's equation is

      phi = dt*(w_a + w_b)/2 + (dt^2/12) * cross(w_a, w_b)

  and the second term is the first-order coning correction. It is derived,
  not quoted: expanding phi_dot = w + 0.5*cross(phi, w) + ... to second order
  with w linear on [0, dt] gives exactly (dt^2/12)*cross(w_a, w_b). The step
  is applied as q_next = q (x) exp(phi) by the package-private
  composeRotationVectors, which is the single authority for the exponential
  map and is shared with integrateAttitudeIncrements; the increment joins on
  the RIGHT
  because it is expressed in the body frame and, with the Hamilton product in
  the frame sense, R(a (x) b) = R(b) R(a).

  WHY THE CONING TERM MATTERS. Under oscillatory motion the rate vector
  sweeps a cone and successive increments do not commute. Dropping the term
  leaves a rectified error that grows linearly with time rather than
  averaging out, which is the regime a real IMU sees and the reason a
  single-step test of an attitude integrator proves nothing (risk R21).

  RENORMALISATION. Every step by default. The exponential is a unit
  quaternion and the product of two unit quaternions is unit, so the norm
  drifts only by rounding - about 1e-16 per step, so 1e-12 over 10^4 steps,
  which is already negligible. The default is nevertheless every step,
  because it costs one square root against several trigonometric evaluations
  and because a cadence that is tuned is a parameter that can be set wrong.
  'RenormalizeEvery' exists so the claim can be tested rather than believed.

  TIME. Intervals come from secondsBetween, so its leap-second rule applies
  unchanged: a UTC series that CROSSES a leap second is refused rather than
  silently mis-spaced by one second. Tag a gyro series with TAI, TT or GPS.

ACCURACY

MEASURED ON THE CLOSED-FORM CONING CASE, against an independent Python
  implementation. The rotation vector traces a circle of half-angle beta at
  rate Omega, so the attitude and the rate are both closed forms:

      q(t)     = [cos(beta/2); sin(beta/2)cos(Omega t); sin(beta/2)sin(Omega t); 0]
      omega(t) = Omega [-sin(beta) sin(Omega t); sin(beta) cos(Omega t);
                        -(1 - cos(beta))]

  With beta = 3 deg and a 4 s cone period, integrating 100 s at dt = 0.01 s:

      method                          error [rad]   drift [arcsec/h]
      ------                          -----------   ----------------
      rotationVector                  8.85e-06      65.7
      quaternionRk4                   8.85e-06      65.7
      rotationVector, coning dropped  1.77e-05      131.4

  THE HEADLINE FIGURE IS NOT THE INTERESTING ONE, AND QUOTING IT ALONE WOULD
  MISLEAD. The residual was decomposed by switching each error source off
  independently. With the trapezoidal quadrature replaced by the exact
  integral of omega, the coning-corrected error falls to 0.0 to 3.0e-08 rad,
  that is to machine precision: THE CONING TERM IS EXACT FOR THIS MOTION AND
  REMOVES THE WHOLE NON-COMMUTATIVITY ERROR. What survives is the trapezoidal
  reconstruction of omega BETWEEN SAMPLES, and for this motion the two
  happen to be equal in size, which is why dropping the coning term costs a
  factor of two rather than the factor of hundreds the term deserves.

  THAT IS ALSO WHY THE TWO METHODS AGREE. They differ by less than 1e-11 rad
  here - which is what the suite asserts and all it can see; the figure of
  5e-11 carried here until rev. 1.119 was FALSIFIED by run 38, because the
  test now bounds the separation at 1e-11 and passes. That figure never came
  from a run: until rev. 1.118 the metric it would have been measured with
  returned exactly zero below 2e-08 rad. An independent mirror puts the
  separation at 5e-14, and that number is attributed rather than adopted -
  two mirrors have now disagreed about it by three orders (O20). The methods
  differ by far less than either differs from the truth, not
  because the integrators are equivalent but because neither is what limits
  the answer: both reconstruct omega from the same two samples per interval.
  Choosing between them on this evidence is choosing between two things that
  are not the bottleneck. The bottleneck is the SAMPLING, and a user who has
  integrated increments from the gyro rather than sampled rates has a
  materially better input than either method can make up for.

  THAT INPUT NOW HAS A FUNCTION: integrateAttitudeIncrements. Measured on the
  closed-form coning case with exact increments, it is between 1.2e+03 and
  9.7e+03 times more accurate than this function on the corresponding sampled
  rates, and the ratio grows as the step shrinks because the two are of
  different order. Prefer it wherever the gyro delivers integrated angle.

  Both errors are O(dt^2) in accumulated angle: 3.54e-05, 8.85e-06, 2.21e-06,
  5.53e-07 rad at dt = 0.02, 0.01, 0.005, 0.0025 s, an order of 2.00 at every
  halving.

  CONSTANT RATE IS EXACT. The coning term vanishes because cross(w, w) = 0
  and every step is the same rotation, so 10^4 steps of a constant
  0.032 rad/s rate reproduce the analytic single-axis answer to
  max|q - exact| = 1.13e-14, which is accumulated rounding alone.

ERRORS

referenceSystem:integrateAttitude:wrongQuaternionSize
  referenceSystem:integrateAttitude:wrongVelocitySize
  referenceSystem:integrateAttitude:sizeMismatch
  referenceSystem:integrateAttitude:tooFewSamples
  referenceSystem:integrateAttitude:samplesNotIncreasing

REFERENCES

J. E. Bortz (1971), A New Mathematical Formulation for Strapdown Inertial
  Navigation, IEEE Trans. Aerospace and Electronic Systems 7(1), 61-66.
  P. G. Savage (1998), Strapdown Inertial Navigation Integration Algorithm
  Design Part 1: Attitude Algorithms, J. Guidance 21(1), 19-28.
  M. B. Ignagni (1990), Optimal Strapdown Attitude Integration Algorithms,
  J. Guidance 13(2), 363-369.

EXAMPLE

t = referenceSystem.daySecondToEpoch([repmat(60310, 1001, 1), ...
      (0:1000)'*0.01], "TAI");
  q = referenceSystem.integrateAttitude([1; 0; 0; 0], gyroRate, t);

KEYWORDS

strapdown, attitude, gyro, coning, rotation vector, quaternion, IMU,
  inertial navigation, propagation

See also ANGULARVELOCITYTOROTATIONRATE, INTERPOLATEQUATERNIONS, ROTATIONNAVTOBODY.
